
<<JPY_WEEK_BEGIN>>

DATA WINDOW: 4 October 2026 to 17 October 2026

# A. JPY WEEK — MACRO SYNTHESIS

**Regime: two-speed growth / fragile household demand / persistent imported inflation / gradual BoJ normalization.** Japan's corporate investment cycle remains resilient despite a weak domestic-consumption backdrop. Core machinery orders rebounded 5.2% m/m and 18.6% y/y, supported by AI-linked equipment and non-manufacturing digital investment, while manufacturing confidence strengthened. Conversely, real household spending contracted for a ninth consecutive month (−3.1% y/y), non-manufacturing sentiment fell sharply and services activity moderated despite continued hiring and backlog accumulation. The external current-account surplus recovered through primary investment income, not through stronger goods trade. Growth resilience is concentrated in business investment and overseas earnings rather than a broad wage–consumption recovery.

**State transition: stronger capex evidence alongside more convincing domestic-demand deterioration; upstream inflation momentum stabilizing without broad disinflation.** September CGPI undershot materially (−0.3% m/m; 6.8% y/y), but agricultural and petroleum declines concealed continued industrial-price breadth and elevated foreign-currency energy costs. Services selling-price pressures remained historically high. Monetary aggregates showed no liquidity contraction, although deposit reallocation and reported financing-cost concerns suggest progressive transmission of higher rates. The BoJ's normalization direction remains supported, but the October-versus-December timing question has become more asymmetric: inflation persistence and corporate resilience support further tightening, while household purchasing-power weakness, deteriorating domestic confidence and incomplete wage-to-consumption transmission argue against accelerating without stronger underlying inflation confirmation. No separately validated central-bank speaker tape was supplied.

# B. JPY MACRO SIGNAL SCOREBOARD

**Growth: ~**

Supporting evidence:

Core machinery orders +5.2% m/m and +18.6% y/y, strengthening manufacturing confidence (+22), and non-manufacturing digital investment support the corporate-capex cycle. However, household expenditure remains −3.1% y/y, Reuters Tankan non-manufacturing sentiment fell to +23 from +29, and services PMI slowed to 51.3. Domestic private investment resilience offsets household weakness without establishing broad demand acceleration.

**Labor: ~**

Supporting evidence:

Services PMI employment growth accelerated to its strongest since February, while backlogs accumulated and the separate labour survey recorded +1.5% real wage growth. However, household-survey real income remained −0.9% y/y and wage improvements have not translated into a sustained consumption recovery. Labour-capacity resilience is evident; broad household-income transmission remains unproven.

**Inflation: ~**

Supporting evidence:

CGPI decelerated to +6.8% y/y from +7.6%, with a second consecutive −0.3% monthly reading and a −0.8pp annual consensus surprise. Yet agricultural and petroleum declines dominate the moderation; chemicals, plastics and utilities remain inflationary, yen-denominated import prices are +24.5% y/y, and services output-price pressures remain historically elevated. Near-term producer-price momentum has eased without establishing durable underlying disinflation.

**Financial Conditions: ~**

Supporting evidence:

M2 +2.0% y/y, M3 +1.2% and broad liquidity +4.3% indicate continued monetary expansion without aggregate liquidity stress. However, transaction deposits are weakening relative to time deposits, property firms report greater borrowing-cost pressure, and large intervention-related financial-account transactions complicate private-flow interpretation. Financial normalization is transmitting through asset allocation and selected financing costs, but a broad credit tightening cannot be established.

**Policy Constraint: -**

Supporting evidence:

Persistent services pricing, elevated industrial inflation and imported-energy vulnerability restrict BoJ flexibility, while weak consumption and deteriorating domestic-facing sentiment increase the potential growth cost of faster tightening. Corporate capex and employment resilience permit continued normalization, but the evidence does not establish a self-sustaining wage–consumption–inflation cycle sufficient to remove the near-term policy constraint.

# C. FULL RELEASE ARCHIVE

MONDAY 5 OCT 2026

JAPAN — S&P Global Services PMI Final / Composite Output Index Final (Sep)

Release Date: 5 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 02:30

Importance: ***

PM FLASH: September services PMI finalized at 51.3 versus 51.6 flash (52.5 August final); composite 52.3 versus 52.5 flash (53.5 August). Expansion persisted but momentum slowed substantially.

PM READ-THROUGH: Softer domestic services and markedly weaker export orders contrast with accelerating hiring, backlogs and still-high selling prices. Growth moderates without a contraction signal or clear relief from domestic inflation pressure.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Debate: Activity deceleration and earthquake disruption versus durable capacity constraints and output-price persistence. Key takeaways: The Reuters priors are September flash estimates, not August observations; the true sequential slowdown is larger. Backlogs can reflect shortages or disruption rather than excess demand. Follow services prices, wages and new orders before assigning a BoJ meeting-timing signal.

1. Reuters Data

S&P Global Comp Op Final SA: 52,3 | Prior: 52,5

S&P Global SVC PMI Final SA: 51,3 | Prior: 51,6

2. Professional Market Commentary

The institutional tape depicts a slowing but still-expanding services sector alongside persistent labour-capacity and selling-price pressures. S&P Global and Reuters attributed the loss of activity and new-order momentum partly to earthquake disruption and weak foreign services demand; the composite grew at its slowest rate since May. Services employment strengthened, backlogs accumulated and historically elevated output charges continued, albeit with slower inflation momentum. Firms' future expectations improved despite weaker current sales.

Annabel Fiddes (S&P Global) emphasized resilient output, accelerating employment and high selling-price inflation as compatible with further BoJ tightening, potentially in October. Chris Williamson's earlier September flash assessment likewise emphasized inflation and possible imminent tightening. Reuters placed greater weight on weaker growth, export orders and local disruption; the producer commentary emphasized capacity and prices. These are differing emphases, not proof of opposing bank forecasts. No independent release-specific sell-side consensus or attributable USD/JPY/JGB reaction was confirmed.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

The central signal is a loss of services momentum within ongoing expansion, while hiring and price diffusion remain materially stronger than activity. The downward final-from-flash adjustments are small; comparing final September with final August reveals the larger deterioration. The release does not establish a regime break, nor does a high output-price diffusion index prove that self-sustaining, wage-driven CPI inflation is accelerating. Activity, domestic versus foreign demand, capacity and pricing should be evaluated separately.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

Activity, survey comparability and industrial mix. Services PMI was 51.3 versus September flash 51.6 and August final 52.5, the fourth consecutive expansion but below the 2026 average. Composite PMI was 52.3 versus flash 52.5 and August final 53.5: eighteenth consecutive expansion, weakest since May. Reuters 'Prior' fields refer to the flash. Manufacturing remained the stronger output engine; composite deceleration was broad, not solely services weakness. The monthly message is slower growth, not contraction.

Orders and geographical composition. Total services new orders rose for a 27th month but more slowly. Domestic demand remained supportive, while services export orders dropped at their second-fastest rate since January 2021. Composite export demand stayed positive through manufacturing, not services. Kumamoto earthquake disruption may have suppressed local activity; it does not by itself explain away the softer foreign-order reading. Strong aggregate exports must not be mistaken for resilient demand in both sectors.

Hiring, bottlenecks and expectations. Services employment increased for a thirteenth month and at the fastest rate since February; backlogs rose most rapidly in seven months. Firms expanded capacity and recruited as new projects continued. Services business optimism was highest since June and composite confidence reached a seven-month high. Backlogs may reflect customer demand, a structural labour shortage, or interrupted processing; the data do not distinguish these mechanisms sufficiently to classify domestic overheating.

Inflation hierarchy. Input-cost inflation eased to a six-month low but remained elevated, with wages, food, materials and oil reported as drivers. Services selling-price increases were among the strongest in the series, though slower than August; the composite also showed moderation at a high level. Thus price persistence remains strong but the rate of acceleration is easing, capacity pressure is firm, and domestic demand acceleration is unproven. Price PMI diffusion captures the incidence of rising prices rather than a numerical CPI contribution; the survey cannot separate wage pass-through from energy/JPY-cost effects.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: deterioration in services growth momentum, without an expansion-regime break; inflation and capacity constraints remain elevated.

Context from prior regional live file: Japan's earlier PMI sequence moved from services stagnation in May to renewed domestic-led expansion in June, while manufacturing strength remained partly exposed to precautionary stockbuilding and external energy costs. September's slowdown partially reverses that improvement without returning services to stagnation. The established divergence between resilient industrial activity, weaker domestic services momentum and elevated inflation pressure therefore persists.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information: Expansion and labour demand continue, but faster hiring and near-record output-price diffusion are accompanied by weaker orders and service activity. These jointly support continued BoJ normalization but are insufficient to identify October rather than December as the next hike date. The producer's October possibility is not a wider independent institutional consensus.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate: If high services prices are sustained by domestic wage growth and demand, they support a tighter path and potentially front-end JGB/JPY expectations. If imported energy costs and capacity interruption are the dominant forces while consumption softens, price persistence may coexist with limited tightening tolerance. September revisions and earthquake effects make a single survey an especially weak market-timing catalyst. No attributable rates or FX move was verified. Higher living costs can also impair real income.

What to verify next: Tokyo and national services/underlying CPI; Services Producer Price Index; sustained wage/labour-cost increases; October services new orders, output charges and backlogs; whether activity stabilizes without renewed price acceleration.

Macro configuration: expansionary but decelerating services + resilient employment/backlogs + historically high but cooling selling-price pressure + conditional BoJ tightening.

WEDNESDAY 7 OCT 2026

JAPAN — Reuters Tankan Non-Manufacturing / Manufacturing Sentiment (Oct)

Release Date: 7 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 01:00

Importance: ***

PM FLASH: Reuters Tankan non-manufacturing confidence fell six points to +23 from +29, while manufacturing improved to +22 from +21, its strongest since December 2021 on semiconductor demand.

PM READ-THROUGH: The domestic-facing confidence downturn is broadening across retail, food, property and information services even as AI-linked manufacturing resilience continues. Positive index levels do not mean corporate conditions are contracting.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Debate: Healthy industrial orders and services hiring versus weaker margins, purchasing power and financing conditions. Key takeaways: The official BoJ survey corroborates direction but has much stronger sampling; the Reuters survey overlaps the September hike and cannot isolate its impact. Watch whether confidence weakness translates into orders/employment rather than interpreting weaker sentiment as contemporaneous output contraction.

1. Reuters Data

Reuters Tankan N-Man Idx: 23 | Prior: 29

2. Professional Market Commentary

Reuters documented the widening split between AI-supported manufacturing and weakening domestic-facing business confidence. Manufacturing sentiment rose for a third month, aided by precision machinery, metals and semiconductors; non-manufacturing confidence weakened to its lowest since November 2024. Respondents cited high costs, household spending pressure, construction expenses and higher borrowing rates. The three-month outlook remained positive but was weaker for non-manufacturing; manufacturers also cautioned that exceptional AI orders could ease.

Takeshi Minami (Norinchukin Research Institute), commenting on the *earlier 1 October official BoJ Tankan* rather than this poll, favoured eventual further tightening without finding a need for consecutive hikes. The official BoJ survey showed the same manufacturing/non-manufacturing direction. No release-specific independent economist consensus or attributable JPY/JGB reaction was confirmed.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

The strongest evidence is deterioration across domestic-facing corporate confidence at the same time as a narrow global industrial cycle improves. The split has deepened rather than reversed the pre-existing two-speed Japanese economy. A positive sentiment balance reflects more optimistic than pessimistic respondents, not an outright growth rate; a six-point fall is therefore meaningful as a change in assessment, but not directly equivalent to lost output.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

Domestic breadth. Non-manufacturing fell from +29 to +23, the lowest since November 2024. Consumer and interest-rate-sensitive sectors weakened together:

| Sector | October | September |
|---|---:|---:|
| Food producers | −40 | −25 |
| Information / communications | +8 | +21 |
| Retail | +8 | +18 |
| Real estate / construction | +28 | +37 |
| Wholesale | +30 | +24 |

Retail and food point to constrained purchasing power; property/construction add rates and building expenses. Information services weakness argues against attributing the decline solely to retail. Wholesale improved amid disaster-recovery construction, a potentially temporary offset rather than generalized acceleration.

Industrial strength and concentration. Manufacturing rose to +22 from +21, the best since December 2021. Precision machinery reached +38 (+9), metal products +35 (+9), and steel/nonferrous +25 from −13. One semiconductor supplier reported orders about 1.5 times normal for four months; this is illustrative company evidence, not a representative sector growth rate. The metal rebound suggests some breadth, although AI/semiconductor capital spending remains pivotal and respondents warned demand might moderate.

Expectations, measurement and independent confirmation. Three-month views were +23 for manufacturers and +21 for non-manufacturers versus current +22/+23, a prospective widening gap. The earlier official BoJ Tankan showed large manufacturers +24 from +22 and large non-manufacturers +35 from +37, with December outlooks of +21 and +30. Levels are not comparable between surveys. Reuters received 215/508 responses, whereas the official Tankan sampled 9,104 firms with a 99.4% response rate; the former adds timeliness but the latter is the stronger benchmark. Reuters collection ran 18 September–2 October, across the September policy increase; rate effects cannot be causally isolated from energy prices, spending or construction costs.

Reconciliation with the 5 October PMI. Services PMI 51.3 reflects still-expanding business activity but weaker monthly momentum; rising PMI employment/backlogs measures continued capacity building. Tankan measures firms' assessments of profitability and conditions, not the same quantity. Selling prices can rise in a cost shock even as margins, orders and household confidence deteriorate. Manufacturing PMI/export resilience is consistent with, but does not prove, widespread industrial demand outside AI.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: deterioration in domestic-facing corporate sentiment; continuation of strong but concentrated manufacturing conditions.

Context from prior regional live file: The May Reuters Tankan had already shown non-manufacturing sentiment softening to +29 from +31, against a backdrop of weak household demand and stronger external corporate activity. October's decline to +23 from September's +29 deepens that domestic-confidence weakness, while manufacturing sentiment has strengthened considerably. The earlier two-speed configuration is therefore becoming more pronounced, not reversing.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information: Higher AI-linked corporate confidence and still-positive services activity preserve the growth case for BoJ normalization; falling consumer/property confidence and explicit rate complaints strengthen the argument for gradual policy timing. The poll does not independently require October tightening following September's 1.25% rate increase.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate: A more hawkish reading needs durable wages, domestic orders and services inflation, rather than imported-cost repricing. Gradualism becomes more compelling if household spending and margins weaken; growth risk would intensify if semiconductor demand also turned. For front-end JGBs, the domestic-growth input is modestly dovish without overriding wages and CPI. For JPY, the normalization offset and domestic slowdown make the signal two-sided. Sector differentiation favours distinguishing AI-capex exposures from domestic consumer/property sensitivity; no attributable market move was validated.

What to verify next: Real household expenditure/retail; underlying services CPI and wages; machinery-order breadth; subsequent PMIs, company orders, employment and the larger-sample official Tankan.

Macro configuration: AI-centred manufacturing confidence + weaker domestic sentiment and purchasing power + persistent selling-price pressure + less certain near-term BoJ timing.

THURSDAY 8 OCT 2026

JAPAN — Balance of Payments Preliminary / Current Account NSA & SA / Goods Balance SA (Aug)

Release Date: 8 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 01:50

Importance: ***

PM FLASH: August current account reached ¥4.062tn NSA versus ¥3.195tn expected, and ¥2.979tn SA versus ¥2.523tn prior. Large primary income masked deteriorating goods trade.

PM READ-THROUGH: Japan's external-income cushion has rebuilt, but this is not a trade recovery or necessarily immediate spot-yen demand. Energy imports and intervention-dominated financial flows remain important counterweights.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Debate: A large current-account surplus reinforces external resilience; earned investment income, particularly reinvested foreign profits, does not automatically imply currency conversion. Key takeaways: Primary income exceeds the entire surplus, goods have deteriorated, and August reserves/financial transactions were distorted by intervention. Validate private repatriation separately before treating BOP strength as an FX catalyst.

1. Reuters Data

Current Account NSA JPY: 4 062,0B | Prior: 2 988,9B | Poll: 3 194,6B | Surprise: 867,40B

Current Account Bal SA: 2 979,39B | Prior: 2 523,09B

Trade Bal Cust Basis SA: -485,18B | Prior: -429,38B

2. Professional Market Commentary

The Ministry of Finance and BoJ confirmed a ¥4.062tn unadjusted current-account surplus (+12.0% y/y), supported by ¥5.133tn in net primary investment income. The SA current account strengthened, while the goods deficit widened as imports grew faster than exports; the services deficit narrowed through tourism and intellectual-property income. Reuters and official reporting emphasized overseas direct-investment dividends, interest and a large foreign-asset base. JPY depreciation simultaneously raised the reported yen value of foreign earnings and the energy-import bill.

The principal professional interpretive distinction is between a structurally strong earnings balance and immediate yen-purchasing flows. Separate MOF/Reuters reporting identified exceptionally large July–August yen-buying intervention and reserve declines. This context informs the financial-account composition but cannot establish October intervention in response to the release. No independent release-specific economist consensus or attributable JPY/JGB reaction was confirmed.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

The August surprise reflects a real SA current-account recovery driven by foreign-asset income, not goods competitiveness. Structural interest and dividend earnings provide external solvency support, but reinvested earnings and JPY translation complicate direct interpretation as available repatriation cash. The opposing goods deficit reflects substantial external price and energy vulnerabilities. Flow, accounting income and currency conversion must remain analytically distinct.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

Headline and annual component reconciliation. NSA data (¥bn):

| Component | August | July | August 2025 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Goods | −687.7 | −399.9 | +113.8 |
| Services | −115.8 | −512.9 | −266.7 |
| Primary income | +5,133.2 | +4,289.6 | +4,222.7 |
| Secondary income | −267.7 | −387.9 | −441.7 |
| Current account | +4,062.0 | +2,988.9 | +3,628.1 |

The annual surplus improved ¥433.9bn, with primary income +¥910.5bn, goods −¥801.5bn, services +¥150.9bn and lower secondary outflows +¥174.0bn. Primary income is greater than the total CA; excluding it would leave a deficit. August SA current account was ¥2,979.4bn versus ¥2,523.1bn (+18.1% m/m): primary income SA ¥3,907.1bn from ¥3,500.0bn (+11.6%), goods/services −¥648.4bn versus −¥583.9bn, secondary income −¥279.2bn versus −¥393.0bn. Income and transfers, not goods/services, explain the rebound. The mechanically retained Reuters 'Trade Bal Cust Basis SA' label actually denotes a BOP-basis SA goods balance, not a customs-cleared trade series.

Goods and terms of trade. Exports increased 16.9% y/y to ¥9.862tn but imports 26.7% to ¥10.550tn. Semiconductor-related and automotive exports could not offset more expensive energy/commodities and yen effects. SA exports fell 0.8% m/m, imports 0.3%, widening the goods deficit. The release cannot quantitatively separate price from volume; importing more expensive energy is not evidence of domestic consumption-led real import demand.

Services composition. The deficit narrowed to ¥115.8bn from ¥266.7bn a year earlier. Travel was +¥399.9bn versus +¥362.4bn; intellectual property +¥682.3bn versus +¥436.2bn; financial services +¥109.4bn versus +¥62.6bn. Structural offsets remain: telecoms/computer/information −¥246.4bn versus −¥193.2bn, other business services −¥479.2bn versus −¥486.5bn, transport −¥96.7bn versus −¥65.0bn. Improved services receipts are not merely a tourism effect.

Investment-income composition and realized conversion. Net primary-income components (¥bn): direct investment +2,646.7, comprising dividends/withdrawals +1,699.1 and reinvested earnings +934.6; portfolio income +2,459.6, including bond interest +1,819.2 and equity/fund dividends +640.3. Bond coupons and dividends are recurring returns on external assets, but overseas reinvested profits can remain outside Japan and are imputed in the BOP; August's ¥934.6bn is a fixed preliminary reinvestment estimate. Yen depreciation increases the translation value of earnings without ensuring yen conversion. Actual FX flow needs investor repatriation and currency-conversion evidence.

Financial account, intervention and data quality. August reserve assets showed −¥15.091tn, portfolio investment +¥4.972tn, other investment +¥13.079tn, financial-account balance +¥2.218tn and errors/omissions −¥1.838tn. MOF recorded ¥15.3993tn yen-buying intervention over 30 July–26 August; Reuters reported the largest monthly reserve-stock fall. Reserve BOP transactions and the differently timed intervention total are directionally comparable, not an exact accounting identity. Foreign reductions in Japanese short-term debt and contractions of other-investment liabilities/deposits/loans complicate private-flow attribution. Preliminary statistics, large errors and omissions, imputed investment earnings and intervention-sensitive flow categories preclude treating the surplus as clean near-term JPY demand.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: stabilization of the overall external surplus after earlier weakness, with continuation of goods-trade deterioration and exceptional intervention-related financial flows.

Context from prior regional live file: Japan's April external improvement had been classified as potentially temporary, and May subsequently showed a decline in the seasonally adjusted current-account surplus alongside a weakening trade balance. August restores current-account momentum through stronger investment income, while the goods deficit confirms the earlier concern over delayed energy-import costs. The external-income cushion remains intact, but the trade and real-income vulnerability has become more evident.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information: Japan's foreign-asset income remains a substantial external buffer, but the energy-related goods deterioration and incomplete cash conversion make the headline beat insufficient for a mechanical long-JPY call. Net investment income and import costs may both rise when the yen weakens.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate: Higher domestic yields could encourage external-portfolio repatriation, but BOP investment income cannot establish that investors actually sold foreign assets or converted proceeds into yen. Intervention-affected reserve and banking flows are distinct. For BoJ policy, import-price inflation may justify vigilance, whereas household purchasing-power damage argues for gradualism; the BOP does not set the next meeting date. Front-end JGB effect is negligible; the structural long-end portfolio question remains open. Overseas earners and energy-intensive domestic firms have contrasting equity exposures.

What to verify next: September customs trade and import-price composition; future BOP investment receipts; MOF private securities investment, FX conversions and JGB flows; household demand and CGPI; in-window BoJ communication and inflation evidence.

Macro configuration: strong external investment income + worsening energy terms of trade + intervention-sensitive JPY flow picture + persistent household real-income vulnerability.

FRIDAY 9 OCT 2026

JAPAN — Family Income and Expenditure Survey / All Household Spending YY & MM (Aug)

Release Date: 9 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 01:30

Importance: ***

PM FLASH: Real household spending fell 3.1% y/y versus −3.6% consensus, ninth straight annual decline; monthly spending rose only 0.1% versus +0.5% expected despite stronger labour-survey real wages.

PM READ-THROUGH: A smaller annual contraction is stabilization at a weak level, not a consumption rebound. The household-income channel remains fragile alongside weaker consumer-facing corporate sentiment.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Debate: Persistent purchasing-power erosion versus exceptional weather and subsidy distortions in utilities, durable goods and education. Key takeaways: Real wages (+1.5% y/y in the labour survey) are not equivalent to household real income (−0.9%). Weak food and recreation volumes matter more structurally than air-conditioner spending. Recheck normalized spending and broad income coverage.

1. Reuters Data

All Household Spending YY: -3,1% | Prior: -3,6% | Poll: -3,6% | Surprise: 0,50%

All Household Spending MM: 0,1% | Prior: 0,5% | Poll: 0,5% | Surprise: -0,40%

2. Professional Market Commentary

Official family-spending data and Reuters show a ninth consecutive annual real contraction, less negative than July but only a marginal SA monthly gain. Reuters emphasized the positive annual surprise and disappointing sequential result; Bloomberg emphasized the failure of better real wages to lift consumer demand. Jiji Press highlighted lower temperatures, typhoons and fewer cooling/appliance purchases; ActionForex stressed the absence of sustained consumption recovery. Food, electricity, apparel and recreation weakened, while car purchases improved. Weather versus underlying purchasing-power erosion is the material interpretive difference, not a clean professional forecast dispute.

The official headline and family-income data are the more secure evidence; some detailed category numbers were obtained from Jiji Press and ActionForex rather than independent checks of government tables. No independently attributable named economist consensus or FX/JGB reaction was established.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

The release weakens the case that positive real wages are driving a self-sustaining consumption upturn. Annual spending contracted for a ninth month; the annual print exceeded depressed expectations but monthly real demand barely advanced. Weather, cost-of-living measures and volatile purchases imply that the entire decline cannot be extrapolated as structural; nonetheless food and discretionary weakness, combined with the Reuters Tankan and services PMI, confirm a persistent demand constraint.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

Levels, surveys and sequential momentum. Official household measures for August:

| Indicator | August 2026 |
|---|---:|
| Average monthly consumption per household | ¥310,975 |
| Nominal consumption y/y | −1.0% |
| Real consumption y/y | −3.1% |
| Real consumption m/m SA | +0.1% |
| Workers' household average monthly income | ¥616,704 |
| Workers' household nominal income y/y | +1.3% |
| Workers' household real income y/y | −0.9% |

Real spending improved 0.5pp y/y from July but its SA monthly rise slowed from +0.5% to +0.1%. The year-on-year surprise was +0.5pp, the monthly surprise −0.4pp. May 2026 spending −0.4% y/y (following a +3.7% m/m rebound) did not hold: June −3.3%, July −3.6%, August −3.1%. This is stabilization after renewed deterioration, not continuation of a May recovery.

Consumption composition (secondary-source category detail, real y/y):

| Category | Real y/y | Interpretation |
|---|---:|---|
| Food | −1.4% | Third decline; household economizing |
| Utilities | −5.2% | Weather/reduced energy use |
| Culture / recreation | −4.3% | Discretionary restraint |
| Western clothing | −6.8% | Weather/seasonal purchases |
| Air-conditioner purchases | −23.2% | Weather-sensitive durable |
| Electricity | −7.8% | Lower cooling demand |
| Automobile-related | +1.6% | Irregular new-car purchasing |

Food purchase reductions despite essential status and weak recreation strengthen the underlying purchasing-power hypothesis. Lower air-conditioning, utilities, summer clothing and beverage demand amid cooler weather, rain and typhoons weaken the inference from aggregate spending. Education outlays may have been reduced by administrative support rather than lower service consumption. Automobile purchases are volatile and should not be given structural weight similar to recurring food purchases. A weather-adjusted series would likely be less negative, but the release does not estimate that counterfactual.

Wages versus household purchasing power. The distinct Monthly Labour Survey recorded real wages +1.5% y/y for an eighth increase, nominal earnings +3.8% and regular/base pay +3.8%. The household survey instead recorded real worker-household income −0.9%. Different populations, samples, income concepts and age/employment coverage explain why the readings cannot be interchanged; pensions and non-employed households may not benefit from wage gains. Aggregate labour earnings are not yet generating measurable broad real-spending strength.

Cross-release and measurement checks. PMI services stayed expansionary but slowed and Tankan consumer/property confidence deteriorated. Strong primary overseas income in the BOP does not guarantee greater household spendable income. Services hiring/backlogs remained firm, so no general labour collapse is established. The family survey excludes single-person households, is monthly volatile, and is sensitive to weather, durables, subsidies and seasonal patterns. Category detail carries less certainty than official headlines.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: stabilization at a weak consumption level, following deterioration since May; no convincing recovery in the wage-to-spending transmission.

Context from prior regional live file: February and March household spending had reinforced Japan's domestic-demand weakness, before May's surprisingly strong monthly rebound and near-zero annual contraction temporarily improved the outlook. June and July reversed that improvement, and August's ninth consecutive annual decline confirms that May was not the start of a sustained consumption recovery. The household-demand constraint on BoJ normalization therefore remains in place despite a modest improvement from July.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information: Household data add realized confirmation of the weakening demand already seen in consumer-facing sentiment, while positive employee real wages and services hiring prevent a simple labour-led recession interpretation. The evidence favours gradual BoJ normalization rather than an acceleration triggered by high imported inflation alone.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate: Cost-push services/energy price increases can compress real consumption instead of signalling excess demand; additional rate hikes may intensify that squeeze. Yet summer weather and administrative education effects blur one month's signal. Immediate October tightening would require wage, domestic services inflation and capex resilience; continued weak orders/consumption would favour December or later. Front-end JGBs receive a modestly dovish growth input, JPY a potentially negative but non-decisive timing signal; no attributable move was verified. Domestic consumer/retail/property earnings remain exposed while AI exporters have separate support.

What to verify next: September consumption and retail; real labour earnings versus disposable household income and pensioner coverage; food/services CPI; consumer confidence, retail surveys and BoJ demand thresholds.

Macro configuration: persistent real consumption contraction + stronger but uneven wage measures + weather distortion + still-resilient services labour + tighter BoJ growth constraint.

TUESDAY 13 OCT 2026

JAPAN — Corporate Goods Price Index / Producer Prices MM & YY (Sep)

Release Date: 13 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 01:50

Importance: ***

PM FLASH: September CGPI −0.3% m/m versus +0.5% poll and +6.8% y/y versus +7.6% expected. Agriculture and petroleum dragged the headline down while industrial-price pressures persisted.

PM READ-THROUGH: Wholesale inflation momentum genuinely eased for a second month, reducing immediate acceleration fears, but underlying imported commodity costs and industrial breadth still constrain the BoJ's normalization options.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Debate: Peaking upstream inflation supports patience; high utilities/chemical prices and renewed foreign-currency energy inflation threaten further pass-through. Key takeaways: Agricultural and petroleum components together subtract 0.57pp against a −0.3% headline; the rest is net positive. Yen appreciation has cushioned contract-currency inflation temporarily. Distinguish next-hike timing from the eventual policy destination.

1. Reuters Data

Corp Goods Price MM: -0,3% | Prior: -0,2% | Revised: -0,3% | Poll: 0,5% | Surprise: -0,80%

Corp Goods Price YY: 6,8% | Prior: 7,6% | Poll: 7,6% | Surprise: -0,80%

2. Professional Market Commentary

The BoJ's September preliminary CGPI showed two consecutive monthly producer-price declines and annual inflation moderating to 6.8% from 7.6%; the surprise was −0.8pp in both monthly and annual measures. Reuters and official data attributed much of the fall to rice/agriculture and petroleum while noting broad ongoing industrial inflation, elevated yen-basis import prices and fresh LNG, naphtha and jet-fuel pressure in contract currency. The report is not a general reversal of upstream cost inflation.

Masato Koike (Sompo Institute Plus), quoted by Reuters on 13 October, warned wholesale inflation could re-accelerate if Middle East energy uncertainty persisted, while observing no sharp near-term acceleration; he favoured an October BoJ hold and a possible December hike. BoJ Governor Kazuo Ueda, speaking separately on 13 October, said underlying inflation had approached 2% and stressed keeping it stable near target; this is policy context, **not** a direct CGPI reaction or explicit immediate-hike endorsement. Reuters stressed persistent inflation and the tightening case; the alternative interpretation emphasized consecutive monthly declines and reduced urgency. No broader independent economist consensus or clean USD/JPY/JGB response was established.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

Producer-price disinflation is genuine in sequential headline momentum but highly uneven in composition. Agricultural and petroleum declines more than explain the total monthly fall; most other producer groups still report positive annual inflation, while foreign-currency import costs rose anew. A lower CGPI headline is therefore evidence of less immediate inflation acceleration, not proof that broad industrial pipeline costs or prospective domestic pass-through have been extinguished. This distinction conditions the BoJ timing debate without invalidating normalization.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

Headline, smoothed momentum and alternative weights:

| Indicator | September | August |
|---|---:|---:|
| Producer prices m/m | −0.3% | −0.3% revised |
| Producer prices y/y | +6.8% | +7.6% |
| Producer Price Index level | 135.7 | 136.1 |
| Change over 3 months | −0.1% | +0.7% |
| Chain-weighted PPI y/y | +6.2% | +6.6% |

The three-month change turned slightly negative following the March–June producer-price surge. The chain-weighted BoJ index also moderated (−0.1% m/m versus fixed-weight −0.3%; +6.2% y/y versus +6.8%), corroborating the direction but implying a smaller short-run fall under alternative weighting. Neither interpretation makes +6.8% annual CGPI low inflation.

Domestic contribution decomposition (official monthly percentage-point contributions):

| Component | September contribution |
|---|---:|
| Agriculture, forestry and fishery | −0.42pp |
| Petroleum and coal products | −0.15pp |
| Electric power, gas and water | +0.08pp |
| Plastics | +0.04pp |
| Beverages and foods | +0.03pp |
| Metal products | +0.03pp |
| Chemicals | +0.03pp |
| Iron and steel | +0.02pp |

Agriculture plus petroleum contributed −0.57pp, greater in magnitude than the −0.3% monthly total. The residual basket was therefore positive. Agriculture fell 8.8% m/m and 13.3% y/y, led by rice and pork; petroleum/coal −2.2% m/m remained +11.5% y/y. The rice correction is genuine household-relevant relief but not generalized input-cost easing.

Annual industrial inflation and breadth:

| Producer group | September | August |
|---|---:|---:|
| Nonferrous metals | +40.2% | +43.3% |
| Information / communication equipment | +15.3% | +14.8% |
| Chemicals | +13.3% | +13.0% |
| Plastics | +12.8% | +11.3% |
| Petroleum / coal products | +11.5% | +14.7% |
| Utilities | +10.5% | +8.2% |
| Beverages / foods | +4.2% | +4.3% |
| Transportation equipment | +2.3% | +2.5% |
| Agriculture / forestry / fishery | −13.3% | +1.5% |

All major listed groups other than agriculture remained positive annually. Utilities, plastics, chemicals and information equipment accelerated, nonferrous metals remained exceptionally high despite moderation, while vehicles and machinery were comparatively restrained. Utilities at +10.5% and chemical/plastics inflation can transmit external energy costs through firms' operating inputs even when headline CGPI declines. The data do not prove this pressure will fully pass to consumers; margins, pricing power and demand elasticity are material.

Imported costs, exchange rate and export pricing:

| Import price indicator | September m/m | August m/m | September y/y |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Yen basis | −0.2% | −2.9% | +24.5% |
| Contract-currency basis | +1.0% | −1.0% | +17.9% |

Contract-currency import-price contributions included petroleum/coal/gas +0.69pp, metals +0.14pp, and electrical/electronic products +0.10pp; LNG, naphtha and jet fuel were important drivers. BoJ's exchange-rate measure showed JPY appreciation of 1.5% m/m, temporarily cushioning yen-denominated imports as foreign-currency supplier prices rose. The +24.5% versus +17.9% annual divergence is consistent with currency amplification, but its 6.6pp gap is **not** an exact standalone FX contribution. Renewed depreciation or sustained energy prices could re-amplify the shock. Conversely further JPY appreciation would restrain domestic currency translation.

Contract-currency export prices increased 1.4% m/m versus +0.5% yen basis, with electric/electronic products adding +0.80pp. Yen-based electronics export prices were +40.9% y/y, including semiconductor memory and industrial controls. This aligns with AI/semiconductor-linked manufacturing confidence but establishes pricing, not physical export-volume growth or economy-wide capex acceleration. Export winners and imported-input-exposed domestic firms have different profit channels.

Cross-checks and uncertainty. Services PMI output charges remain historically high despite softer activity; Reuters Tankan domestic sentiment fell amid costs/rates; household real expenditure declined; and the BOP goods balance worsened under the import bill. Money-stock growth lacks an obvious demand-led excess-money acceleration. These multiple releases point to partial upstream momentum relief amid persistent imported-energy exposure. Preliminary data, weighting differences, supplier-price/FX translation and uncertain downstream pass-through limit certainty about a permanent turn.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: stabilization of producer-price momentum at elevated levels, with continued broad imported-cost and industrial inflation pressure.

Context from prior regional live file: The April–June CGPI sequence established a sharp escalation in imported energy costs, yen-denominated import inflation and industrial price pass-through, creating a stronger inflation constraint for the BoJ. September's second monthly decline and weaker annual reading mark the first meaningful moderation after that surge, but chemicals, utilities and import prices remain elevated. The earlier inflation-escalation regime is therefore stabilizing rather than decisively reversing.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information: The CGPI downside surprise reduces near-term pressure to hike immediately but leaves the normalization direction intact. It is material to the **timing-versus-destination** distinction because inflation remains widespread even as monthly momentum cools.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate: A more dovish October interpretation relies on two −0.3% monthly prints, a negative three-month rate, lower rice/petroleum prices and FX relief. A hawkish interpretation emphasizes 10.5% utilities, persistent chemical/plastic costs, +24.5% yen import inflation and strong services selling prices. Domestic services CPI and wages—not headline CGPI alone—determine whether higher prices are self-sustaining. One professional view favours an October hold / possible December hike, but the data permit alternatives. Front-end JGBs receive a marginal dovish inflation input; long-end yields remain exposed to energy inflation and term-premium risk. JPY effects are two-sided because lower hike urgency coexists with better imported-purchasing-power dynamics; no attributable market move was established. AI exporters and energy-intensive domestic firms remain asymmetric.

What to verify next: Tokyo/national CPI and services/wages; October CGPI on 12 November; yen- versus contract-currency imports and energy prices; household purchasing power; 29–30 October BoJ assessment of underlying inflation versus demand.

Macro configuration: easing producer-price headline + persistent industrial breadth and imported costs + fragile households + conditional, more gradual BoJ normalization.

WEDNESDAY 14 OCT 2026

JAPAN — Money Stock / M2 and Broadly-Defined Liquidity (L) (Sep)

Release Date: 14 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 01:50

Importance: **

PM FLASH: M2 and M3 growth held at +2.0% and +1.2% y/y; broad liquidity slowed to +4.3% from +4.4%. Transaction deposits stagnated while quasi-money and securities grew.

PM READ-THROUGH: The BoJ's tightening is reshaping money and investment allocation, not yet triggering aggregate liquidity contraction or an obvious monetary demand boom. The release is a limited independent policy-timing signal.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Professional insight: Higher domestic rates appear consistent with movement toward time deposits and JGB-linked instruments, but aggregate data cannot trace the same funds or show FX repatriation. Distinguish broad money from the shrinking central-bank monetary base and test whether observed reallocations coincide with actual bank-credit tightening.

1. Reuters Data

M2 Money Supply: 1 297 238,2B | Prior: 1 294 565,9B | Revised: 1 294 630,2B

Broad Money: 2 343,4T | Prior: 2 337,3T | Revised: 2 337,8T

2. Professional Market Commentary

No Tier 1 institutional release-specific debate was detected. BoJ official September statistics showed stable M2 (+2.0% y/y) and M3 (+1.2%), marginally softer broadly-defined liquidity L (+4.3% from +4.4%), and better SA monthly momentum. M1 became negative y/y, deposit money stagnated, quasi-money rose and investment funds/government securities expanded rapidly. The data provide no evidence of sudden broad-money contraction, but exhibit rate-sensitive portfolio composition.

The IMF's October assessment supported continued gradual BoJ normalization toward neutral while balancing inflation persistence and slower growth; it was not commentary specifically on September money stock. No independent named current-release economist or FX/JGB reaction was confirmed. Distinguish official aggregation facts from the interpretation that deposit flows reflect higher yields.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

Money and liquidity stocks continue growing modestly as the composition shifts toward time deposits and other financial assets. This is consistent with transmission of higher opportunity costs for non-interest-bearing balances; it does not prove that funds are moving directly between any two instruments or that the private economy is suffering liquidity scarcity. The monetary base and broader deposits are different liabilities, so their trajectories should not be equated.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

Headline annual and SA growth:

| Aggregate | September y/y | August y/y | September SA annualized m/m |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| M2 | +2.0% | +2.0% | +2.4% |
| M3 | +1.2% | +1.2% | +1.4% |
| M1 | −0.2% | 0.0% | — |
| Broad liquidity L | +4.3% | +4.4% | +2.9% |

M2 remained below May's +2.5% y/y peak and M3 below May's +1.7%. Improved sequential growth since the June–August lull is not a new acceleration regime. The Reuters levels refer to seasonally adjusted average amounts outstanding; official unadjusted levels are different and must not be conflated.

Deposit composition and instrument substitution:

| Component | September y/y | August y/y |
|---|---:|---:|
| Currency in circulation | −1.3% | −1.3% |
| Deposit money | 0.0% | +0.1% |
| Quasi-money | +4.7% | +4.3% |
| Certificates of deposit | −14.0% | −8.2% |

NSA levels: deposit money ¥972.5tn from ¥977.9tn; quasi-money ¥535.7tn from ¥530.6tn; currency ¥109.4tn from ¥109.9tn. Movement away from transactions toward interest-bearing instruments is plausible under rising rates but not directly traceable; coverage, seasonal factors and institutional reallocations matter. CDs are falling sharply, so not all deposit products participate equally. Nor does a change in deposit mix prove aggregate bank funding contraction.

Broad liquidity versus M3:

| Component | September y/y | September level |
|---|---:|---:|
| M3 | +1.2% | ¥1,638.3tn |
| Pecuniary trusts | +12.0% | ¥492.8tn |
| Investment trusts | +14.0% | ¥128.8tn |
| Government securities | +19.5% | ¥48.8tn |
| Foreign bonds | +5.8% | ¥32.5tn |

Investment trusts accelerated from +13.8% to +14.0%; the BoJ series tracks cumulative net investments rather than simple market-value moves. JGB holdings within L rose +19.5% from +19.0%, consistent with rising domestic yield appeal but not proof that purchases were financed by selling overseas assets. Pecuniary trusts, up 12.0%, help explain why L grows more rapidly than conventional money. Financial-asset accumulation is not identical to funds available for current consumption.

Monetary transmission, inflation and revisions. The BoJ may reduce reserves/monetary base while private bank deposits and L expand through distinct credit, cash and portfolio channels. Positive M2 does not prove all borrowers face easy conditions; falling reserve money does not prove broad contraction. Against +6.8% CGPI, +24.5% yen-based import prices and −3.1% household real spending, M2 +2.0% and M3 +1.2% do not imply an obvious domestic money-fuelled overheating regime. This comparison is not a formal quantity-theory inference: credit, velocity, nominal output and composition are missing. BoJ revised January-onward observations and made a March 2026 periodic change to historical L methodology; the earlier April–May broad-liquidity shift is less informative than September's relative stability. Bank credit/loan pricing/standards are necessary to assess real restriction.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: no material change — gradual monetary normalization with continued broad-money expansion and increasing portfolio-allocation sensitivity to interest rates.

Context from prior regional live file: March–May money-stock releases showed continued monetary expansion without a meaningful liquidity-stress signal, while June M2 momentum briefly contracted despite positive annual growth. September confirms that broad-money growth remains positive and sequential momentum has recovered, although transaction deposits are weakening relative to time deposits and investment assets. Japan therefore remains in a normalization-without-broad-liquidity-contraction regime.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information: The data confirm benign aggregate liquidity while suggesting higher-rate-driven portfolio reallocation. They neither support a forced stop to tightening nor resolve October versus December, because inflation, wages, household spending and credit conditions dominate the policy reaction function.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate: Deposit and securities allocation can respond to yields without reflecting fresh demand for goods. Government-security accumulation does not establish overseas-asset liquidation or FX conversion; positive annual money growth does not preclude credit stress in selected firms. Little independent front-end JGB or JPY signal is warranted. Changing deposit costs may affect bank margins, but deposits' rate sensitivity and asset repricing must be observed first. No verified market repricing was obtained.

What to verify next: Lending volumes and standards; deposit rates and term allocation; BoJ Flow of Funds and securities flows; October M1/M2; private-liquidity response as the central-bank monetary base declines.

Macro configuration: low positive broad-money growth + shifting deposit mix + expanding trust/securities liquidity + normalization without generalized monetary contraction.

THURSDAY 15 OCT 2026

JAPAN — Core Private-Sector Machinery Orders MM / YY (Aug)

Release Date: 15 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 01:50

Importance: ***

PM FLASH: Core private machinery orders rose 5.2% m/m versus +3.0% poll and +18.6% y/y versus +15.7% expected, despite total machinery orders declining 3.7%.

PM READ-THROUGH: AI-linked and digital capex sustain Japan's corporate cycle, including both manufacturing and non-manufacturing private buyers. Lumpy plant orders and weaker overseas demand prevent declaring broad investment acceleration.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Debate: The three-month core-orders average improved 3.6%, yet volatile large projects and a declining total-orders measure challenge extrapolation. Key takeaways: Separately seasonally adjusted components do not sum mechanically; September must rise about 10.5% m/m for the Cabinet Office's Q3 +4.9% projection. Watch breadth, realized capex and AI dependence.

1. Reuters Data

Machinery Orders MM: 5,2% | Prior: -3,7% | Poll: 3,0% | Surprise: 2,20%

Machinery Orders YY: 18,6% | Prior: 11,2% | Poll: 15,7% | Surprise: 2,90%

2. Professional Market Commentary

The Cabinet Office confirmed a +5.2% m/m rebound in core private orders (versus −3.7% July) and +18.6% y/y growth, but kept its assessment at 'showing signs of recovering'. Both manufacturing and non-manufacturing core private categories rose. AI/semiconductor equipment, large industrial-plant contracts, factory automation, telecommunications, leasing and digitization supported domestic investment. By contrast, total orders fell −3.7% m/m because public and overseas demand weakened; large project timing remains a substantial caveat.

Keisuke Yoshii (Daiwa Institute of Research), in a 15 October release-specific assessment, highlighted +3.6% growth in the three-month moving average of core orders and projected moderate medium-term investment expansion from labour-saving equipment and AI capex; he flagged Middle East disruption, expensive inputs, material constraints and corporate caution. Jiji Press emphasized the AI impulse and gains in both private-sector groups; official language stayed more cautious about establishing a new regime. The differing institutional views concern persistence/breadth, not whether the published beat occurred. No clean JPY or JGB response was established.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

The August rebound strengthens the private-capex pipeline and Japan's broader industrial resilience without repairing the household-consumption channel. Double-digit annual growth and an improving three-month average reduce the chance that the print is exclusively mechanical payback for July, but alternating monthly moves, semiconductor concentration and exceptional nonferrous projects remain material sources of noise. Domestic private demand diverged from weaker total machinery orders and external purchases.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

Headline, underlying level and smoothed momentum:

| Indicator | August | July |
|---|---:|---:|
| Core orders m/m SA | +5.2% | −3.7% |
| Core orders y/y | +18.6% | +11.2% |
| Core orders level | ¥1,069.8bn | ¥1,016.9bn |
| Total machinery orders m/m | −3.7% | −5.5% |

Core exceeded m/m consensus by 2.2pp and y/y by 2.9pp. The core three-month average increased 3.6% in the Daiwa assessment; annual core growth was already double digits in July, so August is not solely payback. Nevertheless, total intake across demand categories remained negative and the Cabinet Office did not upgrade its assessment.

Manufacturing and non-manufacturing private buyer contributions, SA:

| Buyer category | Orders | m/m |
|---|---:|---:|
| Manufacturing | ¥527.0bn | +1.6% |
| Non-manufacturing ex ships/power | ¥541.3bn | +2.9% |
| Core private sector | ¥1,069.8bn | +5.2% |

Both groups rose, suggesting improvement beyond semiconductor producers. The group rates cannot be added to obtain +5.2% because series are separately seasonally adjusted. Improved non-manufacturing machinery investment is compatible with weak consumer-facing sentiment: firms may spend on automation and digital efficiency despite poor short-run sales or margins.

Concentrated industrial mechanisms. Nonferrous-metals machinery orders rose roughly 2.3 times with heat-exchanger/large plant contracts, after a very weak July. General-purpose and production machinery orders rose 9.3%, including semiconductor manufacturing equipment linked to AI. Other transport equipment, aviation-related activity and machine tools also improved. The October Reuters Tankan showed precision-machinery sentiment +38 (+9) and exceptionally high semiconductor-related orders. A single major plant contract is not equivalent to broad capex; the AI/semiconductor external investment cycle remains a concentrated driver.

Digital/non-manufacturing and total-orders divergence. Core non-manufacturing orders rose 2.9%, led by leasing, telecommunications, wholesale/retail and business/digital equipment. Labour-saving capex can continue under weak demand, so machinery demand is not proof of recovered household spending. Total machinery orders fell 3.7% to ¥4,041.9bn because government/public and overseas demand declined. The domestic private pipeline strengthened while the whole machinery-manufacturer order book deteriorated, limiting broad sector extrapolation.

Q3 arithmetic and exceptional monthly volatility:

| Item | Value (¥bn) |
|---|---:|
| Q2 actual | 3,116.2 |
| Q3 target (+4.9%) | 3,268.9 |
| July + August | 2,086.7 |
| September required | 1,182.2 |

The September level required for the Cabinet Office Q3 +4.9% q/q projection implies approximately +10.5% m/m from August ¥1,069.8bn. The July–August monthly average is only ~0.4% above Q2's monthly average, not roughly +2% as a preliminary scout claim suggested. March–August m/m sequence: March −9.4%, April +8.7%, May −12.4%, June +9.7%, July −3.7%, August +5.2%. Alternation makes smoothing, annual growth, subsequent realized capex and sector breadth more informative than the month-on-month surprise alone.

Cross-release constraints. The Tankan supports strong AI-linked manufacturing; household expenditure −3.1% y/y, weakening domestic service sentiment, expensive materials and rising financing costs argue against a fully generalized growth rebound. AI capex, orders from abroad, plant project timing and corporate cash constraints are possible weak points. The original official Q3 forecast remains demanding even after August's beat.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: improvement in the private-capex pipeline, with persistent volatility and AI-related concentration risk; no broad investment-regime break.

Context from prior regional live file: April's 8.7% machinery-orders rebound was reversed by May's 12.4% collapse, reopening concern about a stop-start investment cycle. June recovered strongly before July weakened again; August's 5.2% rebound and 18.6% annual growth strengthen the evidence that the underlying capital-investment cycle remains positive. Japan's capex trajectory is improving but remains volatile and partially dependent on semiconductor-related demand.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information: Broader domestic private capital-equipment ordering improves the BoJ's growth-resilience case despite fragile households. It mildly supports gradual tightening through investment stability, without resolving the next-meeting question or implying the entire machinery complex is recovering.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate: AI and factory modernization may be partly insulated from short-term consumer demand and financing costs, yet exceptional nonferrous plants, overseas order weakness and steep required September growth could cause the cycle to appear stronger than realized capex. Higher industrial and energy input costs may also delay projects. Front-end JGB and JPY normalization effects are only conditionally and modestly hawkish; actual price responses were not identified. Equities linked to semiconductor equipment, industrial automation and capital goods have more direct sensitivity than the broad domestic index.

What to verify next: September core orders versus +10.5% monthly requirement; demand excluding semiconductors and large plant contracts; subsequent realized capex, BoJ Tankan investment plans, global AI-capex revisions and overseas machinery orders; sustained non-manufacturing investment despite weak consumption.

Macro configuration: recovering but volatile private capital spending + AI/automation leadership + weakening total/foreign order intake + households lagging + gradual BoJ normalization intact.

# D. CENTRAL BANK TAPE

No validated central-bank tape supplied for this window.

# E. SIGNAL TENSION CHECK

**Main contradiction:**

Corporate capex, AI-linked manufacturing and services employment remain resilient, while household consumption and domestic-facing business confidence continue deteriorating. Inflation is still widespread, but producer-price momentum is easing and the wage–consumption transmission remains unconvincing.

**Why it matters:**

The BoJ can justify continued normalization through inflation persistence and corporate resilience, but faster tightening risks intensifying a purchasing-power squeeze that has not yet been offset by stronger domestic demand.

**Implication:**

Gradual normalization remains the most defensible interpretation. An October hike requires stronger confirmation of domestically sustained inflation; continued consumption weakness and easing underlying prices would favour December or a later move without necessarily changing the policy destination.

# F. 5 KEY RELEASES TO DIG INTO

*** Corporate Goods Price Index MM / YY (13 Oct)

Why it matters: The most important inflation-composition test. Producer inflation undershot by 0.8pp, but agricultural and petroleum-price declines exceeded the headline monthly fall, concealing continuing industrial-price pressure and imported-energy risks. Essential for distinguishing lower near-term hike urgency from a genuine change in the BoJ's terminal-rate trajectory.

*** Household Spending MM / YY (9 Oct)

Why it matters: Ninth consecutive annual real-spending contraction despite positive real wages. Weather and administrative distortions complicate interpretation, but the cumulative consumption weakness challenges the assumption that the wage–price cycle has become self-sustaining through household demand.

*** Reuters Tankan Non-Manufacturing / Manufacturing Sentiment (7 Oct)

Why it matters: Clearest corporate-sector divergence. Manufacturing confidence strengthened to +22, supported by semiconductor activity, while non-manufacturing sentiment dropped six points to +23. Consumer-facing weakness and explicit financing-cost concerns reveal an increasingly restrictive domestic transmission channel.

*** Core Machinery Orders MM / YY (15 Oct)

Why it matters: The +5.2% monthly and +18.6% annual readings confirm resilience in private capital spending, including non-manufacturing digital investment. Exceptional industrial projects, weaker total orders and a demanding September requirement for the Q3 projection limit extrapolation into broad investment acceleration.

*** S&P Global Services PMI / Composite Output Final (5 Oct)

Why it matters: Services PMI slowed to 51.3 while employment and backlogs strengthened and output-price pressure remained historically high. The clearest release-level evidence that weaker activity momentum can coexist with inflation persistence, complicating the BoJ's timing decision.

# G. RED TEAM QUESTIONS

1. **Inflation composition:** After excluding agriculture and petroleum, does September CGPI still show convincing disinflation, or does the positive residual producer basket imply continued downstream price pressure?

2. **Wage–consumption transmission:** Once weather, education subsidies and irregular durable purchases are controlled for, is the ninth consecutive real-spending contraction evidence of persistent underlying demand weakness despite positive real wages?

3. **BoJ timing:** Does underlying services inflation and wage persistence justify another hike immediately after September, or is the case for October being inferred excessively from elevated imported costs and corporate-price diffusion?

4. **AI-capex concentration:** Would the improvement in core machinery orders survive excluding semiconductor equipment and exceptional nonferrous plant contracts? What subsequent breadth or realized-capex evidence would falsify investment resilience?

5. **External income versus spot flows:** How much of the ¥5.133tn primary-income surplus is actually converted into yen, and can sustained private repatriation be distinguished from the large intervention-related financial transactions?

6. **Services margins and labour:** Are rising employment and backlogs evidence of durable domestic demand, or are they compatible with deteriorating margins, labour shortages and temporary disruption despite weaker orders and sentiment?

7. **Monetary transmission:** Does the shift toward time deposits, combined with corporate financing-cost complaints, precede measurable bank-credit tightening, or is it benign portfolio substitution without a material constraint on domestic investment?

# H. CURRENCY MARKET-CONSENSUS SCAN

## Bottom line

**Pair convention: USD/JPY. Lower USD/JPY = stronger JPY.**

The publicly visible institutional bias during 4–17 October was **moderately constructive JPY over the medium term, but broadly neutral and range-bound tactically**. Strategists increasingly recognized constraints on further USD/JPY upside around 160, given intervention risk and prospective BoJ tightening. Yet a decisive yen appreciation remained conditional on narrowing US–Japan policy differentials, less aggressive Fed pricing and actual improvement in Japan's underlying domestic inflation and growth transmission.

The starting forecast distribution was not aggressively bullish yen. MUFG entered October targeting USD/JPY 155 at end-Q4 versus 156 in its September forecast, while Desjardins anticipated continued volatility around 160 in coming months before a potential move toward 150 in 2027. Rabobank maintained 155 over three months, whereas ING's 12 October assessment projected 158 across one, three and six months. This was a **155–160 indicative tactical forecast distribution**, rather than a uniform institutional call for rapid yen appreciation. Sources: MUFG, 1 October 2026 (https://www.mufgresearch.com/fx/monthly-foreign-exchange-outlook-october-2026); Desjardins, 2 October 2026 (https://www.desjardins.com/en/savings-investment/economic-studies/fx-analysis-october-2026.html); ING, 12 October 2026 (https://think.ing.com/articles/g10-fx-talking-october-2026).

By the end of the window, USD/JPY traded around 157.8–158.0 on 16 October, compared with ING's 158.46 reference on 12 October. Reuters described the yen as recovering modestly during that final week after four successive weeks of depreciation, helped by reduced Fed hike expectations and pressure on crowded AI-related positions. Spot had not converged toward the stronger-yen 155 target cluster, but it was already close to the neutral 158 tactical forecasts. There was no confirmed broad public-bank target reset between 12 and 17 October. Source: Reuters, 16 October 2026 (https://www.marketscreener.com/news/dollar-set-for-weekly-loss-as-fed-hike-bets-fade-ai-clouds-outlook-ce785cdadc88f325).

**Public-bank Q4 2026 / 1–3M indicative range:** USD/JPY 155–160. The observation horizons differ slightly; this is a visible institutional distribution, not a formal statistical consensus interval.

**Constructive JPY cluster:** USD/JPY 155–156, represented by MUFG's Q4 155, its published consensus benchmark of 156 and Rabobank's three-month 155.

**More cautious / tactical view:** USD/JPY 158–160, represented by ING's 158 short-horizon profile and Desjardins' expectation of volatility around 160.

**Bias at window close:** Neutral/range-bound tactically; moderate JPY appreciation expected over subsequent quarters, conditional on policy convergence. Limited upside in USD/JPY above 160, but insufficient evidence for an immediate sustained decline toward 150.

## What changed through the data window

**At the opening — lower targets, but limited conviction.**

MUFG's 1 October global outlook revised its Q4 USD/JPY forecast from 156 to 155 and its assessment of the BoJ terminal policy rate higher. Its 5 October Japan-specific report simultaneously warned that persistent US rate support, energy-import exposure and Japanese fiscal concerns could continue weighing on the yen. The stronger JPY forecast did not mean the bank expected a rapid currency revaluation. Intervention risk primarily constrained further depreciation. Source: MUFG Japan monthly, 5 October 2026 (https://mufgresearch.com/fx/jpy-monthly-october-2026/).

**During the window — policy convergence traded against carry and imported-cost pressures.**

Rabobank's 6 October stance favoured a decline toward 155 over three months, partly on the expectation that markets had priced excessive future Fed tightening. ING's 12 October publication instead characterized the yen re-rating as paused: USD/JPY around 158, with the next BoJ hike more likely in December or January than October. The divergence was primarily over the speed at which relative monetary policy would become supportive of JPY, not over the existence of intervention risk near 160. Sources: Rabobank view relayed 6 October 2026 (https://www.tmgmsea.com/en/analysis/market-news/article/japanese-yen-range-trade-holds-against-us-dollar-as-fed-path-questioned-rabobank-202610061341); ING, 12 October 2026 (https://think.ing.com/articles/g10-fx-talking-october-2026).

**At the close — modest spot recovery, not a structural consensus shift.**

On 16 October, Reuters recorded USD/JPY around 157.79, with US tightening expectations easing despite sticky inflation and the market reassessing the sustainability of AI-related financial exposure. The spot adjustment improved the yen's immediate backdrop but did not validate a lasting narrowing in US–Japan policy differentials. The available published forecast set remained predominantly a 155–160 medium-near-term framework.

Crucially, **spot moved modestly within an established forecast distribution; a synchronized in-window upward revision to JPY forecasts is not established**.

## Main consensus drivers

**US–Japan policy differential / carry:**

The Fed's policy outlook remained the central constraint on sustained yen appreciation. Continued BoJ normalization was constructive, but the market questioned the case for consecutive hikes after September. A more dovish Fed repricing offered potentially faster JPY support than a marginally earlier BoJ move alone.

**Intervention / official resistance:**

USD/JPY around or above 160 was widely treated as a region of escalating official intervention risk, including possible US–Japan coordination. This created asymmetric upside risk for short-JPY positions, but intervention without a narrowing interest-rate differential was not considered a durable appreciation mechanism.

**External income versus energy-import exposure:**

Japan's substantial foreign-asset income provided a structural external cushion, but its weaker goods balance and elevated energy-import costs limited direct spot support. Foreign earnings were not automatically converted into yen. Rising imported prices simultaneously increased pressure on the BoJ and reduced Japanese purchasing power.

**Domestic financial flows / fiscal credibility:**

Higher JGB yields and possible domestic pension allocation changes offered a potential yen-supportive portfolio channel. However, evidence of large and persistent private repatriation remained insufficient, while fiscal expansion concerns and the attractiveness of US-dollar assets continued restraining conviction.

## Where consensus could be wrong

**Consensus too bullish JPY:**

Fed tightening expectations remain persistent, the BoJ waits longer than expected, imported-energy inflation further damages household demand, and private capital remains invested abroad. Under that combination, intervention may repeatedly interrupt USD/JPY upside without creating lasting yen appreciation.

**Consensus too cautious JPY:**

US rate expectations fall faster, underlying domestic inflation sustains BoJ normalization, and Japanese institutional investors materially increase domestic-asset allocations. A simultaneous narrowing in rate differentials and observable private repatriation could push USD/JPY below the 155 target cluster more quickly than tactical forecasts imply.

**Key falsification:**

A sustained narrowing of US–Japan front-end rate differentials accompanied by verified private yen repatriation and continued domestic inflation persistence would strengthen the constructive JPY case. Conversely, a renewed Fed–BoJ divergence and sustained USD/JPY appreciation through the intervention-sensitive 160 area would challenge it.

DATA WINDOW: 27 September 2026 to 3 October 2026

A. JPY WEEK — MACRO SYNTHESIS

Japan ends the 27 September–3 October window in a two-speed, increasingly inflation-constrained expansion: corporate manufacturing, exports, capital spending and profits remain resilient, while hard industrial activity and household-facing demand have weakened. Q3 Tankan manufacturing conditions improved across firm sizes (large DI 22 to 24; small 9 to 14), FY2026 large-firm capex intentions remained +11.3%, and the manufacturing PMI held an expansionary 54.1. But August industrial production fell 1.7% m/m versus +1.7% expected, shipments declined 2.5%, retail sales contracted 1.2% m/m SA, and housing starts' seasonally adjusted run rate fell despite +6.1% annual growth distorted by 2025 regulation effects. The labor market is structurally tight (Tankan all-firm labor-shortage DI -38), yet consumer-services vacancies and hiring are easing. State transition: sustained corporate/AI-export resilience with worsening realized production and consumer-demand confirmation, not a synchronized growth acceleration.

Inflation replaces growth as the more acute immediate BOJ constraint. September Tokyo ex-fresh-food CPI jumped to 2.7% from 1.8%, +0.3pp versus poll, with some genuine broadening in goods and distributional measures; however, water and nursery-fee effects explain a large part of the annual rise, and the wage-sensitive services acceleration is unproven. August services PPI rose to 3.7% but remained 3.2% excluding international transportation, confirming persistence more than a domestic inflation breakout. BOJ balance-sheet normalization continues (September monetary base -15.21% y/y, bank reserves the main contraction channel), and Tankan corporate loan-cost DI rose from 61 to 68, without demonstrable financing-access stress. The policy configuration is a continuing tightening bias from 1.25%, tempered by weak consumption, supply-distorted industrial output and uncertain post-September-rate-hike transmission; the provided macro archive favors October optionality and a credible December window, not an automatic consecutive hike.

B. JPY MACRO SIGNAL SCOREBOARD

Growth: ~
Supporting evidence:
September manufacturing PMI at 54.1 and the Tankan large-manufacturer DI at 24 and small-manufacturer DI at 14 support continued factory resilience; large-firm capex plans at +11.3% and sharply improved manufacturing profits provide an investment buffer. Yet August industrial output -1.7% m/m versus +1.7% expected, shipments -2.5%, retail sales -1.2% m/m SA and declining sequential housing starts undermine the realized-demand picture. July leading-index revision to 117.7 (+1.5 points) remains positive but inventory-heavy. Surveys and hard data remain divergent.

Labor: ~
Supporting evidence:
August unemployment edged up to 2.5% from 2.4% while the Tankan continued to report acute labor scarcity (employment conditions DI -38 for all firms, -42 at small firms). Manufacturing PMI hiring was strong, but official and recruitment data show softer marginal vacancies and weaker consumer-facing employment, especially retail and accommodation/food. Tight labor supply coexists with uneven demand and no decisive acceleration in aggregate hiring.

Inflation: -
Supporting evidence:
Tokyo ex-fresh-food CPI rose to 2.7% y/y from 1.8%, above the 2.4% poll; ex-fresh-food-and-energy CPI reached 3.0%, and several adjusted inflation-distribution measures firmed. Water and nursery-fee effects account for a large part of the increase, however. August SPPI rose to 3.7% from 3.6% but the ex-international-transportation reading stayed at 3.2%; the wage-sensitive SPPI component rose only to 2.8% from 2.7%. Adverse inflation persistence, with uncertain domestic acceleration.

Financial Conditions: -
Supporting evidence:
BOJ monetary base contracted 15.21% y/y in September, overwhelmingly through declining reserve/current-account balances; average bank current accounts fell to JPY 413.7tn from 423.4tn. Tankan loan-interest-rate DI increased from 61 to 68 (small firms 61 to 70), while lending-attitude and financial-position DIs stayed at 13 and 11. Higher price of credit and shrinking BOJ liquidity, but not verified availability or funding stress.

Policy Constraint: -
Supporting evidence:
The inflation upside, persistent price-setting intentions and labor shortages argue for further normalization; yet the underlying services signal is ambiguous, August industrial/retail activity weakened and Tankan outlooks deteriorated across firm sizes. Most Tankan responses preceded the 18 September BOJ hike to 1.25%, limiting inference about completed monetary transmission. October-versus-December timing remains conditional on national CPI, real consumption, wages and realized production, not determined by monetary-base shrinkage.
