2. Professional Market Commentary

The September Tankan confirmed that Japan's corporate sector remains resilient, but delivered a less uniformly hawkish signal than the June survey.

Reuters and institutional commentary emphasized the divergence between improving manufacturing confidence, particularly among firms exposed to AI-related demand, and softer non-manufacturing conditions. Large manufacturers reached their strongest sentiment reading since 2018, while large non-manufacturers weakened from elevated levels.

Differentiated institutional views:
- Takeshi Minami — Norinchukin Research Institute: manufacturing benefited from AI demand and easing supply constraints, but weak durable-goods consumption and mixed services sentiment reduced the case for an immediate follow-up hike.
- Yoshiki Shinke — Dai-ichi Life Research Institute: corporate fundamentals remain solid; the services decline is modest relative to the high starting level, while upward profit revisions and investment plans argue against interpreting the survey as a growth downturn. December rather than October is his central timing expectation.
- Kanako Nakamura — Daiwa Institute of Research: AI demand supports manufacturers, but fuel, labour and financing costs weigh on consumer-facing services. The survey remains consistent with accommodative financial conditions, although rising borrowing costs are becoming visible.
- Koichi Fujishiro — Dai-ichi Life Research Institute: corporate earnings and price-setting capacity remain more robust than sentiment alone suggests; loan-rate increases have not yet translated into materially tighter reported access to finance.
- Matt Simpson — StoneX: the survey sustains the case for additional normalization without requiring a faster tightening pace; softer JPY OIS pricing limits near-term yen support.
- Hideyuki Ishiguro — Nomura Asset Management: upward earnings revisions and the persistence of price pass-through support Japanese equities despite the prospect of additional BOJ tightening.

The central institutional debate is therefore timing rather than direction: corporate resilience, labour scarcity and elevated inflation expectations support further hikes, but softer forward sentiment, easing input-price pressures and incomplete transmission of September's hike argue against accelerating immediately.

Market reaction was not cleanly attributable to Tankan alone. The scout records weaker JPY and easing six-month to one-year OIS pricing during a window that also contained the BOJ Summary of Opinions and external US-inflation influences. The combined reaction was inconsistent with a decisive hawkish Tankan surprise.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

The Tankan confirms a durable but internally divergent corporate expansion. Manufacturing conditions improved across firm sizes and profit plans were revised substantially upward, while large non-manufacturing sentiment and consumer-service subindustries softened. Strong large-company capex intentions contrast with still-negative small-firm plans.

Price-setting capacity and labour shortages remain material, but inflation expectations did not re-accelerate. Loan interest-cost perceptions rose without deterioration in lending attitudes or firms’ current financial positions. Most survey responses preceded the September BOJ hike, making this an incomplete read on the latest transmission shock.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

B1. Manufacturing breadth and consumer-facing services divergence

Focus — Manufacturing improvement is broader across firm sizes, but uneven across industries.
Large manufacturing DI increased from 22 to 24, below the consensus of 25. The improvement extends across medium-sized manufacturers (17 to 23) and small manufacturers (9 to 14), the latter materially outperforming expectations. The size distribution is important: manufacturing strength is no longer confined to large exporters, unlike the earlier recovery phase.

Composition is less uniformly positive:
- Nine of sixteen large manufacturing industries improved, with two unchanged and five deteriorating.
- Basic-materials DI rose from 18 to 25, exceeding the improvement in processing industries (23 to 25).
- Petroleum and coal increased from 9 to 36, an unusually large contribution that should not be extrapolated as broad industrial acceleration.
- Production machinery improved from 36 to 43, electrical machinery from 29 to 33, and nonferrous metals from 36 to 45, consistent with semiconductor, electrical infrastructure and AI-investment exposure.
- Motor vehicles remained subdued at 11 versus 12, while food and beverages fell from 9 to 3.

Underlying demand measures provide additional confirmation: large manufacturers' domestic supply-demand DI improved from -4 to -1 and overseas demand from -3 to -1, while excess finished-goods inventories eased from 13 to 11. The manufacturing recovery therefore has identifiable demand support, but the headline should not be treated as evidence of uniformly accelerating final demand.

Focus — Services weakness is concentrated in consumer-facing industries, although breadth deteriorated.
Large non-manufacturing DI fell from 37 to 35, remaining historically elevated but declining for the first time in five quarters. Nine of twelve industries weakened.

The key declines were:
- Accommodation, eating and drinking: 46 to 38.
- Retail: 33 to 29.
- Transport and postal services: 25 to 21.
- Services for individuals: 37 to 34.
- Business services: 43 to 39.

Construction improved from 52 to 56 and real estate remained at 52.

This distinction matters. The decline does not indicate a generalized corporate contraction: capital-intensive and property-linked industries remain exceptionally confident. Rather, weakness is concentrated in activities dependent on household spending, tourism and the ability to pass through rising costs. The negative services breadth, however, makes it difficult to dismiss the entire deterioration as statistical noise. Weather, supply disruptions and weaker visitor flows could explain part of the decline, but the survey's response timing limits confident attribution to late-September disturbances.

B2. Forward DIs, profitability and capital-expenditure quality

Focus — Forward sentiment weakens universally, but expected levels remain expansionary.
December forecasts fall across every reported size-sector aggregate:
- Large manufacturing: 24 to 21.
- Large non-manufacturing: 35 to 30.
- Medium manufacturing: 23 to 18.
- Small manufacturing: 14 to 12.
- Small non-manufacturing: 15 to 10.

The common direction suggests a widespread perception of risk from energy costs, financing rates and weaker final demand. However, the distinction between forecast deterioration and an actual contraction is essential. Every aggregate remains positive, and conservative forward responses are common when current sentiment is elevated.

A further technical distinction: the Reuters outlook-row prior values are forecasts made in the June survey for September, not June actual conditions. Comparing the September actual readings against the newly reported December forecasts is the relevant within-survey forward-momentum test.

Focus — Profit revisions are substantially stronger than the sentiment surprise.
FY2026 current-profit plans were revised materially upward:
- Large manufacturing profits: +13.6% y/y, with a +21.7% upward revision to the June plan.
- Large non-manufacturing: -4.7% y/y, despite a +1.8% revision.
- Large all industries: +4.3% y/y, versus approximately -6.5% implied by the June plan.
- All enterprises: +2.2% y/y, with a +9.4% revision.

Large manufacturing exports are planned at +10.3% y/y, versus domestic sales of +5.9%. This is a more constructive earnings signal than the modest manufacturing sentiment surprise implies. Better external demand, cost pass-through and margins are cushioning the corporate sector. But the distribution matters: manufacturing profitability has improved much more than services profitability, and small-enterprise profit growth remains negative. The first-half/second-half split in manufacturing profits (+31.1% versus -3.1%) also argues against extrapolating the strong current earnings revisions mechanically into 2027.

Focus — Investment remains a central growth buffer, with persistent SME divergence.
FY2026 fixed-investment plans, including land but excluding software and R&D:
- Large all industries: +11.3% y/y, versus +11.5% in June and a +12.3% consensus.
- Large manufacturing: +11.6%.
- Large non-manufacturing: +11.2%.
- Small all industries: -4.7%, versus -8.3% in June.
- All enterprises: +7.6%.

The miss versus consensus does not negate the absolute strength of large-firm capex intentions. The improvement in small-firm plans is meaningful, particularly in non-manufacturing, but the planned aggregate investment level still contracts. Software, R&D and non-land investment plans are +9.3% across all firms, which supports the interpretation that investment in productive capacity and technology remains more resilient than the headline land-inclusive measure alone suggests. The survey therefore confirms strong large-company investment demand, not a fully synchronized investment recovery across the economy.

B3. Pricing power, inflation expectations and labour scarcity

Focus — Inflation expectations are persistent, not re-accelerating.
All-enterprise expectations for general prices:
- One year: 2.6%, versus 2.7% in June.
- Three years: 2.6%, unchanged.
- Five years: 2.5%, versus 2.6%.

Manufacturing input-price DI eased from 62 to 59 among large firms and from 76 to 71 among small firms. Large manufacturing output-price DI stayed at 40.

This combination matters for the inflation regime:
- Upstream cost pressures are moderating.
- Selling-price behavior has not materially weakened.
- Medium-term inflation expectations remain above 2%.
- Small manufacturers expect output-price DI to increase from 41 to 47 in December.

The evidence is therefore more consistent with sustained price-setting capacity than renewed inflation acceleration. Importantly, firms' one-year expectations for their own selling prices (+3.5%) exceed expectations for general prices (+2.6%). These are different measurement concepts, but the gap suggests that price-setting intentions remain active.

Focus — Labour shortages remain a structural constraint.
Employment conditions DI, excessive minus insufficient:
- All enterprises: -37 to -38; December forecast -41.
- Large firms: -28 to -29.
- Small firms: -39 to -42; December forecast -45.
- Small non-manufacturing: -46 to -47; December forecast -50.

Labour scarcity persists despite weakening sentiment in consumer-facing services. This is important for the BOJ because weaker services activity need not immediately translate into softer wage pressure when labour supply remains constrained. The stronger shortage signal at small firms also points to possible wage-cost and margin pressure where pricing power is least secure.

B4. Monetary transmission and survey-timing limits

Focus — Monetary transmission is emerging in interest costs, but not yet in financing availability.
The all-enterprise loan-interest-rate DI rose from 61 to 68, with a December forecast of 71. Small firms registered a particularly pronounced increase from 61 to 70, with a December expectation of 74.

By contrast:
- Financial-position DI remained at 11.
- Lending-attitude DI remained at 13.
- Large-firm financial-position DI improved slightly.

This is the key distinction between rising costs of credit and restrictive access to credit. BOJ tightening is affecting borrowing rates, but there is not yet evidence of a broad financing squeeze through either lending willingness or firms' self-reported liquidity. The survey nonetheless provides an early transmission warning: if loan costs continue rising while household-facing revenues soften, financial conditions may become more restrictive even without an immediate banking-credit shock.

Focus — Survey timing materially limits the inference about the latest BOJ hike.
The survey covered 26 August–30 September, with Reuters reporting that roughly 70% of firms responded by 9 September, before the 18 September rate increase. The financial-position and capex readings therefore predominantly reflect conditions before that latest adjustment. They should not be treated as conclusive evidence that firms can absorb the full cumulative impact of BOJ normalization without damage.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: continuation of corporate resilience, with stabilization of inflation pressure and incipient monetary-transmission deterioration.

Context from prior regional live file:
The Q1 Tankan had shown resilient large firms but weak small-firm investment, while the June survey strengthened manufacturing, capex and inflation expectations without eliminating that divergence. September extends the manufacturing recovery to smaller firms and confirms stronger earnings, but weak consumer-facing services and rising borrowing costs prevent a broad-based growth upgrade. Japan remains in a resilient-corporate / constrained-household regime, with policy normalization increasingly dependent on the durability of domestic demand.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information:
The Tankan keeps the BOJ tightening cycle intact but weakens the case for consecutive hikes in September and October.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate:
The combination of resilient corporate earnings, positive investment plans, persistent labour shortages and above-target inflation expectations supports additional normalization from the 1.25% policy rate.

However, the release does not signal a new inflation acceleration or a decisive broadening of domestic demand.

Policy mapping:
- October: weaker justification for an immediate follow-up hike, especially given the survey's incomplete coverage of September tightening.
- December: remains a plausible normalization window if subsequent activity, wage and inflation data confirm resilience.
- Medium term: the BOJ retains scope to tighten, but the impact of cumulative rate increases on smaller firms and household-facing industries becomes increasingly relevant.

Shinke's December base case and Umezawa's caution about further consecutive hikes are consistent with the release's balance of evidence. The Tankan supports policy optionality rather than any particular meeting outcome.

Rates and FX:
The market distinction is between the direction of policy and the speed of delivery.

If the BOJ remains committed to eventual normalization but markets reduce expectations for rapid increases, JPY front-end rates can soften even as the longer-term policy bias stays hawkish.

StoneX reported easing in six-month to one-year JPY OIS tenors. The scout also records yen depreciation in the combined Tankan / Summary of Opinions window. Neither observation establishes a Tankan-specific causal price reaction.

For JPY, the principal near-term sensitivity is therefore the change in expected BOJ timing relative to foreign front-end rates, not simply whether Tankan sentiment is positive.

Equities:
Corporate earnings revisions, AI-related industrial demand and continued capex offer support to Japanese equities.

Rising borrowing costs are not necessarily negative for aggregate equities if earnings growth offsets the increase in the discount rate. Sector differentiation becomes more important, however: globally exposed manufacturers and firms with strong pricing power are better positioned than domestic consumer businesses with weak volumes and limited margin flexibility.

What to verify next:
- Subsequent domestic spending and services releases: distinguish temporary weather-related weakness from sustained real-consumption deterioration.
- Wage developments and labour-market data: test whether persistent recruitment shortages continue feeding wage growth.
- October BOJ forecasts and communication: clarify whether the committee prioritizes persistent inflation and wage dynamics or the lagged effects of tightening.
- December Tankan: check whether the common deterioration in outlook DIs materializes and whether rising loan costs begin to affect investment plans and financial-position DIs.

Macro configuration: resilient manufacturing and investment, softer household-facing services, entrenched wage and price-setting pressures, and the first signs of more restrictive borrowing costs.

The survey supports further BOJ normalization, but not an acceleration of the hiking cycle on its own.

JAPAN — S&P Global Manufacturing PMI Final, SA (Sep)
Release Date: 1 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 02:30
Importance: ***

PM FLASH: September manufacturing PMI finalized at 54.1, unchanged from flash but down from 54.9 in August; export orders remained strong as overall output and order growth slowed.

PM READ-THROUGH: The release confirms export- and AI-supported manufacturing resilience with no fresh headline surprise. Supply delays and inventories reduce the reliability of extrapolating PMI strength to factory volumes.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Debate: Expansionary surveys, exports and hiring argue against an industrial slump, but August official output and shipments fell sharply. Key takeaways: Supplier bottlenecks can inflate diffusion readings while constraining deliveries; customer destocking and a disputed finished-goods inventory signal cloud order quality. Verify September production, shipments and the primary inventory breakdown.

1. Reuters Data

S&P Global Mfg PMI Final SA: 54,1 | Prior: 54,1

RIC;Date;Time;Rel;Indicator;Period;Actual;Prior;Revised;Poll;Surprise
JPRPMI=ECI;1 oct;02:30;High;S&P Global Mfg PMI Final SA;Sep;54,1;54,1;;;

2. Professional Market Commentary

Japan's final manufacturing PMI confirmed a solid expansion in September, but one that had lost momentum relative to August. The final reading of 54.1 matched the flash estimate and was below August's 54.9. As a result, the final publication delivered no additional headline surprise relative to the preliminary survey. Reuters and S&P Global emphasized the distinction between slower manufacturing growth and an outright deterioration in activity.

Differentiated professional observations:
- Output and new orders continued growing, but at their slowest rates in several months.
- New export orders remained strong, supported by demand from Asia and the United States.
- Employment increased rapidly despite slower output and orders.
- Supplier delivery times deteriorated as electronic-component shortages and technology-related demand constrained supply.
- Input-cost inflation moderated but remained elevated, while manufacturers continued raising selling prices.
- Business confidence remained above its historical average, supported by semiconductor and AI-related demand.

Annabel Fiddes — S&P Global Market Intelligence: the final survey showed slower output and order growth, with some customers reducing inventory accumulation after an earlier stockbuilding phase. She nevertheless characterized manufacturing growth as solid over the quarter.

Chris Williamson — S&P Global Market Intelligence: his earlier September flash analysis emphasized robust export-led manufacturing activity, supply-chain delays and persistent cost inflation, all of which maintained pressure on the BOJ to consider further tightening. This was flash-period commentary, not an independent reaction to the final release. The main professional framing is therefore moderation from strong levels, not a manufacturing downturn. The final release is most useful for its component detail rather than its unchanged headline. No independent release-specific external economist debate was identified.

No release-specific market reaction was confirmed. The PMI followed the BOJ Tankan and Summary of Opinions by approximately forty minutes and matched the flash, limiting the basis for attributing the morning yen move to this publication.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

Manufacturing remained expansionary at 54.1, yet the unchanged final reading contained no new flash-level surprise and momentum slowed versus August. Exports and hiring were comparatively strong, while new-order and output growth moderated.

Supply-chain delays and inventory adjustments complicate the index: slower deliveries may reflect disruption rather than pure demand strength. The PMI and hard industrial data measure different populations, periods and concepts; the September official production outcome is needed before the survey-led recovery can be treated as realized output growth.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

B1. Headline momentum, export orders and hiring

Focus — The headline remains strong, but momentum has moderated.
The final PMI stood at 54.1. This compares with 54.9 in August and 54.5 in July. The index remained above the 50 expansion threshold for a ninth consecutive month. September was the weakest reading in six months, but the level still indicates a substantial expansion in the surveyed manufacturing sector. Importantly, the final index was unchanged from the flash. The distinction between the information contained in the flash release and information added by the final publication is relevant for market interpretation.

The headline slowdown was already known before the 1 October release. Its value for the weekly macro file is confirmation of the manufacturing expansion and additional evidence on the composition of activity, costs, hiring and inventories.

Focus — Output and new orders slowed while exports remained strong.
The pace of manufacturing output growth declined to a three-month low. New-order growth also slowed materially from August's exceptionally strong performance.

Some surveyed businesses reported weaker-than-expected sales and customers adjusting inventories after earlier stockpiling. This suggests that part of the previous order expansion may have reflected precautionary inventory accumulation rather than solely accelerating final consumption or investment. However, new export orders continued increasing strongly, close to August's multi-year high. External demand, particularly from Asian markets and the United States, remained an important support.

The domestic-versus-external distinction matters. Strong overseas orders can sustain Japanese manufacturing activity even when Japanese household spending and domestic services demand are weakening. The PMI is therefore consistent with a two-speed Japanese economy: export- and technology-exposed manufacturing remains comparatively resilient, while domestically oriented demand is less convincing.

Focus — Employment strength creates a favorable but potentially lagging signal.
Manufacturing employment rose at one of the fastest rates since 2018. This is consistent with continued corporate confidence and expectations of sustained future output. However, employment decisions may lag actual changes in orders. Firms experiencing persistent shortages of skilled workers can continue hiring through temporary slowdowns to preserve production capacity.

Moreover, the official Labour Force Survey showed manufacturing employment lower than a year earlier. These two observations are not mechanically contradictory: PMI employment measures surveyed firms' month-on-month direction, while the Labour Force Survey measures employment levels across a different population and period. The strong PMI hiring signal therefore suggests resilience among surveyed manufacturers, but does not prove that aggregate manufacturing employment has fully recovered.

B2. Supply bottlenecks, inventories and pricing

Focus — Supply-chain constraints remain material.
Supplier delivery times deteriorated at one of the fastest rates in approximately four years. Respondents cited shortages of electronic components and AI-related technology products. Delivery delays can influence the PMI headline because its construction treats lengthening supplier delivery times differently from conventional activity weakness.

When delivery delays reflect exceptionally strong demand, they may coincide with manufacturing expansion. When delays instead result from geopolitical disruption, transport constraints or shortages, they can contribute to an elevated composite PMI without implying equivalent growth in real output. This distinction is especially relevant given the disruption to Japanese vehicle production and the sharp increase in ocean freight costs reported in SPPI.

Supply constraints may simultaneously support the PMI headline and restrict realized production volumes. The strong index should therefore not be treated as a direct forecast of industrial-output growth of comparable magnitude.

Focus — Inventory behavior introduces a demand-quality warning.
The surveyed manufacturing sector reported an increase in finished-goods stocks for the first time in more than two years. Meanwhile, respondents described customers running down or adjusting earlier accumulated inventories. The combination is potentially important. A supplier can experience rising finished-goods inventories if production remains strong while shipments or new orders slow. Customers reducing precautionary stocks can also depress new orders even while underlying final consumption remains stable.

But the precise mechanism requires caution. The scout identified a discrepancy between secondary sources over whether the first inventory increase referred to finished goods or purchased inputs.

The balance of readable final-release coverage favors finished goods, but the official PMI bulletin was not available for direct verification. Consequently, the inventory increase should be regarded as a provisional composition signal rather than a decisive conclusion about involuntary stock accumulation.

Focus — Inflation pressure remains significant despite slower input-cost increases.
Manufacturers reported that input-price inflation moderated to a six-month low but remained rapid. Key cited cost pressures included raw materials, energy, transportation, staffing, electronic components and exchange-rate-related import costs. Output-price inflation remained among the strongest since late 2022. This combination is relevant for BOJ policy. Firms appear to retain pricing power despite a moderation in input-price momentum.

However, the data do not establish that stronger factory selling prices will transmit fully into consumer inflation. Part of the price pressure may be concentrated in intermediate goods, export-oriented products and internationally traded components. The PMI is therefore consistent with a persistent production-cost inflation regime, but not conclusive evidence of accelerating domestic household-demand inflation.

B3. PMI-versus-production reconciliation and inventory-cycle risk

Focus — PMI and industrial production provide materially different readings of manufacturing conditions.
The August official industrial-production release reported output -1.7% m/m, shipments -2.5%, and declining production in twelve of fifteen industries. The September manufacturing PMI, meanwhile, remained elevated at 54.1. The divergence has several possible explanations. First, the periods differ. August industrial production measures realized output during a month disrupted by the Kumamoto earthquake and typhoons, while September's PMI survey covers 8–24 September. Second, the concepts differ. The PMI measures directional survey responses, whereas industrial production measures actual physical output volumes.

Third, the coverage differs. The PMI may capture strong export-oriented and AI-related firms even when automobile and refinery disruptions weigh on aggregate industrial volumes. Fourth, supply constraints and delivery delays may raise aspects of the PMI while preventing stronger actual production. Fifth, firms can report improving conditions relative to the prior month without achieving the production levels previously planned. The appropriate conclusion is therefore not that either indicator is necessarily wrong. The PMI provides evidence of resilient manufacturing conditions in September, but the official production rebound remains unconfirmed.

Focus — The broader manufacturing regime remains export-led and exposed to inventory-cycle risk.
The best quarterly survey performance since early 2014 is a meaningful indicator of business confidence and demand strength. However, the slowdown in new orders from August's exceptional pace suggests that momentum may be normalizing. The inventory adjustment reported by customers is also important.

If earlier precautionary purchasing created an unusually strong order cycle, some slowing could occur even without weaker final demand. Alternatively, if end-market demand is deteriorating, order moderation may become more persistent and eventually affect production and employment. The distinction cannot be resolved from the PMI headline alone.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: continuation of manufacturing expansion, with deterioration in growth momentum and greater uncertainty over inventory and supply-chain quality.

Context from prior regional live file:
Japan's earlier manufacturing indicators showed a recovery supported by machinery and external demand, while official production remained volatile and vulnerable to supply interruptions. September's PMI sustains that expansionary survey trajectory but slows from August's stronger reading, contrasting with the recent deterioration in actual industrial output. The manufacturing regime remains resilient in sentiment and orders, while realized production still needs to confirm the recovery.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information:
The PMI supports the BOJ's normalization direction but adds little fresh impetus to the October timing decision because the headline merely confirms the flash.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate:
A 54.1 manufacturing PMI is difficult to reconcile with an imminent generalized industrial downturn. Strong exports, hiring and selling-price increases support a constructive corporate backdrop.

However, slower orders, inventory adjustment and the divergence from industrial production argue against interpreting the reading as an unambiguous acceleration in real activity.

Policy mapping:
- October: no decisive new reason to accelerate tightening beyond the information already contained in the flash.
- December: continued manufacturing expansion would support further normalization if domestic wages, consumption and inflation remain sufficiently resilient.
- Medium term: the BOJ should distinguish export-driven manufacturing strength from a self-sustaining domestic demand and wage-price cycle.

Rates and JPY:
A strong PMI can support Japanese front-end rates and the yen when it materially raises expectations for growth and BOJ tightening. But the unchanged final headline limits the incremental policy surprise.

Moreover, an export-led manufacturing expansion is less directly informative about domestic inflation persistence than wage-sensitive services and consumer prices. The PMI also followed the Tankan and BOJ Summary of Opinions during the same morning, limiting the possibility of isolating its market effect.

No release-specific FX or JGB reaction was verified.

Equity implications:
The survey remains supportive of exporters, semiconductor-related businesses and technology-supply-chain firms. Yet the equity signal is conditional on the conversion of strong orders into actual deliveries and earnings.

A stronger PMI driven partly by supplier delays or temporary inventory accumulation is less favorable than one driven by sustained final demand and rising shipments. The distinction is especially relevant for evaluating the durability of the AI-related manufacturing cycle.

What to verify next:
- September official industrial production: confirm whether realized activity catches up with survey strength.
- New export orders and actual export shipments: test demand persistence.
- Finished-goods inventories and customer destocking: distinguish intentional inventory rebuilding from involuntary accumulation.
- Supplier delivery times: identify whether constraints reflect strong demand or continuing disruption.
- Output-price inflation: verify whether manufacturing price-setting remains strong as input inflation moderates.

Macro configuration: strong export-oriented manufacturing, moderating new-order momentum, persistent pricing pressure and unresolved supply-chain/inventory distortions. The release offsets excessive recession pessimism, but does not establish a synchronized domestic recovery.

FRIDAY 2 OCT 2026

National CPI and post-subsidy measures must establish persistence.
