2. Professional Market Commentary

The September Tokyo CPI release delivered a significant upside inflation surprise, strengthening the case for further BOJ tightening after September's rate increase.

Reuters framed the print as evidence of spreading price pressure, with inflation returning above 2% and additional increases in food, household necessities and services. The professional debate was nevertheless divided over how much of the apparent acceleration represented genuine underlying inflation rather than the reversal of temporary subsidy effects.

Differentiated institutional views:
- Yoshiki Shinke — Dai-ichi Life Research Institute: the headline acceleration substantially overstates the change in underlying inflation because water-charge and nursery-fee effects explain much of the increase. Nevertheless, the remaining price pressure is significant, particularly in household goods, durable products and food. He expects national inflation to strengthen further, potentially exceeding 3%, and sees December as a plausible BOJ hike window.
- Takuya Hoshino — Dai-ichi Life Research Institute: BOJ-style underlying inflation indicators constructed for Tokyo accelerated across the board. The trimmed mean, weighted median, modal measure and special-factor-adjusted series collectively suggest that price pressure is not limited to a handful of volatile items.
- Masato Koike — Sompo Institute Plus: the combination of higher imported energy costs and secondary price effects should continue feeding inflation, supporting further BOJ tightening, with December preferred over an immediate October move.
- Taro Kimura — Bloomberg Economics: the result reinforces the risk that underlying inflation could settle above the BOJ's target, although his reported timing expectation remains December.
- Shotaro Kugo — Institute for International Monetary Affairs: rising corporate purchasing costs point to further price increases, particularly from October onward.

The important disagreement concerns services inflation.

Reuters emphasized the rise in services inflation to 2.3% as evidence of wage-cost pass-through. Shinke was more cautious, arguing that nursery-fee base effects and holiday-related accommodation prices make it premature to conclude that underlying services inflation has accelerated. Market-desk commentary, particularly investingLive, raised the possibility of renewed October-hike pricing following the inflation surprise. Named economists with explicit policy forecasts generally continued to favor December. The release therefore shifts the inflation debate in a hawkish direction without establishing that the BOJ needs to act immediately.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

Tokyo CPI delivered a significant upside surprise: ex-fresh-food inflation rose 0.9pp to 2.7% y/y, exceeding consensus by 0.3pp, with core-core and monthly measures strengthening. Yet water and childcare effects explain a large part of the year-on-year change and prevent treating the full acceleration as organic.

Goods-price breadth and increases in trimmed-mean, median and other underlying measures support a genuine positive inflation impulse. The wage-sensitive services interpretation is less decisive because public services and accommodation contribute heavily. Ongoing energy subsidies produce an opposite distortion and future upside risk.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

B1. Headline acceleration and administrative arithmetic

Focus — The headline surprise is large, but the arithmetic requires separating base effects from current inflation momentum.
Tokyo CPI excluding fresh food rose from 1.8% to 2.7% y/y, above the Reuters consensus of 2.4%. Overall CPI accelerated from 1.9% to 2.7%. The measure excluding fresh food and energy increased from 2.0% to 3.0%, its strongest reading since August 2025.

The monthly seasonally adjusted measures also strengthened:
- Overall CPI: +0.6% m/m.
- Ex fresh food: +0.9% m/m.
- Ex fresh food and energy: +0.7% m/m.

The monthly movement establishes that price pressure was not purely an annual-base phenomenon. Nevertheless, the official decomposition reveals that the year-on-year acceleration is unusually sensitive to administrative measures. Water charges and nursery-school fees together contributed approximately 0.53 percentage points to the 0.8-point increase in overall inflation. Water charges rose 65.6% y/y following the end of a temporary basic-charge waiver. Their change in contribution added approximately 0.24 point to annual inflation.

Nursery-school fees remained down 100% y/y, but the negative contribution became much smaller as the initial comparison effect of the subsidy reform rolled through the annual calculation. This added approximately 0.29 point to the change in overall inflation. The same mechanisms materially affected the ex-fresh-food measure. Shinke estimates that water and nursery effects contributed approximately 0.55 point to its 0.9-point acceleration. The important distinction is therefore a large measured inflation acceleration versus a smaller, but still positive, change in underlying inflation.

B2. Goods prices, external costs and services decomposition

Focus — Goods inflation provides the cleaner evidence of renewed price pressure.
Goods inflation increased from 2.5% to 3.1% y/y, while goods excluding fresh food accelerated from 2.3% to 3.4%. Food excluding fresh food remained elevated at 3.6%, despite a substantial fall in rice prices.

The composition is revealing:
- Rice prices fell 19.8% y/y, following a 14.2% decline in August.
- Household consumables increased 10.6%, versus 9.4%.
- Household durables accelerated from 2.2% to 5.9%.
- Recreational durables increased from 3.4% to 7.0%.
- Furniture and household utensils rose 5.5%, versus 3.7%.
- Overseas package tours increased 23.5%, versus 9.7%.

Food price pressure is therefore not simply a continuation of the earlier rice shock. The rice disinflation is offset by persistent increases across other food categories and household necessities. The acceleration in durables is particularly relevant. Semiconductor and memory prices, imported input costs and the exchange rate are plausible transmission channels, supported by institutional commentary. This suggests that external cost shocks are reaching consumer goods prices more broadly. However, the goods impulse is not equivalent to a self-sustaining domestic wage-price cycle. It can remain sensitive to oil prices, foreign producer prices and currency movements.

Focus — Services inflation accelerated, but its underlying signal is weaker than the headline implies.
Total services inflation rose from 1.4% to 2.3% y/y. General services increased from 2.3% to 2.7%, while public services moved from -2.6% to +0.6%.

The official contribution data show:
- Total services: +0.50 point change in contribution.
- Public services: +0.32 point.
- General services: +0.18 point.

This is a central composition distinction. A substantial proportion of the apparent services acceleration comes from administratively determined prices rather than newly observed private-sector wage transmission.

Within market services:
- Eating out inflation moderated from 5.0% to 4.7%.
- Private rents increased from 2.0% to 2.2%.
- Imputed rent rose from 1.9% to 2.0%.
- Accommodation inflation increased from -1.4% to +4.6%.

Accommodation prices were influenced by holiday timing and tourism conditions. The increase in private and imputed rents is more persistent in character, but relatively modest. The services data therefore contain some evidence of broader inflation, particularly rents and general services, without yet proving a decisive acceleration in the wage-sensitive services trend. This helps reconcile the Reuters interpretation with Shinke's caution.

B3. Underlying distributional measures and measurement caveats

Focus — Underlying inflation measures strengthen the case that the rise is not entirely technical.
Hoshino's Tokyo replication of BOJ-style indicators shows:

Indicator                                   August   September
Ex fresh food, special factors              2.6%      3.1%
Ex fresh food, energy and special factors    2.5%      3.0%
Ex food, energy and special factors          2.1%      2.8%
Trimmed mean                                 2.0%      2.3%
Weighted median                              1.8%      2.0%
Mode                                         1.4%      1.6%
Low-volatility CPI                           1.9%      2.0%

Every measure accelerated. The trimmed mean and weighted median are particularly useful because they reduce the influence of extreme price observations. Their increases indicate that the distribution of inflation is shifting, not merely that a few administered prices have moved. However, the rise in the median is much smaller than the rise in conventional core CPI. The data therefore support a genuine broadening in inflation pressure, but not a uniform 0.9–1.0 percentage-point underlying acceleration.

There is also a methodological caveat: the scope of Hoshino's special-factor adjustments is not fully reconciled with Shinke's water and nursery-fee decomposition. Their estimates should not be treated as directly interchangeable.

B4. Energy subsidies and regime persistence

Focus — Energy subsidies are suppressing current inflation even as imported energy risks rise.
Measured energy prices remained negative at -1.9% y/y, versus -2.0% in August. The official calculation indicates that energy and gasoline policy measures continued to subtract materially from measured overall inflation. Electricity and city-gas support is particularly relevant because the current subsidy period ends before the November reading.

This creates an important near-term asymmetry:
- The September increase is exaggerated by the reversal of certain water and childcare comparison effects.
- Current energy inflation is still being suppressed by government measures.
- The later withdrawal of those measures and lagged fuel-cost pass-through may increase measured inflation again.

Consequently, discounting September's administrative distortions does not automatically support a disinflationary forecast for the next several months. The future inflation path is unusually exposed to subsidy timing, oil prices and the yen.

Focus — Level versus momentum: September interrupts the earlier disinflation trajectory.
Tokyo CPI excluding fresh food had fallen from 2.0% in January to 1.2% in May, before recovering gradually to 1.8% in August. September's 2.7% reading represents a substantial departure from that path. Importantly, core-core inflation increased from 2.0% to 3.0%, suggesting that the change is not simply a reversal in energy-price movements. Nevertheless, a single month containing large administered-price distortions cannot establish the persistence of the acceleration. The appropriate interpretation is an upside inflation break whose persistence still requires confirmation.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: reversal of the earlier consumer-price disinflation, with underlying inflation pressure strengthening but the magnitude of the reversal distorted by policy-related price effects.

Context from prior regional live file:
The earlier Tokyo inflation sequence had shown consumer-price weakness despite resilient producer-price pressure, with the June recovery in underlying measures suggesting renewed pass-through. July and August extended the gradual normalization in core inflation without a decisive upside break. September sharply strengthens that trajectory, although administered-price effects prevent treating the entire acceleration as persistent inflation.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information:
This is the most important marginally hawkish Japanese release in the mini-batch. It increases the BOJ's inflation constraint without conclusively advancing the next hike to October.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate:
The release directly challenges the interpretation that Japan's inflation problem is easing sufficiently to allow a prolonged BOJ pause.

The acceleration in adjusted underlying measures, rising goods prices and possible future energy pass-through support continued normalization. However, the timing decision is not settled by the headline.

Policy mapping:
- October: an upside inflation surprise raises the probability that the BOJ considers an earlier move, but water and childcare distortions, uncertain services momentum and the short interval since the September hike argue against treating October as automatic.
- December: remains a credible tightening window if national CPI, wages and household demand confirm that inflation is broadening.
- Beyond December: sustained high inflation would increase pressure to normalize further, but the cumulative effect of higher rates on demand and financing conditions must be assessed alongside price data.

The Tankan and Tokyo CPI should be read as two different signals. The Tankan showed that firms continue to expect inflation around the mid-2% range, without a fresh upward shift in expectations. Tokyo CPI shows that realized prices have accelerated more sharply than those expectations.

That combination strengthens the case for monitoring inflation persistence, but it does not establish that the corporate inflation-expectations regime has abruptly moved higher.

JPY and JGB transmission:
A durable upside inflation surprise should, other things equal, support expectations for BOJ tightening and put upward pressure on Japanese front-end yields.

JPY would benefit if Japanese rates repriced higher relative to external rates. But the immediate market implications are conditional on whether investors view the CPI increase as persistent or primarily administrative.

The scout reports slight yen appreciation during the combined 08:30 JST release window, around USD/JPY 157.88. That move cannot be cleanly attributed to Tokyo CPI alone, while a desk forecast of higher short-dated JGB yields is not evidence of an observed yield increase.

The later indication of approximately 26% October hike probability on 7 October is evidence that a back-to-back hike had not become the dominant market scenario, but is not a direct measurement of the 2 October reaction.

What to verify next:
- National September CPI on 23 October: confirm whether goods and underlying inflation pressures extend beyond Tokyo.
- BOJ Outlook Report and meeting on 29–30 October: determine whether inflation forecasts are revised in response to energy costs, exchange-rate developments and underlying price momentum.
- October Tokyo CPI on 30 October: check whether the jump persists once the water and nursery distortions are less influential.
- November CPI: assess subsidy withdrawal and subsequent energy pass-through.

Macro configuration: headline inflation has broken higher, goods price pressure is broadening, services inflation is not yet conclusively wage-led, and fiscal-price interventions remain a major source of measurement noise.

The CPI strengthens the underlying BOJ tightening case but leaves October versus December a conditional timing decision.

JAPAN — Labour Force Survey / Unemployment Rate (Aug)
Release Date: 2 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 01:30
Importance: **

PM FLASH: August unemployment rose to 2.5% from 2.4%, above the unchanged 2.4% poll; expanding labour-force participation and voluntary transitions explain much of the move.

PM READ-THROUGH: The labour market remains tight rather than turning decisively weak, but falling employee volumes and softness in consumer-facing sectors warrant monitoring of hiring quality.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Professional insight: A modest unemployment increase can reflect better labour supply and job matching, not simply layoffs. Key takeaways: Voluntary job switching dominated the change, yet involuntary unemployment also increased. Year-on-year regular employment remains firm while selected domestic sectors weaken; follow participation, regular jobs and involuntary separations.

1. Reuters Data

Unemployment Rate: 2,5% | Prior: 2,4% | Poll: 2,4% | Surprise: 0,10%

RIC;Date;Time;Rel;Indicator;Period;Actual;Prior;Revised;Poll;Surprise
JPUNR=ECI;2 oct;01:30;High;Unemployment Rate;Aug;2,5%;2,4%;;2,4%;0,10%

2. Professional Market Commentary

Japan's unemployment rate edged higher in August, but the institutional and official commentary did not interpret the increase as evidence of a material labour-market deterioration. Reuters emphasized that the increase was primarily associated with voluntary job departures, as workers continued seeking better pay and employment conditions. The Ministry of Internal Affairs and Communications described the labour market as broadly healthy, despite the unemployment-rate increase. The rise in voluntary unemployment was treated as more consistent with job switching than with a widespread increase in layoffs.

The investingLive desk similarly considered the unemployment increase insufficient to alter the BOJ debate materially, especially alongside the stronger Tokyo inflation release. The main professional interpretation is therefore a modest deterioration in the headline rate without a confirmed weakening in underlying labour-market conditions. No Tier 1 institutional debate detected beyond the official explanation and Reuters' release-specific reporting.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

The 0.1pp unemployment increase marginally weakens the uninterrupted labour-tightening narrative, but a 2.5% rate remains low and much of the increase reflects labour-force entry and voluntary job search. The rise is not evidence by itself of a sustained cyclical employment contraction.

A weaker count of employees and deteriorating wholesale/retail and hospitality employment nevertheless aligns with consumer-facing Tankan and vacancy weakness. The distinction between total employment, employees and participation is essential to interpreting the BOJ wage-pressure constraint.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

B1. Unemployment versus expanding labour supply and separations

Focus — The unemployment increase is partly a labour-supply phenomenon.
The seasonally adjusted unemployment rate rose from 2.4% to 2.5%, above consensus.

Underlying movements:
- Labour force: +110,000 m/m to 70.09 million.
- Employment: +30,000 to 68.34 million.
- Unemployment: +100,000 to 1.77 million.
- Persons outside the labour force: -70,000.

The increase in unemployment occurred alongside an expansion in the labour force rather than a decline in employment. This is analytically different from unemployment rising because firms are reducing aggregate employment. It is consistent with additional people entering or re-entering the labour market and with employment transitions taking longer. However, the increase in unemployment relative to employment growth suggests labour absorption was not strong enough to prevent the rate rising.

Focus — Voluntary departures dominate the increase, but involuntary unemployment also rose.
Seasonally adjusted unemployment by reason:
- Voluntary leavers: +60,000 to 810,000.
- Involuntary unemployed: +20,000 to 400,000.
- New job seekers: +10,000 to 460,000.

The predominance of voluntary exits supports a relatively benign interpretation. Employees seeking better compensation or conditions may leave existing positions before securing new ones. This can temporarily raise unemployment even when labour demand remains healthy.

But two qualifications matter. First, voluntary exits are not always a direct indication of stronger bargaining power: they may also reflect dissatisfaction with working conditions or wages insufficient to offset inflation. Second, involuntary unemployment also increased, even if by much less. The headline should therefore not be dismissed as entirely harmless.

B2. Employment counts, worker categories and domestic-sector weakness

Focus — Employment quantities reveal a mixed underlying picture.
Although total seasonally adjusted employment increased by 30,000, employees fell by 150,000 on the month.

Within that:
- Regular employees: unchanged.
- Non-regular employees: -100,000.

The decline in employees introduces a weaker demand-side reading than the headline employment increase suggests. However, the separately seasonally adjusted categories do not sum mechanically to aggregate totals, and individual monthly movements can be volatile.

The original year-on-year series is more reassuring:
- Total employment: +140,000.
- Employees: +390,000.
- Regular employees: +240,000.
- Non-regular employees: +150,000.
- Employment rate: 62.5%, up 0.2 percentage points.
- Employment rate ages 15–64: 80.3%, up 0.3 percentage points.

Regular employment growth remains stronger than the aggregate monthly weakness would suggest. The labour-market level is therefore still tight.

Focus — Sectoral employment weakness aligns with the Tankan's domestic-demand warning.
Annual industry movements include:
- Medical and welfare: +230,000.
- Transport and postal services: +80,000.
- Manufacturing: -210,000.
- Wholesale and retail: -100,000.
- Accommodation and food services: -50,000.

Manufacturing employment weakness contrasts with the improving manufacturing sentiment reported in Tankan. That is not necessarily contradictory: profitability, productivity, automation and external orders can improve before employment expands, particularly in capital-intensive industries. More important is the overlap between Tankan weakness and labour-market softness in retail, accommodation and food services. These industries are more closely exposed to household spending. The employment data therefore provide partial confirmation that consumer-facing sectors face more difficult conditions, although they do not yet demonstrate an economy-wide employment contraction.

B3. Historical tightness and monitoring threshold

Focus — The monthly rise remains small relative to the recent unemployment range.
The unemployment rate has fluctuated between 2.4% and 2.7% throughout 2026. August's 2.5% therefore remains within the established range rather than representing a new labour-market regime. Moreover, the original number of unemployed persons declined year on year for the first time in thirteen months. This combination is consistent with an economy where labour supply continues increasing and unemployment remains structurally low, but sector-specific hiring is becoming less uniform.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: stabilization of a tight labour market, with a minor deterioration in monthly composition but no confirmed cyclical reversal.

Context from prior regional live file:
Earlier Japanese labour releases showed unemployment remaining low while employment participation and worker availability continued to expand, sustaining the structural labour-shortage narrative. August's increase from 2.4% to 2.5% is modest relative to that history and is mainly associated with greater labour-force participation and voluntary job changes. Tight labour conditions persist, although weakening employment in manufacturing and consumer-facing industries makes the distribution less reassuring.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information:
The release is slightly dovish in isolation, but its composition does not materially weaken the BOJ normalization case.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate:
The increase in unemployment reduces the appearance of uninterrupted labour-market tightening. However, the level remains low, labour supply is expanding and the unemployment increase is largely driven by voluntary job transitions.

The central BOJ question is whether wage pressure remains durable despite slower hiring in particular sectors.

Policy implications:
- The print alone does not justify delaying further hikes.
- It offers a modest caution against accelerating tightening solely because inflation has risen.
- Continued employee declines, especially in regular jobs, would become more important if they persisted across several releases.

For JPY and Japanese front-end rates, the release is secondary to the simultaneous upside Tokyo CPI surprise.

The relevant verification tests are subsequent employment volumes, real wages, labour-force participation and evidence of sustained involuntary job losses.

What to verify next:
Track subsequent new-vacancy flows, effective openings, regular employment and wages.

Macro configuration: low unemployment, expanding labour supply, modest employment mismatches and emerging weakness in selected domestic industries.

JAPAN — Employment Referrals / Jobs-to-Applicants Ratio (Aug)
Release Date: 2 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 01:30
Importance: **

PM FLASH: August jobs-to-applicants ratio held at 1.18, matching both prior and poll; effective openings and applicants grew 0.7%, but new job vacancies weakened.

PM READ-THROUGH: Stable aggregate vacancies conceal deterioration in forward recruitment, notably retail and hospitality, against comparatively resilient manufacturing and technology-related hiring demand.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Professional insight: The stock of advertised jobs can remain stable after employers begin reducing the flow of new postings. Key takeaways: New openings ratio dropped to 2.06, while consumer-facing openings fell at double-digit annual rates. Structural scarcity and shifting recruiting channels could also explain part of the decline; confirm through employee flows and wages.

1. Reuters Data

Jobs/Applicants Ratio: 1,18 | Prior: 1,18 | Poll: 1,18 | Surprise: 0,000

RIC;Date;Time;Rel;Indicator;Period;Actual;Prior;Revised;Poll;Surprise
JPJOBA=ECI;2 oct;01:30;High;Jobs/Applicants Ratio;Aug;1,18;1,18;;1,18;0,000

2. Professional Market Commentary

The August jobs-to-applicants ratio was unchanged and matched consensus, reinforcing a broadly stable aggregate labour-demand picture. MHLW reported that both effective job openings and effective applicants increased by 0.7% m/m, leaving the ratio at 1.18. Reuters also highlighted the growing divergence between strong demand in semiconductor-related manufacturing and softer hiring conditions in wholesale, retail and other domestic consumer-facing businesses. The underlying labour-demand signal was weaker than the headline suggests: the new-openings ratio declined and the number of newly advertised jobs fell from a year earlier.

This is consistent with the broader professional interpretation of a still-tight Japanese labour market in which the momentum of new hiring is becoming less uniform. No Tier 1 institutional debate detected. The release-specific professional coverage did not establish a substantive alternative view to the official data.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

The effective jobs-to-applicants ratio stayed at 1.18 as both effective openings and applicants increased. This level continues to indicate tightness within the public employment referral system, but it is not a complete economy-wide vacancy measure.

The stronger marginal signal is weaker new hiring creation, with the new-openings ratio declining and household-service industries showing marked cuts in openings. Manufacturing recruitment provides an offset. Whether lower postings reflect demand weakness, persistent hiring difficulty or migration to other channels remains unresolved.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

B1. Effective ratio versus new-opening flows

Focus — The unchanged headline masks symmetrical growth in openings and applicants.
The effective jobs-to-applicants ratio remained at 1.18, matching the Reuters poll. Both effective vacancies and effective applicants increased by 0.7% m/m. The unchanged ratio is therefore not the result of simultaneous declines in labour demand and job seeking. It reflects a parallel expansion in both sides of the employment-referral market. A reading above 1 still indicates more effective vacancies than applicants in the MHLW system. However, the ratio is not a direct economy-wide vacancy-to-unemployment measure. Its coverage reflects public employment referral activity and should not be equated with all hiring channels.

Focus — New job openings provide a weaker forward signal.
The new job openings ratio declined from 2.10 to 2.06. New openings fell 3.4% y/y. This distinction between effective vacancies and new vacancies is important. Effective openings are a stock that includes vacancies remaining available from earlier periods. New openings are closer to a flow measure of employers' current recruitment decisions. Consequently, the stability of the effective ratio may coexist with weakening marginal hiring intentions. A sustained decline in new vacancies would eventually be expected to weigh on the effective ratio unless applicant flows also declined.

For now, the two indicators signal stabilization in the aggregate labour market but weaker forward hiring momentum.

B2. Sector hiring dispersion and alternative explanations

Focus — The industry distribution reinforces a widening domestic-demand divergence.
New job openings by sector:
- Manufacturing: +3.9% y/y.
- Information and communications: +3.3%.
- Other services: +0.9%.
- Accommodation and food services: -11.1%.
- Wholesale and retail: -10.2%.
- Living-related services and amusement: -5.7%.

This is the release's principal macro contribution. The manufacturing improvement is consistent with Tankan evidence of stronger sentiment in machinery, electrical equipment and AI-linked activities. By contrast, the sharp declines in hospitality and retail vacancies directly overlap with the sectors reporting weaker confidence in Tankan. The simultaneous Labour Force Survey also showed annual employment declines in retail and accommodation. This three-way consistency increases confidence that hiring conditions in consumer-facing activities are weaker than aggregate labour indicators imply.

Focus — Falling vacancy creation does not uniquely identify weaker final demand.
Several mechanisms could explain declining new job offers:
- Employers may be reducing recruitment because margins and sales are weakening.
- Persistent labour scarcity could cause firms to abandon vacancies they cannot fill.
- Firms may use automation or less labour-intensive business processes.
- Hiring may migrate toward channels outside the MHLW referral system.
- Rising labour costs may lead employers to revise staffing plans.

The available data cannot determine their relative importance. Reuters' coverage adds a relevant qualitative consideration: some retail businesses are reviewing recruitment plans amid weak profitability, with self-checkout systems and flexible spot work increasingly used. That provides a plausible mechanism linking persistent labour shortages, rising costs and weaker new hiring. The observed vacancy decline should therefore be treated as a potential demand warning rather than conclusive evidence of an employment downturn.

B3. Regular employment and medium-term wage-pressure implications

Focus — The regular-employee ratio indicates a tighter margin for stable employment matches.
The effective ratio for regular employees remained at 1.00. This is lower than the overall effective ratio of 1.18. It suggests that the apparent excess of vacancies is less pronounced when focusing on regular employment. The MHLW definition is not a strict permanent-job supply-demand balance, since the applicant denominator also includes some workers seeking dispatch or contract work. Nevertheless, the distinction reinforces the need to avoid treating all job vacancies as equivalent in employment quality or wage bargaining power.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: stabilization of aggregate vacancy pressure, with deterioration in forward hiring demand concentrated in consumer-facing services.

Context from prior regional live file:
Earlier employment-referral releases had already shown the jobs-to-applicants ratio hovering around 1.17–1.18, consistent with persistent labour scarcity but little additional tightening. August preserves that stable aggregate level while the decline in new openings and marked weakness in consumer-sector recruitment introduce a less favorable forward signal. This confirms labour-market tightness in level terms without confirming an acceleration in labour demand.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information:
The headline does not materially alter the BOJ reaction function. An unchanged ratio at 1.18 indicates that labour-market conditions remain relatively tight.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate:
However, weaker vacancy creation argues against assuming that all sectors can continue absorbing higher labour costs without affecting hiring decisions.

The policy implications are therefore asymmetric. The level of labour-market tightness supports continued wage pressure, but the deterioration in marginal hiring demand introduces caution about the durability of domestic demand.

For BOJ policy:
- No standalone reason to accelerate or reverse the tightening path.
- Consumer-sector hiring weakness reinforces the need to monitor the cumulative impact of inflation and higher rates.
- Additional weakness in new vacancies, particularly if accompanied by rising involuntary unemployment, would provide a more consequential dovish signal.

For JPY and rates:
The release is unlikely to dominate pricing relative to the simultaneous CPI surprise.

Its importance lies in the medium-term policy constraint: Japan can have persistent inflation and labour scarcity while businesses gradually reduce new hiring.

The next test is whether declining new vacancies translate into lower effective job openings, weaker regular employment or reduced wage growth.

What to verify next:
Track subsequent new-vacancy flows, effective openings, regular employment and wages.

Macro configuration: stable vacancy pressure, weakening new hiring flows, stronger manufacturing labour demand and softer consumer-facing recruitment.

JAPAN — Monetary Base (Sep)
Release Date: 2 Oct 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: *

PM FLASH: September monetary base contracted 15.21% y/y versus -15.73% in August; average outstanding base money fell JPY 10.2tn, mostly through central-bank current-account balances.

PM READ-THROUGH: Quantitative normalization continues despite modestly slower annual and seasonally adjusted monthly contraction; bank reserves are shrinking without established broad-money or lending-system stress.

PM DEBATE & KEY TAKEAWAYS: Professional insight: A less-negative annual rate does not imply renewed liquidity creation when outstanding balances continue falling. Key takeaways: Reserve withdrawals dominate, not cash demand; base effects and operational timing matter, and central-bank base money must be distinguished from private deposits and credit. Funding spreads, not the headline contraction alone, reveal transmission risk.

1. Reuters Data

Monetary Base YY: -15,21% | Prior: -15,73%

RIC;Date;Time;Rel;Indicator;Period;Actual;Prior;Revised;Poll;Surprise
JPMBSE=ECI;2 oct;01:50;High;Monetary Base YY;Sep;-15,21%;-15,73%;;;

2. Professional Market Commentary

Japan's monetary base continued contracting sharply in September, consistent with the BOJ's ongoing balance-sheet normalization. The official report showed the annual decline narrowing modestly from August, while the monetary base continued falling in level terms. The largest contribution to the contraction came from current-account balances held by financial institutions at the BOJ rather than physical cash in circulation. There was no substantive release-specific institutional or market debate around the September result.

Trading Economics' available monetary-base coverage focused on August's particularly sharp contraction. A third-party consensus indicated an expected September decline of approximately 16.3%, although this is not part of the Reuters mechanical feed. The observed contraction was therefore slightly less severe than that external estimate, but remained exceptionally large. The broader professional interpretation of monetary-base contraction in Japan is associated with the reversal of earlier asset-purchase stimulus and reduced reserve balances as monetary policy normalizes. However, the release does not establish the exact operational cause of the September deceleration.

No Tier 1 institutional debate detected. No release-specific yen, JGB or equity reaction was confirmed.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

A. Economist Assessment

September confirmed substantial BOJ balance-sheet withdrawal with a small slowing in the pace of decline. The monetary base fell 15.21% y/y and its level continued shrinking; banking-system current accounts and reserves accounted for most of the contraction.

The monthly pace moderated but the year-on-year change is affected by comparison bases. Base money is not equivalent to M2 or bank lending, and the available corporate survey does not indicate broad credit access deterioration. This is a financial-condition background signal, not a mechanical signal about the next BOJ policy-rate decision.

B. Detailed Evidence & Economic Mechanisms

B1. Annual trend and current level of monetary contraction

Focus — The annual contraction slowed marginally but remained historically large.
The monetary base declined 15.21% y/y according to the Reuters feed, compared with 15.73% in August. The BOJ's rounded figures are -15.2% and -15.7%, respectively. This is the first narrowing in the annual rate of contraction since April.

However, the full-year trajectory remains strongly negative:
- January: -9.5%.
- February: -10.6%.
- March: -11.6%.
- April: -11.3%.
- May: -12.2%.
- June: -13.7%.
- July: -13.8%.
- August: -15.7%.
- September: -15.2%.

The narrowing in September is small compared with the cumulative deterioration since the beginning of 2026. The third-quarter average decline was 14.9%, versus 12.4% in Q2 and 10.6% in Q1. Therefore, in quarterly terms, the contraction in monetary-base liquidity continued intensifying.

Focus — The level of monetary-base liquidity continued falling.
Average outstanding monetary base:
- August: JPY 543.0 trillion.
- September: JPY 532.8 trillion.

The level declined by approximately JPY 10.2 trillion during the month.

Seasonally adjusted monetary base:
- August: JPY 536.0 trillion.
- September: JPY 531.3 trillion.

The seasonally adjusted annualized monthly rate of change improved from -28.8% to -10.2%. These data establish two simultaneous facts. First, the monetary base continued shrinking. Second, the short-term pace of contraction moderated. The moderation could reflect slower reserve withdrawal, operational timing or statistical effects. The release alone does not identify which mechanism dominates.

B2. Reserve composition, comparison effects and broad-money distinction

Focus — Reserve balances are the principal source of contraction.
The monetary base consists of cash in circulation and financial institutions' current-account balances at the BOJ.

September annual changes:
- Monetary base: -15.2%.
- Current-account balances: -18.3%.
- Reserve balances: -17.3%.
- Banknotes: -2.0%.
- Coins: -1.2%.

Average current-account balances declined from JPY 423.4 trillion to JPY 413.7 trillion. Reserve balances stood at approximately JPY 382.9 trillion. The distribution demonstrates that monetary-base contraction is predominantly a banking-reserves phenomenon. The much smaller decline in banknotes and coins suggests that ordinary demand for physical cash has not experienced a comparable contraction. The principal policy interpretation is therefore reduced central-bank-provided liquidity rather than an abrupt collapse in currency use.

Focus — Annual base effects explain part of the improvement in the growth rate.
September 2025 monetary-base growth was already more negative than August 2025. That lowers the comparison base for the September 2026 annual calculation. Consequently, the year-on-year contraction can become less negative even while the level of outstanding base money continues falling. This is precisely why the annual rate should not be interpreted in isolation. The seasonally adjusted monthly pace did moderate, providing some evidence of slower current-period contraction. But one month's change is insufficient to establish a durable operational slowdown in reserve run-off.

Focus — Monetary-base contraction is not equivalent to broad-money contraction.
The monetary base measures BOJ liabilities, including bank reserves and currency in circulation. Broad monetary aggregates such as M2 include deposits created and held within the private banking system. The relationship between the two is not mechanically proportional. Reduced excess reserves can coexist with continuing deposit growth and private credit creation. The July leading-index report, for example, recorded positive M2 annual growth of 2.1%. The distinction is essential for Japan's monetary regime. A 15% contraction in the monetary base should not be interpreted as equivalent to a 15% reduction in private-sector liquidity or credit availability.

The Tankan's corporate finance indicators provide useful corroboration: firms reported higher borrowing costs, but broadly unchanged lending attitudes and financial positions. This is consistent with tightening in the price of credit before a generalized contraction in credit availability.

B3. Short-rate reaction function and funding-transmission risks

Focus — The monetary-base release should be separated from the BOJ's short-rate reaction function.
The BOJ's overnight policy rate and monetary-base quantities influence financial conditions through different channels. A reduction in reserves may change market liquidity, collateral conditions and the composition of financial assets. But the monthly monetary-base rate alone does not determine future short-term policy decisions. The BOJ can continue balance-sheet normalization while holding the policy rate steady for a meeting. Equally, a temporary moderation in monetary-base contraction does not prove that the BOJ has become less committed to eventual rate normalization.

The key market issue is whether reserve reduction begins affecting money-market functioning, bank funding conditions or credit availability. The September report does not demonstrate such a deterioration.

Focus — Quantitative tightening is increasingly relevant as a background constraint, not an immediate FX signal.
The monetary base has been contracting sharply through 2026. Meanwhile, Tankan evidence suggests rising loan rates but still accommodative credit availability. This configuration indicates tightening financial conditions without a clear credit-system stress event. However, continued reserve reduction could become more consequential if it eventually affects funding spreads, market liquidity or banks' willingness to lend. Those effects require direct evidence from money-market and banking indicators. They cannot be inferred solely from the annual monetary-base percentage change.

C. State Transition & Historical Context

State transition: continuation of monetary normalization, with a tentative stabilization in the pace of reserve contraction but no reversal of the balance-sheet trend.

Context from prior regional live file:
The Japanese monetary-base sequence had already shifted into sustained contraction as the BOJ reduced the reserve-intensive legacy of earlier stimulus, while broad money and private liquidity remained more resilient. September continues that quantitative-tightening regime despite a slightly less negative annual rate and slower monthly contraction. The distinction between shrinking reserves and a still-functioning private credit system remains intact.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Main incremental information:
The monetary-base release confirms balance-sheet normalization but does not materially change the BOJ's immediate reaction function.

Macro mechanism / strategic debate:
The decline in reserve balances is consistent with a central bank gradually removing extraordinary accommodation. However, the rate of monetary-base contraction is not a reliable standalone indicator of the timing of the next policy-rate hike.

Policy mapping:
- October: the release has negligible incremental relevance to the next-rate decision.
- December: continued balance-sheet contraction is compatible with additional normalization if inflation and growth conditions warrant.
- Medium term: reserve reduction could become more relevant if money-market liquidity or banking-system funding conditions begin deteriorating.

JPY and rates:
The direct FX implication is limited.

A shrinking monetary base may be consistent with tighter monetary conditions, but JPY pricing depends more immediately on expected short-rate differentials, foreign yields, risk appetite and external balances.

Likewise, lower reserve quantities do not necessarily imply higher JGB yields in a simple proportional relationship.

The more relevant verification is whether BOJ liquidity withdrawal begins affecting short-term funding spreads or financial intermediation.

The release should not be used to infer a specific policy repricing or currency reaction without market evidence.

What to verify next:
- October monetary-base levels and seasonally adjusted momentum.
- BOJ reserve balances and asset-purchase operations.
- M2, bank lending and deposit growth.
- Money-market spreads and short-term funding conditions.
- Tankan lending-attitude and financial-position indicators.

Macro configuration: continued quantitative normalization, persistent reduction in bank reserves, slower monthly run-off and no verified broad liquidity stress.

The release is a background confirmation of monetary tightening, not a reason to change October-versus-December policy timing.

D. CENTRAL BANK TAPE

No validated central-bank tape supplied for this window.

E. SIGNAL TENSION CHECK

Main contradiction:
Inflation persistence, tight labor supply and positive corporate orders/profits justify continued BOJ normalization, but weak production, consumer spending and housing imply incomplete domestic-demand transmission. The key inflation impulse may be partly administrative/imported rather than an accelerating domestic wage-price cycle.

Why it matters:
The BOJ could tighten into a real-demand slowdown if it mistakes relative-price and administered-price effects for durable domestically generated inflation, or delay excessively if broadening persists.

Implication:
Further normalization remains credible, but the immediate timing bar is higher than the direction-of-travel bar; verify national underlying inflation and production/consumption follow-through before treating October as the base case.

F. 5 KEY RELEASES TO DIG INTO

**** Tokyo CPI / CPI Ex Fresh Food (2 Oct)
Why it matters: Ex-fresh-food CPI 2.7% from 1.8%, +0.3pp above poll; roughly 0.55pp of its 0.9pp annual acceleration was attributed by a named economist to water/nursery effects. Broadening goods and adjusted inflation measures compete with administrative noise and unresolved wage-sensitive services persistence.

**** BOJ Tankan Survey (1 Oct)
Why it matters: Resilient manufacturing earnings, capex +11.3%, shortages and pricing power versus falling consumer-services DIs, uniformly lower outlooks and loan-cost DI 61 to 68. Crucial for the tightening-path-versus-demand constraint; most respondents replied before September's BOJ hike.

*** Indices of Industrial Production, Preliminary (30 Sep)
Why it matters: August -1.7% m/m versus +1.7% poll, shipments -2.5%, twelve of fifteen industries contracting. Automotive/weather/refinery disruption is material, but cannot fully explain breadth; forecast +3.2% September rebound requires hard verification.

*** Current Survey of Commerce / Retail Sales and Large-Scale Retail Sales (30 Sep)
Why it matters: Retail +2.7% y/y versus +3.3% poll, but -1.2% m/m SA; motor-vehicle +17.7% y/y masks weaker categories and large-store sales +1% versus +2%. Most direct test of household-demand sustainability.

*** Services Producer Price Index, Preliminary (28 Sep)
Why it matters: Headline +3.7% y/y from 3.6%, but ex-international transportation flat at 3.2%; freight + leasing explain much of marginal rise. Distinguish externally concentrated business costs from a self-sustaining wage-sensitive services-price acceleration.

G. RED TEAM QUESTIONS

1. How much of Tokyo September CPI's 0.9pp ex-fresh-food acceleration survives a consistent removal of water, nursery fees, energy subsidies and holiday effects, and does the trimmed-mean / median improvement persist?

2. Does flat 3.2% SPPI ex-international transportation and 2.8% labor-intensive SPPI undermine the argument for accelerating wage-led services inflation, or are individual domestic categories about to broaden?

3. Can realized September industrial output and, critically, shipments meet even METI's +1.6% bias-corrected production projection after August's enormous forecast miss, reconciling strong manufacturing PMI/Tankan with declining volumes?

4. Are August's retail slowdown, consumer-services Tankan DI declines and sector-specific vacancy losses evidence of sustained household-demand weakness despite shortages and wage gains, or merely weather, timing and volatility?

5. Do high July leading-index readings genuinely forecast stronger investment and shipments, or will inventory-ratio normalization and weak machinery orders reverse the forward-indicator gains?

6. At what observable level of money-market spreads, loan availability or corporate financial-position deterioration would persistent BOJ reserve runoff become a policy-relevant credit constraint?

7. What incremental national inflation, wage, spending and post-hike credit evidence would warrant a consecutive October increase, versus supporting patience until December?

H. CURRENCY MARKET-CONSENSUS SCAN

Bottom line

Pair convention: USD/JPY; lower USD/JPY means a stronger yen. The contemporaneous institutional public bias for 27 Sep–3 Oct was moderately constructive JPY into Q4/2027, not uniformly bullish in the near term. September's BOJ hike to 1.25% and perceived improvement in Japanese policy credibility supported medium-term narrowing of US–Japan rate differentials; however, the Fed's hawkish September pivot, rising US Treasury yields and continuing USD carry capped immediate JPY upside. Standard Chartered's 25 Sep market outlook explicitly called for gradual yen strength toward 153 over an unspecified medium-term horizon; UBS's 21 Sep-reported view expected little sustained appreciation and USD/JPY 160 by December.

The window's most useful comparable public targets are MUFG's 1 Oct Q4 USD/JPY 155 and the UBS December 160 forecast; MUFG independently displayed a Bloomberg-sourced Q4 consensus reference of 156. That benchmark is a reported aggregated consensus reference, not a third independent named-bank forecast. MUFG's September Q4 target had been 156, so the 1 Oct move to 155 was an actual one-yen forecast revision, whereas some of the yen's earlier appreciation was simply spot convergence. MUFG's broader BOJ policy-rate destination was revised upward to 2.00% by mid-2027, but MUFG highlighted that tighter forecasts for other G10 central banks limited the relative-spread impact.

Spot had already moved substantially: MUFG's comparable London fix declined from USD/JPY 160.03 on 28 Aug to 157.15 on 30 Sep. A 2 Oct daily historical market close was approximately 157.86 (source-dependent cut), with an alternative contemporaneous market review around 157.79; both sit inside the public Q4 155–160 interval, closer to the Bloomberg-sourced Q4 reference of 156 than to the cautious UBS endpoint of 160. The price had not reached the constructive Q4 155 target. The source-limited forecast distribution is not a robust percentile range for the entire banking community.

A Reuters poll published on 2 Oct documented an aggregate FX-strategist inclination toward medium-term dollar weakness but frequent concern about near-term dollar outperformance amid yields and bond volatility. It corroborates the cross-currency USD backdrop, not a separately surveyed USD/JPY target distribution. Conviction in immediate yen upside therefore remained weaker than the medium-term directional view.

Public-bank Q4 2026 range: USD/JPY 155–160 (MUFG 155; UBS December 160; only two directly comparable house targets). MUFG's Bloomberg-reported aggregated Q4 consensus reference: 156.
Constructive JPY cluster: 155–156 in Q4 (MUFG and its Bloomberg-sourced market reference); Standard Chartered 153 over an unspecified medium-term horizon, not directly Q4-comparable.
More cautious / tactical view: USD/JPY 160 by December (UBS); US yields and Fed policy can keep spot elevated despite BOJ hikes.
Bias at window close: Moderately constructive JPY over the medium term, but tactical upside capped by the residual US rate advantage; USD/JPY around 157.8–157.9 remained within the limited forecast interval.

What changed through the data window

The starting forecast backdrop was already constructive on a gradual BOJ normalization path, but September's yen rise had absorbed part of the anticipated convergence. MUFG's 1 Oct publication explicitly lowered the Q4 USD/JPY target from 156 to 155 and the displayed Bloomberg Q4 consensus reference from 158 in September to 156 in October; this is a change in forecasts, not merely spot moving toward old targets. The revised MUFG BOJ rate path (1.50% Q4 2026, 2.00% by Q2 2027) increased policy-credibility support, but higher US rates restrained relative-yield convergence. During 1 Oct, global bond-market selling and the stronger USD pressured the yen; by 2 Oct softer US payrolls restrained immediate Fed-hike bets and triggered a dollar pullback, but bond yields remained elevated. The public directional bias thus became a little more yen-positive in target space without a clean short-term bullish-JPY breakout. No post-3 Oct developments are used in this judgment.

Main consensus drivers

BOJ normalization / credibility: September's 25bp increase and a higher perceived policy destination support eventual JPY appreciation; precise hike sequencing and credible independent tightening matter more than monthly monetary-base contraction.

US rates / Fed carry: September's hawkish Fed pivot and surging Treasury yields sustained the wide US–Japan differential; the 2 Oct jobs surprise tempered near-term hike pricing but did not mechanically reverse the US term-premium environment.

Energy / external purchasing power: Japan's imported energy and freight shocks undermine terms of trade and real incomes; inflation can justify BOJ tightening without automatically strengthening JPY if domestic demand deteriorates.

Intervention / global risk: Japanese policy rhetoric and past intervention caution against extrapolating USD/JPY upside above 160; global risk and positioning shocks can dominate short-term policy-rate convergence.

Where consensus could be wrong

Consensus too bullish JPY: US inflation and yield pressure remain persistent, while Japan's domestic consumption and realized output disappoint and the BOJ waits longer than expected; USD carry overwhelms normalization-driven support.

Consensus too cautious JPY: National underlying CPI and a resilient wage-price/consumption cycle validate further BOJ hikes while weaker US activity reduces Fed path expectations; narrower front-end rate spreads pull USD/JPY below the constructive target cluster.

Key falsification: sustained confirmation in both the expected BOJ path and US–Japan front-end rate spreads, corroborated by Japan's underlying inflation and actual private-demand recovery, rather than a transitory intervention-led FX move.
