DATA WINDOW: 20 September 2026 to 26 September 2026
A. JPY WEEK — MACRO SYNTHESIS
Japan remains in a two-speed, normalization-compatible regime: industrial/export activity is resilient and corporate pricing/labor signals remain firm, while domestic-demand momentum and household spending quality are materially weaker. The September flash PMI slowed at the composite level to 52.5 from 53.5 and services to 51.6 from 52.5, but manufacturing remained strong at 54.1, export orders stayed exceptionally firm, employment growth accelerated to a seven-month high and selling-price inflation remained just below August’s survey record. Chain-store sales reinforced the domestic-demand qualification: same-store turnover rose only 0.2% y/y, with higher prices supporting nominal sales while customer traffic weakened and clothing declined.
The validated BoJ tape materially shifts the policy overlay hawkishly. September Tokyo core CPI jumped to 2.7% y/y from 1.8%, above the 2.4% consensus, with headline also at 2.7% and ex-food-energy momentum still firm at +0.4% m/m. That print arrived one week after the 7–2 September hike and directly weakens the dissenters’ soft-realized-inflation argument, while validating the majority’s risk-management / overshoot-prevention logic earlier than expected. State transition: the macro regime itself remains manufacturing-led and domestically softer, but the policy regime is re-rated hawkishly — October remains a hold-oriented meeting, while December tightening is re-armed and the cadence question shifts back toward the hawkish side pending national CPI and the October Outlook decomposition.
B. JPY MACRO SIGNAL SCOREBOARD
Growth: ~
Supporting evidence:
Composite and services momentum slowed, domestic new-order growth cooled and chain-store sales showed only marginal nominal stabilization. Against that, manufacturing remained strong at 54.1, export orders stayed exceptionally firm and business expectations improved. The growth signal is therefore mixed: industrial/external resilience versus softer domestic breadth.
Labor: +
Supporting evidence:
Private-sector employment growth accelerated to a seven-month high and backlogs increased more quickly, inconsistent with a broad demand break. The hiring signal is stronger than the composite slowdown implies, although confirmation in wages and broader labor data remains necessary.
Inflation: -
Supporting evidence:
Input-cost inflation eased but remained historically elevated, while aggregate selling-price inflation stayed just below August’s survey record. The central-bank tape then adds a decisive policy-relevant shock: September Tokyo core CPI accelerated to 2.7% y/y from 1.8%, above consensus, with ex-food-energy momentum still positive. Composition is partly affected by subsidy rollback, but the direction clearly reinforces above-target inflation risk.
Financial Conditions: -
Supporting evidence:
The BoJ’s policy rate had just been raised to 1.25%, its highest since 1995, and the monetary base continued to contract in the background. Household demand remains price-sensitive and domestic breadth soft, showing that tighter policy is transmitting unevenly rather than producing a broad break.
Policy Constraint: -
Supporting evidence:
The post-meeting constraint shifts hawkishly. Tokyo CPI at 2.7% materially strengthens the majority’s case for continued normalization and weakens the dissenters’ reliance on soft realized inflation. However, no Board member spoke in-window and the CPI jump is partly mechanical, so the immediate constraint is not “hike now” but “hold October while preserving December and a potentially faster cadence if national inflation and the October Outlook confirm persistence.”
C. FULL RELEASE ARCHIVE
THURSDAY 24 SEP 2026
Japan — S&P Global Manufacturing / Services / Composite PMI Flash (Sep)
Release Date: 24 Sep 2026 | Local Time: 02:30
Importance: ****
1. Reuters Data
S&P Global Mfg PMI Flash SA: 54,1 | Prior: 54,9
S&P Global Comp Op Flash SA: 52,5 | Prior: 53,5
S&P Global SVC PMI Flash SA: 51,6 | Prior: 52,5
2. Professional Market Commentary
The professional tape is slower private-sector growth with a still-firm manufacturing and inflation backdrop, rather than a broad activity break.
S&P Global reported that composite activity slowed to 52.5 from 53.5, the softest expansion since May, as both manufacturing and services lost momentum. The slowdown was concentrated mainly in domestic demand: new business growth cooled in both sectors, while manufacturing export orders remained exceptionally strong and services continued to report weaker overseas demand. Manufacturing remained the stronger side of the economy despite output easing from July-August highs.
The inflation and capacity signals remained materially firmer than the activity headline:
- input-cost inflation eased but remained historically elevated
- aggregate selling-price inflation stayed just below August’s survey record
- supplier delivery delays intensified again
- private-sector employment growth accelerated to a seven-month high
- backlogs increased more quickly
- business expectations improved to their strongest since February
S&P Global’s economic commentary mapped the composite reading to roughly 0.5% q/q GDP growth and argued that persistent price pressure alongside resilient activity kept further BoJ tightening in play. Its explicit suggestion that another hike could come as early as October is a producer-house interpretation rather than an independently corroborated consensus view.
State Street separately classified the 54.1 manufacturing PMI as “trending high” and placed Japan alongside the US and Taiwan at the leading edge of the developed-market manufacturing upswing, linking the strength partly to AI investment.
3. Comment — Economist Layer
This is a deceleration print, but not a clean weakening signal.
The key distinction is between momentum and level. Composite, services and manufacturing all slowed sequentially, but every headline index remained expansionary and manufacturing activity remained unusually strong by Japan’s recent historical standards.
Signal hierarchy:
1. Domestic demand: deterioration at the margin. New-order growth cooled across both sectors and the producer explicitly attributes most of the slowdown to softer domestic demand.
2. Manufacturing: still the principal growth engine. The headline PMI remained at 54.1 and output growth, although slower, remained historically strong. Strong exports continue to provide an external cushion.
3. Export quality: unusually firm but concentrated. Manufacturing export orders retained the exceptionally strong pace reached in August, supported by weak-yen competitiveness and global technology / AI demand; services exports remained weak. The economy therefore remains externally and industrially stronger than its domestic-services impulse. S&P Global describes recent export-order gains as among the largest in the survey’s history.
4. Stockbuilding / supply-chain qualification: manufacturing demand should not be read as pure final-demand strength. Customer buffer-stock accumulation remains part of the order story, while worsening supplier delivery times can also mechanically support the headline manufacturing PMI. This remains an important quality caveat given the Middle East-related supply shock.
5. Inflation: still the main policy constraint. Input-cost momentum has eased from the May-June peak, but selling-price inflation remains close to a survey record. That combination suggests firms are preserving pricing power even as current demand growth moderates.
6. Labour / capacity: stronger than the headline slowdown implies. Employment growth accelerated to the strongest since February and outstanding business rose at the fastest pace in seven months. That is inconsistent with a broad demand collapse and gives more weight to the inflation-persistence signal.
7. Services: the weakest component. Activity remains above 50 but has lost momentum, new business growth slowed and foreign demand remains negative. This is the clearest evidence that the manufacturing boom has not translated into equally strong domestic breadth.
The internal contradiction is therefore sharper than the headline PMI decline suggests: domestic momentum is cooling while export manufacturing, labour demand, capacity pressure and corporate price-setting remain firm.
State transition: continuation — slower growth momentum within the same manufacturing-led, inflation-constrained expansion.
Context from prior regional live file:
June had already shifted Japan away from the May services stall, while warning that manufacturing strength was partly inventory-driven. By July, the archive described a two-speed expansion led by AI-linked manufacturing, exports and near-record corporate pricing while services lagged. September preserves that regime but shows momentum cooling on the domestic side rather than resolving the divergence.
4. HF Take — Strategist Layer
The release preserves a hawkish asymmetry for the BoJ, but does not independently establish the timing of the next hike.
The Bank had decided on 18 September to raise the policy rate, with the guideline subsequently taking effect at around 1.25%. The relevant question is therefore whether subsequent data validate continued normalization rather than whether this PMI itself triggers another move. The BoJ currently states that it will encourage the uncollateralized overnight call rate to remain at around 1.25%.
BoJ interpretation:
- softer composite and services momentum argues against mechanically accelerating the hiking cycle
- near-record selling-price inflation prevents the Bank from reading the activity deceleration as dovish
- stronger employment and rising backlogs reduce concern that tighter policy has already generated a broad private-sector break
- manufacturing export strength gives the economy an external growth cushion
- weak domestic new-order momentum and continued reliance on export / stockbuilding support argue for calibration rather than aggressive tightening
- services pricing and wage-sensitive inflation remain more important than another strong manufacturing headline for judging whether inflation is becoming sustainably domestic
Rates / FX mapping:
- JGB front end: hawkish asymmetry remains if price-setting, wages and services inflation stay firm
- JPY: the domestic policy signal is supportive at the margin, but relative global rates and broader dollar dynamics can dominate
- equities: export / semiconductor / AI exposure remains supported by the industrial composition, while domestic-demand-sensitive sectors receive a weaker signal
- broader 24 September JPY, JGB and equity moves should not be attributed mechanically to this PMI because Tokyo was also absorbing global bond, oil and post-holiday catch-up effects
The producer’s October-hike interpretation is therefore best treated as a live scenario, not the release’s unique conclusion.
Next verification tests:
- whether services and composite finals preserve the flash slowdown
- whether services prices remain close to extreme survey levels
- whether stronger hiring translates into wage and domestic-price persistence
- whether household demand confirms the softer domestic-order signal
- whether manufacturing strength survives without continued buffer-stock accumulation and supply disruption
Macro configuration: manufacturing/export resilience + softer domestic momentum + persistent corporate pricing pressure.
That combination keeps further BoJ normalization live while leaving timing dependent on broader inflation and domestic-demand confirmation.
FRIDAY 25 SEP 2026
Japan — Chain Store Sales (Aug)
Release Date: 25 Sep 2026 | Local Time: 07:00
Importance: **
1. Reuters Data
Chain Store Sales YY: 0,2% | Prior: 0,1%
2. Professional Market Commentary
The professional tape is marginal nominal sales growth supported by higher prices rather than a convincing recovery in household demand.
JCSA reported same-store sales up 0.2% y/y, with food and household goods both up 0.3% while clothing fell 1.0%. The association explicitly attributed the positive overall result partly to higher shelf prices, while adverse weather weighed on seasonal categories.
Kyodo added the more important demand-quality detail: customer numbers were below year-earlier levels because of typhoons and heavy rain, while higher food prices lifted sales values. An association official said elevated prices continued to restrain consumer sentiment.
Broader sector reporting corroborated the same interpretation: August chain-store sales were barely positive because higher food prices lifted average tickets despite falling customer traffic. July real household spending had also remained weak, reinforcing concern that renewed inflation could intensify household economising and purchase deferral.
No release-specific market reaction was identified.
3. Comment — Economist Layer
The 0.2% headline is better described as nominal stabilization than consumption improvement.
Signal hierarchy:
1. Same-store sales remained positive for a second month, but only marginally.
2. The quality of the growth is weak. JCSA directly attributes part of the gain to rising shelf prices, while reported customer traffic fell. Nominal turnover is therefore overstating the underlying quantity signal.
3. Food provides little evidence of real demand acceleration. It accounts for almost three-quarters of total sales yet rose only 0.3% on a same-store basis despite continued food-price inflation. Agricultural, livestock and fisheries categories were firmer, but broader food was weaker and price effects remain important.
4. Discretionary breadth remains poor. Clothing declined 1.0% y/y same-store, including weakness across men's and women's apparel. Weather provides a legitimate qualification, but it does not transform the report into a broad consumer-strength signal.
5. Household goods were only +0.3% same-store despite an +11.4% all-store increase. Similar large gaps elsewhere between all-store and same-store growth mean changes in store coverage materially distort the unadjusted figures; the same-store measure should therefore dominate the macro interpretation.
6. Weather creates genuine noise. Eastern Japan was materially cooler than the record-hot August 2025 comparison, while typhoons and heavy rain disrupted traffic. This particularly affects beverages, seasonal clothing and other weather-sensitive lines.
7. The inflation-demand interaction is the more durable signal. Higher prices are still sufficient to support nominal turnover, but household behaviour is adapting through lower traffic and greater price sensitivity. That is closer to real-income compression than demand-led reflation.
The report therefore does not contradict the flash-PMI message. Both point to a Japanese economy where the corporate/manufacturing side remains considerably stronger than household-sensitive domestic demand.
State transition: stabilization — nominal sales remain marginally positive, but there is no convincing improvement in underlying real demand.
Context from prior regional live file:
Earlier DM history had chain-store growth slowing from 2.7% in January to 1.0% in February before falling 1.7% in March, while May household spending later suggested a tentative consumption floor. August's small positive nominal reading keeps that floor from breaking outright, but price support and weaker traffic leave household demand materially less convincing than the industrial side of the economy.
4. HF Take — Strategist Layer
This is a secondary BoJ input, but directionally it qualifies the hawkish message from the preceding PMI block.
BoJ interpretation:
- marginally positive sales argue against an outright consumer contraction signal
- falling customer traffic prevents the nominal gain from being treated as stronger domestic demand
- price-led turnover confirms that inflation is still transmitting into household expenditure
- weak consumer sentiment and price-sensitive behaviour increase the risk that further inflation erodes real consumption
- weather noise argues against over-reading one month's magnitude
- the release therefore supports policy calibration rather than a rapid tightening inference
The BoJ’s new overnight-rate guideline around 1.25% took effect during this release week. Against that backdrop, this report does not challenge continued normalization, but it gives the Bank little evidence that household demand can comfortably absorb an accelerated hiking path.
Rates / FX mapping:
- JGB front end: modestly offsets the hawkish read from near-record PMI selling-price pressure, but is too weak to drive repricing independently
- JPY: low standalone relevance; relative rates and broader BoJ guidance dominate
- domestic equities: reinforces caution toward consumption-sensitive exposures facing price fatigue and weaker traffic
- no release-specific market move should be inferred
Next verification tests:
- whether household spending volumes remain weak
- whether retail-sales growth is also increasingly price-led
- whether real wages can offset renewed food and energy inflation
- whether services demand follows the softer domestic-order signal in the PMI
- whether consumer traffic stabilizes as weather distortion fades
Macro configuration: nominal spending stabilization + weak traffic / real-demand quality + persistent price pressure.
That mix is consistent with cautious continued BoJ normalization, but not with treating household demand as confirmation for an accelerated tightening cycle.
D. CENTRAL BANK TAPE
Summary — Policy reaction function
The week was communication-light but data-heavy: no BoJ policymaker spoke on record, yet September Tokyo CPI delivered a regime-relevant hawkish shock only days after the 7–2 September hike. Core inflation jumped to 2.7% y/y from 1.8%, above the 2.4% consensus, while ex-food-energy momentum remained firm at +0.4% m/m. This directly weakens the dissenters’ soft-realized-inflation case and strengthens the majority’s risk-management logic. The key caveat is composition: subsidy rollback contributes to the y/y jump, so the national September CPI and October Outlook decomposition are required before concluding that the cadence itself should accelerate.
September Tokyo CPI — 25 Sep 2026
Importance: ****
Key policy signal:
Core CPI rose to 2.7% y/y from 1.8%, above the 2.4% consensus; headline also reached 2.7%. The print confirms above-target inflation earlier than the BoJ’s prior H2 FY26 framing and supports continued normalization.
Representative quote:
No representative policy quote — this is a data release, not a speech.
Incremental reaction function:
Re-arms the December hike case and weakens the dissenters’ argument that realized inflation was too soft to justify continued tightening. It does not by itself make October the base-case hike because the composition is partly mechanical and no policymaker endorsed faster timing in-window.
Ueda presser transcript — released 24 Sep 2026
Importance: **
Key policy signal:
Formalizes the post-meeting “no preset pace” and overshoot-risk framework; no materially new delta versus the 18 September live coverage was identified.
Representative quote:
No new incremental quote isolated in the supplied validated tape.
Incremental reaction function:
Keeps October timing flexible and confirms that the BoJ wants to preserve discretion rather than pre-commit to a fixed hiking rhythm. Tokyo CPI makes that flexibility asymmetrically hawkish rather than neutral.
Monetary base — in-window
Importance: *
Key policy signal:
Monetary base fell 15.2% y/y, consistent with the passive QT backdrop.
Representative quote:
No quote applicable.
Incremental reaction function:
Structural tightening continues alongside the rate path, but this is background information rather than a meeting-timing trigger.
Bottom line — central bank tape
Policy remains in a tightening cycle at 1.25%, and the first post-hike data shock pushes the balance hawkishly. October remains best framed as a hold / communication-and-Outlook meeting, while December tightening is re-established as the working baseline with upside risk to cadence if national CPI confirms Tokyo and the Outlook pulls forward the inflation path. The main falsification risk is that Tokyo’s 2.7% proves largely mechanical once subsidy effects are decomposed or that national inflation fails to confirm the city signal.
E. SIGNAL TENSION CHECK
Main contradiction:
BoJ-relevant inflation has reaccelerated sharply at the leading-indicator level even as domestic demand and household spending quality remain soft.
Why it matters:
The Bank now has stronger evidence for continued normalization, but not stronger evidence that households can absorb a faster hiking cadence without damage.
Implication:
The policy direction is more hawkish, but the pace remains conditional on national CPI, services/wage persistence and whether the Tokyo jump is broad-based rather than mostly subsidy arithmetic.
F. 5 KEY RELEASES TO DIG INTO
**** S&P Global Manufacturing / Services / Composite PMI Flash (24 Sep)
Why it matters: The dominant release of the week. Manufacturing and exports remain powerful and price-setting stays close to record strength, but composite and services momentum slowed and domestic orders softened. This is the clearest read on the growth/inflation tension confronting the BoJ.
** Chain Store Sales (25 Sep)
Why it matters: The headline remained positive, but falling traffic and price-led turnover make the underlying demand signal weak. It directly tests whether Japan's industrial strength is transmitting into household demand.
G. RED TEAM QUESTIONS
1. How much of Tokyo core CPI at 2.7% is subsidy/base-effect arithmetic versus genuine underlying services and demand-driven inflation?
2. Does national September CPI confirm the Tokyo signal strongly enough to validate a December hike and reopen a quarterly cadence?
3. Are near-record PMI selling prices translating into persistent domestic services inflation, or primarily reflecting energy, FX and supply-chain shocks?
4. Does the seven-month-high hiring signal materially contradict weak household demand, or is employment responding with a lag to earlier industrial strength?
5. How much of chain-store weakness is weather noise versus a genuine price-fatigue / real-income problem?
6. Does the post-hike yen response show that BoJ policy-path credibility is returning, or does the rate differential still dominate even with inflation above target?
7. What would falsify the current “hold October / re-arm December” policy state: a soft national CPI decomposition, a sharper domestic-demand break, or a renewed yen slide despite stronger inflation?
H. CURRENCY MARKET-CONSENSUS SCAN
Bottom line
Pair convention: USD/JPY, where a lower pair means a stronger JPY.
The public institutional view during 20–26 September was yen-constructive over the medium horizon but much less confident tactically. The BoJ had just raised the policy rate to 1.25%, intervention risk remained unusually high and September bank forecasts generally expected USD/JPY to drift lower over time. But the September meeting’s two dovish dissents and lack of forceful forward guidance weakened confidence in rapid follow-up tightening, while the Fed’s own hawkish turn and elevated U.S. yields kept the bilateral rate differential wide. Reuters described the yen as vulnerable despite the BoJ hike; ING explicitly argued that much yen-positive policy news was already priced.
The cleanest public anchors were clustered around the high-150s. A Reuters article on 25 September cited 157 as the median six-month USD/JPY projection from analysts polled earlier in the month. MUFG’s September forecast had 158 for Q3, 156 for Q4 and 154 for Q1 2027. ING’s September profile was more cautious on the yen, with 159 at one and three months, 158 at six months and 157 at twelve months; its published quarter-end forecast had USD/JPY at 160 for Q4. The visible public sample therefore did not support a clean immediate yen-appreciation trade even though the medium-term skew remained toward a lower USD/JPY.
By 25 September, the pair had traded as high as roughly 158.60 before falling toward 157.20 after Japan disclosed that President Trump had expressed concern about yen weakness and U.S. and Japanese officials reaffirmed cooperation. That official-policy channel was important: it placed a political/intervention cap on unchecked yen depreciation even as monetary-rate differentials still favored USD tactically.
Public-bank relevant-horizon range: USD/JPY 156–160 across visible Q4 / near-term ING and MUFG forecasts, with the Reuters six-month poll median at 157.
Constructive JPY cluster: USD/JPY 156–157 over the medium horizon, anchored by MUFG Q4 156 and the Reuters six-month median near 157.
More cautious / tactical view: USD/JPY 159–160, reflecting ING’s near-term/Q4 profile and the market’s skepticism that the BoJ would tighten quickly enough to offset Fed/U.S.-yield support.
Bias at window close: medium-term JPY constructive, but tactically two-sided; official intervention/cooperation risk supported the yen while the global rates differential and uncertain BoJ follow-through capped conviction.
What changed through the data window
The week began after a “dovish hike” reaction. The BoJ’s move to 1.25% was already largely priced, and the two dissenting votes plus limited hawkish guidance left USD/JPY vulnerable to renewed upside. Reuters on 22 September had the pair around 157.4 and highlighted the market’s concern that the BoJ might not raise rates quickly enough to narrow the gap with global peers.
The domestic data did not resolve that problem. The 24 September PMI kept the normalization story alive through strong manufacturing, hiring and pricing, but softer services and domestic orders argued against an aggressive pace. At the same time, higher U.S. yields and a hawkish Fed kept USD support intact.
The larger change came on 25 September from the policy/intervention channel rather than from the macro releases. Japan disclosed that Trump had raised concern over yen weakness and that Katayama/Bessent reaffirmed cooperation, taking USD/JPY from around 158.60 toward 157.20. The result at window close was not a fresh broad yen-bull consensus, but a stronger sense that upside USD/JPY would face official resistance even before the BoJ delivered another hike.
Main consensus drivers
BoJ normalization versus pace uncertainty:
The September hike established a firmer policy floor, but dissent and cautious guidance made rapid follow-up tightening uncertain. Medium-term yen forecasts depended on continued normalization, not simply on the September move itself.
Fed / global yield differential:
The Fed hike and strong U.S. data kept U.S. yields high. With other major central banks also tightening, the rate differential was not narrowing fast enough to generate a clean sustained yen rally.
Intervention / bilateral policy cooperation:
Joint U.S.–Japan intervention in July, September rate checks and explicit U.S.–Japan concern over yen weakness created an unusual policy backstop. This reduced the probability of an unchecked move materially above the high-150s / 160 area.
Repatriation / positioning asymmetry:
Fast-money yen shorts had already been substantially unwound and speculative positioning had flipped net long, but Reuters reported that the larger “slow-money” repatriation by pensions and life insurers had not yet begun. That limited the immediate fuel for a durable yen appreciation trend.
Where consensus could be wrong
Consensus too bullish JPY:
If the BoJ pauses for longer than expected, domestic demand remains weak and U.S. yields stay elevated, the rate differential can dominate intervention rhetoric and leave USD/JPY closer to 159–160 or higher.
Consensus too cautious JPY:
If BoJ normalization continues, Japanese yields stabilize enough to trigger genuine domestic repatriation and U.S.–Japan authorities keep resisting yen weakness, USD/JPY can fall through the 156–157 forecast cluster toward a materially stronger yen path.
Key falsification:
A durable break in USD/JPY requires either clear narrowing of the U.S.–Japan rate differential or evidence that slow-money repatriation has begun; absent either, intervention/rate-check pressure alone is unlikely to establish a sustained JPY appreciation trend.
Source anchors: Reuters, 22 and 25 September 2026; Reuters six-month analyst projection cited 25 September; ING September G10 FX Talking / forecasts; MUFG September 2026 JPY Monthly / FX Outlook.

<<JPY_WEEK_BEGIN>>

DATA WINDOW: 6 September 2026 to 19 September 2026

## A. JPY WEEK — MACRO SYNTHESIS

Japan remained resilient enough to sustain policy normalization, but the growth mix was narrow and increasingly two-speed. Q2 GDP was revised to +0.4% q/q, corporate sentiment remained firm and AI-linked manufacturing/export activity was strong, yet private consumption was flat, capex contracted for a second quarter, machinery orders retraced and much of the external growth contribution came through import compression rather than stronger real exports. The current account remained structurally protected by very large overseas income receipts, but the customs deficit and sharply higher import unit values showed a worsening terms-of-trade channel.

Inflation remained the binding policy constraint. Nationwide core CPI eased to 1.7%, but CGPI remained 7.6% y/y and yen-basis import prices +24.8%, leaving an unusually large gap between current household inflation and the upstream pipeline. The BoJ nevertheless accelerated the normalization cycle with a 25bp hike to 1.25% only three months after June, while Masu and Ueda formally embedded an overshoot-prevention doctrine into the reaction function. The important state transition was therefore **hawkish delivery plus institutional bifurcation**: the seven-member majority continues to favor normalization, but Asada and Sato's dissents establish a counted two-member reflationist bloc and Ueda explicitly refused to pre-commit the next hiking pace. The macro regime is **narrow growth resilience / exceptionally strong upstream inflation / weak household breadth / normalization intact but cadence and FX transmission increasingly contested**.

## B. JPY MACRO SIGNAL SCOREBOARD

Growth: ~

Supporting evidence:  
Positive Q2 GDP, resilient corporate sentiment, strong AI/electronics demand and continued export growth are offset by flat household consumption, a second quarterly capex contraction, volatile/soft machinery orders and only modest real export-volume growth. Aggregate activity remains resilient enough to remove a near-term growth veto on BoJ normalization, but domestic-demand breadth remains insufficient for a clean acceleration signal.

Labor: ~

Supporting evidence:  
No dedicated labor-market release was contained in this DATA WINDOW. Wage/price transmission remains central to the BoJ's forward inflation framework, but the supplied week contains no new labor release sufficient to move the bucket directionally.

Inflation: -

Supporting evidence:  
Realized consumer inflation is currently close to target rather than accelerating—core CPI slowed to 1.7%, its seventh consecutive sub-2% month—but CGPI at 7.6%, import-price inflation near 25%, elevated oil/input costs and earlier yen weakness leave a substantial forward pass-through risk. The BoJ is explicitly tightening against projected overshoot risk rather than current CPI acceleration.

Financial Conditions: -

Supporting evidence:  
The BoJ raised the policy rate to 1.25%, compressing the hiking interval from roughly six months to three months. Financial conditions remain described as accommodative, but the trajectory is toward less accommodation. The yen's failure to strengthen durably through the hike also means the FX channel has not delivered the intended tightening in imported-price conditions.

Policy Constraint: -

Supporting evidence:  
The constraint has become more institutional as well as macroeconomic. The seven-member majority has adopted the deviation-prevention / continued-normalization doctrine, but Asada and Sato dissented against the September hike and Ueda refused to commit to a specific pace. The Bank is therefore balancing a large upstream inflation pipeline and weak-yen risk against subdued realized CPI, weak household demand and an increasingly visible committee split as rates enter the estimated neutral range.

## C. FULL RELEASE ARCHIVE

TUESDAY 8 SEP 2026

JAPAN — Balance of Payments / Current Account / Goods Balance (Jul)

Release Date: 8 Sep 2026 | Local Time: 01:50  
Importance: ***

1. Reuters Data

Current Account NSA JPY: 2 988,9B | Prior: -92,3B | Poll: 2 869,8B | Surprise: 119,10B  
Current Account Bal SA: 2 523 100M | Prior: 1 396 900M  
Trade Bal Cust Basis SA: -429 381,00M | Prior: -580 795,00M

2. Professional Market Commentary

The headline is a strong current-account rebound, but one driven primarily by overseas income and services rather than by goods trade. The unadjusted surplus returned to JPY2.989tn after June's deficit and exceeded consensus by JPY119.1bn; the seasonally adjusted surplus also rose sharply to JPY2.523tn.

The principal support came from a JPY4.290tn primary-income surplus and a narrower services deficit, while the goods account remained negative as imports rose faster than exports. Travel remained a clear positive through strong inbound receipts, while transport services deteriorated amid higher freight costs.

Professional coverage therefore treated the release less as evidence of renewed export strength and more as confirmation of Japan's structural overseas-income buffer against a deteriorating trade and energy-cost backdrop.

The Reuters `JPVTBS` row is mechanically preserved but its value corresponds to the official BoP-basis seasonally adjusted goods balance rather than the separate customs-clearance trade balance.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

This is a headline external-account recovery without a corresponding recovery in the goods / terms-of-trade leg.

Signal hierarchy:

- current account NSA: JPY2.989tn, comfortably above consensus
- current account SA: JPY2.523tn, +80.6% m/m
- primary income: JPY4.290tn, still the dominant surplus engine
- services deficit: materially narrower
- travel: JPY558.5bn surplus
- goods: JPY399.9bn NSA deficit, more than double the year-earlier deficit
- exports: +24.1% y/y
- imports: +25.9% y/y
- seasonally adjusted goods balance: still negative
- portfolio flows: strong foreign purchases of Japanese equities alongside continued Japanese purchases of overseas assets.

The main analytical distinction is between Japan's stock of overseas wealth and the quality of current trade flows.

Income on foreign assets is sufficiently large to generate a substantial current-account surplus even while the goods-and-services account remains negative. That is structurally supportive for Japan's external balance, but the direct JPY implication is weaker than the surplus headline suggests because overseas income is not necessarily repatriated immediately.

The July improvement also contains a timing component. Dividend receipts from overseas subsidiaries were unusually strong, making it inappropriate to extrapolate the current-account beat one-for-one into subsequent months.

Meanwhile, the trade configuration remains adverse. Imports continue to outpace exports as energy and freight costs rise, consistent with a healthy overall external balance coexisting with a deteriorating terms-of-trade channel.

State transition: stabilization.

Context from prior regional live file:

Japan's prior external-account sequence had already shown a large current-account surplus increasingly dependent on overseas investment income as the goods balance weakened under higher energy costs. July restores the headline surplus after June's negative print, but the continuing goods deficit means the regime remains strong external income offsetting a weaker trade and terms-of-trade position rather than a broad external-sector improvement.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The release is JPY-supportive as an external-balance buffer, but considerably less bullish than the headline surplus alone implies.

Constructive:

- very large primary-income surplus
- strong tourism balance
- sharp SA current-account rebound
- meaningful foreign purchases of Japanese equities
- no conventional external-funding vulnerability.

Caution:

- goods remain in deficit
- imports are growing faster than exports
- higher oil and freight costs are a negative real-income shock
- part of the income surge is timing-related
- portfolio flows remain two-way.

BoJ implication: limited direct reaction-function value, but the composition reinforces the weak-yen / imported-inflation constraint.

JPY implication: structurally constructive relative to a deficit economy, but insufficient for a clean spot-FX bullish signal while the trade account remains weak and Japanese investors continue buying foreign assets.

Next verification: the August BoP goods balance, persistence of primary-income strength, tourism receipts and whether the stronger yen eventually reduces the import bill.


JAPAN — GDP Second Preliminary Estimate / Capex Revision (Q2)

Release Date: 8 Sep 2026 | Local Time: 01:50  
Importance: ***

1. Reuters Data

GDP Rev QQ Annualised: 1,4% | Prior: 1,1% | Poll: 1,6% | Surprise: -0,20%  
GDP Revised QQ: 0,4% | Prior: 0,3% | Poll: 0,4% | Surprise: 0,00%  
GDP Cap Ex Rev QQ: -0,9% | Prior: -1,2% | Poll: -0,8% | Surprise: -0,10%

2. Professional Market Commentary

The professional read was an upward headline revision that removes a near-term growth objection to BoJ tightening, but with much weaker composition than the 1.4% annualised number suggests.

Q2 growth was revised from 0.3% to 0.4% q/q as business investment proved less weak than initially estimated, although the annualised 1.4% remained below the Reuters consensus and capex at -0.9% was slightly weaker than expected.

Daiwa's Kento Minami emphasized that positive growth through a quarter exposed to the Middle East shock left little immediate reason for the BoJ to refrain from normalization.

The official accounts qualify that resilience. Private consumption was flat, business investment contracted for a second consecutive quarter, domestic demand subtracted 0.1pp from growth, and the +0.5pp net-export contribution came predominantly through falling imports rather than stronger exports.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

The second estimate improves the measured growth rate but not materially the quality of Q2 growth.

Signal hierarchy:

- GDP: +0.4% q/q versus +0.5% in Q1
- revision: modestly positive through less-weak capex
- private consumption: 0.0% q/q
- household consumption: -0.1%
- capex: -0.9%, second consecutive quarterly decline
- private inventories: +0.3pp contribution
- government consumption: +0.3pp
- public inventories: -0.5pp
- net exports: +0.5pp
- exports contribution: only +0.1pp
- falling imports contribution: +0.4pp
- real compensation of employees: revised higher.

The external contribution is therefore not a conventional export-led growth signal. Most of the arithmetic reflects import compression and hence weak domestic absorption.

The private-demand detail is similarly fragile. Household services consumption fell sharply, durables were weak and capex remained negative despite the upward revision. The investment mix was highly uneven, with positive machinery/structures components offset by weak transport equipment and intellectual property.

There is nevertheless an income-side offset. Real compensation of employees improved, providing a potentially better base for household demand in subsequent quarters.

The second-estimate methodology also limits precision around capex: the incorporated corporate-survey measure and supply-side source were unusually far apart. The appropriate signal is therefore capex contraction confirmed, magnitude uncertain.

For the BoJ, this is adequate aggregate growth but weak private-demand breadth.

State transition: deterioration.

Context from prior regional live file:

Q1 had delivered solid positive growth, but the prior tape already treated the strength cautiously because household momentum was uneven and capex had weakened. Q2 preserves positive headline growth but validates that caution: consumption is flat, capex remains negative and much of the external contribution is import compression. Japan remains resilient enough for normalization without exhibiting a broad private-demand acceleration.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The release is BoJ-enabling rather than BoJ-driving.

It removes a growth veto on tightening: Japan continued expanding through a difficult quarter and real employee compensation improved.

But it does not provide a growth-led argument for faster tightening:

- domestic demand is negative
- consumption is flat
- capex has declined for two quarters
- inventories materially influence the arithmetic
- most of the external contribution comes from lower imports.

Rates implication: supportive of continued normalization in conjunction with inflation and wages, but insufficient to justify acceleration.

JPY implication: modestly positive through the BoJ reaction-function channel, not through a standalone growth shock.

The key Q3 test is whether improving household income converts into consumption and whether business investment turns positive outside AI-linked sectors.


WEDNESDAY 9 SEP 2026

JAPAN — Reuters Tankan Non-Manufacturers Index (Sep)

Release Date: 9 Sep 2026 | Local Time: 01:00  
Importance: ***

1. Reuters Data

Reuters Tankan N-Man Idx: 29 | Prior: 28

2. Professional Market Commentary

The Reuters Tankan points to continued improvement in corporate sentiment, led more strongly by AI- and semiconductor-facing manufacturing than by domestic services.

The non-manufacturing index edged to +29 from +28, while the manufacturing index within the same survey rose to +21 from +18, its highest level since December 2021. Electronics surged to +39 from +24 on semiconductor and data-centre demand, while steel/non-ferrous remained negative.

Firms continued to flag Middle East risks, higher input costs and weak domestic consumption. The manufacturing three-month outlook nevertheless rose to +27.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

This is a constructive corporate-confidence print, but the improvement remains more convincing on the manufacturing / AI side than on broad domestic demand.

Signal hierarchy:

- non-manufacturing: +29 from +28
- manufacturing: +21 from +18
- electronics: +39 from +24
- manufacturing outlook: +27
- non-manufacturing outlook: +27
- steel/non-ferrous: -13
- soft domestic consumption remains a stated risk
- input-cost/geopolitical risks remain active.

The corporate sector is absorbing higher rates and energy costs better than households, but the concentration of the strongest improvement in electronics prevents a clean inference that overall domestic demand is accelerating.

At the same time, this is broader than a single semiconductor-orders spike: several business-facing sectors remain positive.

State transition: continuation.

Context from prior regional live file:

The prior Reuters Tankan had already shown a two-speed economy, with technology-facing manufacturing more resilient than household-facing activity. September strengthens the manufacturing leg while non-manufacturing merely remains healthy. The regime is therefore corporate-resilient but still structurally uneven.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

BoJ-enabling, not BoJ-driving.

The survey weakens the argument that further normalization would destabilize the corporate sector, but it does not establish broad domestic overheating.

Rates implication: mildly hawkish through activity resilience.

JPY implication: modestly constructive through the policy channel.

The key verification test is the 1 October official BoJ Tankan, particularly whether manufacturing breadth extends beyond electronics and whether selling-price expectations confirm the producer-price pipeline.

FRIDAY 11 SEP 2026

JAPAN — Corporate Goods Price Index (Aug)

Release Date: 11 Sep 2026 | Local Time: 01:50  
Importance: ****

1. Reuters Data

Corp Goods Price MM: -0,2% | Prior: 0,1% | Revised: 0,4% | Poll: 0,0% | Surprise: -0,20%  
Corp Goods Price YY: 7,6% | Prior: 7,2% | Revised: 7,7% | Poll: 7,4% | Surprise: 0,20%

2. Professional Market Commentary

The institutional framing was persistently hot wholesale inflation despite softer monthly momentum.

CGPI rose 7.6% y/y, above consensus and only marginally below July's sharply revised 7.7%, leaving wholesale inflation above 7% for a third consecutive month.

Import prices remained the more policy-relevant line. Yen-basis import prices were still up 24.8% y/y despite easing from 29.3%, showing that earlier yen weakness and the energy shock continued to feed the domestic cost structure.

The pressure was broad: non-ferrous metals, ICT equipment, petroleum/coal and chemicals all recorded large increases.

The monthly details are less hawkish. CGPI fell 0.2% m/m and import-price inflation was moderating, indicating that the inherited price pipeline remained extremely elevated but the incremental shock was no longer intensifying at July's pace.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

This is the clearest upstream-inflation constraint in the Japan tape, but it is better described as persistent elevated pressure than fresh acceleration.

Signal hierarchy:

- CGPI y/y: +7.6%, above consensus
- CGPI m/m: -0.2%
- July revised substantially hotter
- yen-basis import prices: +24.8% y/y
- import-price inflation: slowing from July
- non-ferrous metals: +43.3%
- ICT equipment: +19.5%
- petroleum/coal: +14.7%
- consumer CPI cross-check: only 1.7% core / 1.9% core-core.

The key macro tension is between pipeline pressure and final-price realization.

Corporate prices are running at multiples of consumer inflation. Some of the gap reflects energy, FX and commodity sensitivity; some reflects government subsidies depressing household energy prices. But either producer pressure must eventually fade or a greater portion must be passed into consumer prices and corporate margins.

The breadth argues against treating this solely as crude oil. Metals, information equipment and other categories are contributing, including AI-related demand.

The counter-signal is momentum: the monthly PPI decline and slower import-price inflation suggest the marginal shock is beginning to stabilize.

State transition: stabilization.

Context from prior regional live file:

June CGPI had already accelerated materially and import prices were running near 30%, establishing producer-price pass-through as one of the principal constraints on gradual BoJ normalization. July was subsequently revised still hotter. August retains essentially all of that annual pressure but shows softer monthly momentum, shifting the pipeline from acceleration toward stabilization at an exceptionally high level.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

A high-value hawkish confirmation for the September BoJ meeting, but stronger evidence for continued normalization than for mechanically faster hikes.

At the time of release:

- wholesale inflation remained above 7%
- the y/y headline exceeded expectations
- import prices were nearly 25% above year-earlier levels
- energy, metals, AI-linked equipment and FX were contributing.

The subsequent BoJ decision validated that mapping by explicitly focusing on elevated producer prices, import costs and the risk of pass-through into consumer inflation.

Rates implication: reinforces the structural tightening bias.

JPY implication: supportive via relative-rate policy, but a stronger yen itself becomes part of the mechanism that can cool future CGPI.

The decisive next test is whether the pipeline reaches core/core-core CPI and services rather than remaining concentrated upstream.
