<<JPY_WEEK_BEGIN>>

DATA WINDOW: 16 August 2026 to 29 August 2026

# A. JPY WEEK — MACRO SYNTHESIS

Japan ended the window in a **growth-stabilization / inflation-broadening / hawkish-policy-consolidation** regime rather than a clean domestic-demand expansion. Q2 GDP exposed weak internal quality — flat consumption and a second consecutive capex contraction — but subsequent machinery orders, PMIs, export strength and high coincident activity materially reduced the risk that this represented the start of a broad downturn. The economy remained two-speed: semiconductor/AI-linked manufacturing and exports strong, household-sensitive activity materially softer, and realized capex still lagging forward intentions. Inflation became the clearest directional signal: nationwide core-core re-accelerated to 1.9%, Tokyo core-core reached 2.0% despite policy suppression, PMI selling-price pressure remained near record levels and SPPI stayed elevated, albeit with the marginal July acceleration heavily freight-driven. Labour remained tight in stock terms but soft in flow terms.

The validated BoJ tape materially strengthens the normalization interpretation. Deputy Governor Himino's 27 August speech explicitly framed still-accommodative policy as continuing to press the accelerator, argued for easing off through timely rate hikes and said upside inflation risks deserved greater attention, while dismissing the Q2 GDP disappointment as likely technical. That converts the earlier faster-pace debate into a more center-owned reaction-function signal ahead of 17–18 September. The counterweight is no longer simply weak growth: the late-window yen firming without intervention reduces FX urgency and could itself damp imported inflation if sustained. The policy tension therefore shifts toward **September tightening with uncertainty concentrated in vote/guidance and the pace beyond 1.25%, rather than whether normalization remains viable at all.**

# B. JPY MACRO SIGNAL SCOREBOARD

Growth: ~

Supporting evidence:
Q2 GDP expanded +0.3% q/q but materially undershot expectations and relied on external demand while consumption was flat and capex fell 1.2%. The subsequent tape was considerably stronger: machinery orders rebounded +9.7% m/m with a +4.9% Q3 forecast, the composite PMI rose to 53.4, manufacturing to 55.1 and services to 52.3, while exports remained strong. Himino subsequently characterized the Q2 miss as likely technical rather than a reason to halt normalization. The result is stabilization and improving forward momentum without confirmation of broad domestic-demand re-acceleration.

Labor: ~

Supporting evidence:
The unemployment rate fell to 2.4%, confirming extremely limited slack, but employment also declined and the improvement came through a larger contraction in the labour force. The jobs/applicants ratio remained at 1.18 versus 1.19 expected, active vacancies edged lower and new-vacancy momentum weakened. Labour remains tight enough to support wages, but hiring demand is not strengthening.

Inflation: -

Supporting evidence:
Underlying inflation deteriorated materially as a macro constraint. Nationwide core rose to 1.8% and core-core to 1.9%; Tokyo core accelerated for a third month and core-core reached 2.0% despite energy and childcare measures suppressing measured inflation. PMI selling prices remained near record levels and SPPI reached 3.6%, although freight accounted for much of its marginal acceleration. Himino explicitly shifted attention toward upside price risks and the possibility of underlying inflation exceeding 2%.

Financial Conditions: ~

Supporting evidence:
The imported-energy and weak-yen channel remained adverse through much of the period, but the validated central-bank/FX tape records a material late-window yen firming without intervention, beginning to cool import-cost pressure. Strong exports and semiconductor demand remained an external cushion. Financial conditions therefore became more two-sided rather than uniformly restrictive.

Policy Constraint: -

Supporting evidence:
The constraint on remaining at 1.00% strengthened materially. Himino argued that the economy remained in an accelerator-like policy configuration and that rates should be adjusted in a timely manner to avoid more abrupt tightening later. July CPI and Tokyo CPI moved in the direction of the BoJ's inflation projection, while the Q2 GDP miss was explicitly discounted by Himino. The principal brakes are weak domestic-demand quality, softer hiring flows and the possibility that continued yen strength reduces imported-inflation pressure. The supplied tape characterizes a September hike as heavily priced, though the pricing anchor is secondary-sourced.

# C. FULL RELEASE ARCHIVE

## MONDAY 17 AUG 2026

### Japan — GDP / Private Consumption / Capital Expenditure / External Demand (Q2 Preliminary)

Release Date: 17 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: ****

**1. Reuters Data**

GDP QQ: 0,3% | Prior: 0,5% | Poll: 0,5% | Surprise: -0,20%
GDP QQ Annualised: 1,1% | Prior: 1,8% | Revised: 1,9% | Poll: 2,0% | Surprise: -0,90%
GDP QQ Pvt Consmp Prelim: 0,0% | Prior: 0,3% | Revised: 0,5% | Poll: 0,5% | Surprise: -0,50%
GDP QQ Capital Expend.: -1,2% | Prior: -0,7% | Revised: -1,0% | Poll: 0,4% | Surprise: -1,60%
GDP QQ External Demand: 0,5% | Prior: 0,3% | Poll: 0,3% | Surprise: 0,20%

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

The professional read is positive headline growth but weak domestic composition. Japan recorded a third consecutive quarterly expansion, but the 1.1% annualized pace missed the 2.0% consensus and was carried overwhelmingly by the external side rather than household or business demand.

Key tape focus:

* household consumption effectively flat versus +0.5% expected
* business investment -1.2%, a second consecutive quarterly decline
* net exports provided the key growth contribution
* part of the domestic-demand weakness reflected unusual inventory accounting, notably the government oil-reserve drawdown
* disagreement is therefore less about whether Q2 was soft internally than about whether that softness persists into Q3

The more constructive institutional interpretation is that Japan nevertheless avoided the feared supply-side contraction during the Middle East shock. Government stockpile releases and alternative sourcing prevented severe resource shortages, while global AI demand continued to support activity. The risk is shifting from physical supply constraints toward delayed price/income pressure on households and firms.

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

The headline understates the weakness of final domestic demand, but the domestic-demand headline itself also overstates it because of unusual inventory effects.

Real GDP rose +0.3% q/q / +1.1% annualized, but domestic demand subtracted 0.2pp while net exports added 0.5pp. Private demand was flat. Private consumption was effectively unchanged, residential investment fell 0.5%, and non-residential investment contracted 1.2%, contributing -0.2pp.

The hierarchy inside the print is:

* **Primary negative signal — domestic final demand:** consumption failed to follow Q1's revised +0.5% rise and capex contracted for a second quarter.
* **Primary offset — external demand:** net exports contributed +0.5pp. Exports rose only +0.5%, while imports fell -1.5%, meaning a meaningful part of the GDP contribution came mechanically from import compression rather than a large export-volume impulse.
* **Inventory distortion:** private inventories contributed +0.3pp while public inventories subtracted -0.5pp. The latter largely reflected the strategic oil-stock release rather than a conventional collapse in public demand.
* **Consumption quality is weaker than the zero headline:** durables were firm, helped by autos and air conditioners, but non-durables fell sharply and services edged lower.
* **Capex is the cleanest concern:** -1.2% follows a revised -1.0% in Q1. Unlike the public-inventory drag, this is not primarily an accounting artefact.
* **Income/spending divergence:** real employee compensation rose approximately 0.8–0.9% q/q while consumption was flat.
* **GDI warning:** real GDI was unchanged even as real GDP rose 0.3%, consistent with the terms-of-trade deterioration absorbing part of the apparent output gain.

There is also a meaningful measurement issue. Supply-side indicators had suggested stronger Q2 consumption than the national accounts, while first-preliminary business investment is especially revision-sensitive because the second estimate incorporates the Ministry of Finance corporate survey.

State transition: deterioration.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
Q1 had initially produced a strong +0.5% q/q growth print with improving consumption, but subsequent revisions had already exposed a capex-quality warning. Q2 deepens that split: headline growth remains positive, yet consumption stalls and business investment contracts again while external demand carries the expansion. Japan therefore moves from resilient-but-mixed growth toward a more explicit domestic-demand vulnerability, without yet entering a broad contraction regime.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

This is a growth-quality negative for the BoJ reaction function, but not a standalone dovish trigger.

The release weakens the clean case for near-term normalization because the two domestic channels most relevant for sustaining endogenous inflation — household consumption and business investment — both disappointed.

However:

* the economy is still expanding rather than contracting
* real employee compensation is rising
* severe supply constraints were avoided
* part of the domestic-demand drag is inventory/accounting related
* inflation and wages remain more important than one GDP estimate for the BoJ
* the second GDP estimate is an unusually relevant verification test because capex can be materially revised

For JPY/rates, the clean interpretation is less support for aggressive near-term BoJ tightening, not confirmation that normalization is over.

Macro configuration: positive headline growth / weak domestic final demand / external support / incomplete wage-consumption transmission.

### Japan — Tertiary Industry Activity Index (Jun)

Release Date: 17 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 06:30
Importance: **

**1. Reuters Data**

Tertiary Ind Act NSA: 5,3% | Prior: -1,4% | Revised: -1,6%

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

No Tier 1 institutional debate detected.

The critical distinction is between the Reuters NSA +5.3% line and the official seasonally adjusted activity measure. METI reported the tertiary-industry index at 106.7, down 0.2% m/m, the first decline in three months, while maintaining its assessment that activity was still picking up as a trend, though with pauses in parts.

The monthly weakness was concentrated in consumer-facing sectors:

* retail fell sharply
* leisure and restaurant-related activity weakened
* electricity demand declined

Against that, business-oriented services strengthened, particularly finance/securities activity and transport.

The official details therefore describe a soft consumer-services month against stronger corporate-facing activity, rather than the acceleration that a face-value reading of the Reuters +5.3% NSA figure would imply.

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

This is primarily a composition signal and a data-presentation issue, not a +5.3% services-growth print.

Signal hierarchy:

* Reuters NSA figure: +5.3% after a revised -1.6%; dominated by the normal June seasonal pattern.
* Official SA headline: -0.2% m/m, the first decline in three months.
* Underlying annual comparison: NSA index +2.5% y/y, arguing against a broad collapse.
* Household-facing services: broadly personal services -1.9%.
* Business-facing services: broadly business-oriented services +1.4%.

On the weak side:

* retail: -5.1%
* living/amusement-related services: -3.3%
* electricity/gas/water: -4.6%
* real estate, wholesale and medical/welfare activity also edged lower

METI linked an important part of the retail, restaurant and leisure weakness to adverse weather and event-calendar effects.

On the stronger side:

* finance and insurance: +4.7%
* securities/commodity activity roughly +24% y/y
* transport and postal services: +4.4%

This produces a consumer/corporate split that is more informative than the aggregate -0.2% headline.

The same-day GDP release makes the household weakness more relevant. National accounts showed essentially flat Q2 consumption, while this activity index shows June weakness in retail, dining and leisure.

The stronger transport reading should not automatically be interpreted as evidence of precautionary stockbuilding or war-related inventory movement. No source attribution establishes that mechanism.

State transition: no material change.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
Japan had already exhibited a two-speed activity pattern, with strong manufacturing and external sectors alongside weaker household-sensitive indicators and slower services momentum. June reinforces the household/corporate divergence without yet establishing a broader services downturn.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

Policy relevance is modestly dovish at the margin, but low-force.

For the BoJ, the useful information is not the Reuters +5.3% headline but:

* -0.2% SA services activity
* weakness in consumer-facing sectors
* same-day GDP evidence of flat household consumption
* continued resilience in corporate-facing activity

That configuration slightly weakens the case that rising wages are already generating a self-sustaining domestic-demand cycle.

But METI kept its recovery assessment unchanged, several weak categories were weather/event distorted, business services remained strong and annual tertiary activity was still positive.

For JPY/JGBs, this is a small confirmation of the domestic-demand caution contained in Q2 GDP, not an independent repricing catalyst.

## WEDNESDAY 19 AUG 2026

### Japan — Machinery Orders (Jun) / Q3 Forecast

Release Date: 19 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: ***

**1. Reuters Data**

Machinery Orders MM: 9,7% | Prior: -12,4% | Poll: 7,8% | Surprise: 1,90%
Machinery Orders YY: 16,9% | Prior: -1,9% | Poll: 10,8% | Surprise: 6,10%

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

The professional framing is a strong monthly rebound in forward capex demand, but not yet evidence of a broad realized-investment acceleration.

Core private-sector machinery orders excluding ships and electric power rose 9.7% m/m, reversing much of May’s 12.4% fall, while Q2 as a whole increased only 0.2% q/q.

More constructively, surveyed manufacturers expect core orders to rise 4.9% q/q in Q3, and the Cabinet Office maintained its assessment that machinery orders are showing signs of a pickup.

Key tape focus:

* strong beat after May’s collapse
* annual growth reaccelerated sharply
* Q2 aggregate essentially flat
* forward Q3 corporate-order guidance considerably stronger
* monthly manufacturing strength partly concentrated in a large order
* capex intentions look healthier than Q2 realized GDP investment

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

This release improves the forward capex pipeline, but the strongest signal is the Q3 forecast rather than the June monthly bounce itself.

Signal hierarchy:

1. **Forward signal:** surveyed manufacturers project core machinery orders at +4.9% q/q in Jul–Sep.
2. **June rebound:** +9.7% m/m beats the +7.8% Reuters poll and reverses most of May’s -12.4% decline.
3. **Annual confirmation:** +16.9% y/y versus +10.8% expected.
4. **Quarterly reality:** core machinery orders rose only +0.2% q/q in Q2.
5. **Breadth:** manufacturing +19.9% and non-manufacturing +4.5%, though manufacturing was helped by a large nonferrous-metals order.

This distinction matters against the GDP print two days earlier. Q2 national accounts showed realized non-residential investment falling 1.2% q/q for a second consecutive decline.

The data therefore describe:

orders/intention → constructive
realized Q2 capex → weak

That can be reconciled if projects were delayed rather than cancelled.

The available evidence does not establish whether the nonferrous-metals order, shipbuilding demand or other industrial strength reflects AI-related investment, geopolitical precautionary procurement, replacement demand or another specific mechanism.

State transition: stabilization.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
Japan machinery orders had already shown extreme month-to-month volatility. June’s +9.7% bounce after May’s -12.4% fall extends that noisy pattern, but the +4.9% Q3 forecast provides a more credible positive signal than the monthly headline alone. The capex story remains constructive at the intentions level but unconfirmed in realized investment.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

This is modestly BoJ-supportive, particularly after the weak investment component of Q2 GDP, but it is not a clean hawkish trigger.

* the Q3 +4.9% forecast reduces concern that corporate investment is entering a sustained contraction
* broad manufacturing/non-manufacturing improvement supports the corporate-demand side
* but Q2 orders were only +0.2% and realized GDP capex fell 1.2%
* concentration in one large nonferrous order further reduces the information value of the June monthly surge

For JPY/JGBs, the signal is mildly JPY/front-end supportive relative to the GDP disappointment.

Macro configuration: weak realized Q2 investment / resilient forward orders / high monthly volatility / cautiously constructive corporate capex pipeline.

## THURSDAY 20 AUG 2026

### Japan — Trade Statistics: Exports / Imports / Trade Balance (Jul)

Release Date: 20 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: ***

**1. Reuters Data**

Exports YY: 23,2% | Prior: 19,3% | Poll: 19,9% | Surprise: 3,30%
Imports YY: 27,8% | Prior: 25,4% | Poll: 26,5% | Surprise: 1,30%
Trade Balance Total Yen: -634,5B | Prior: -406,9B | Revised: -409,9B | Poll: -680,0B | Surprise: 45,50B

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

The professional framing is a record nominal trade boom with a worsening energy-driven external balance.

Exports accelerated to +23.2% y/y, their eleventh consecutive increase and an all-time monthly value record. Imports rose even faster at +27.8%, also to a record, leaving Japan in deficit for a third consecutive month despite the balance being modestly better than consensus.

The key distinction is between trade values and volumes. Export values rose 23.2%, but export volumes rose materially less; import values increased 27.8%, while import volumes rose only modestly.

Key tape focus:

* exports at a record value, led by autos and semiconductor-related products
* imports at a record value, led by crude oil and electronic components
* crude-oil import values surged
* semiconductor-component exports rose sharply
* third consecutive trade deficit despite very strong export growth
* physical energy availability improving through rerouting, but at materially higher import cost
* no confirmed release-specific JPY reaction

The scout evidence shows sharply reduced Middle East crude volumes alongside restored overall crude volumes through alternative sourcing, reinforcing the distinction between a quantity shock being managed and a price/terms-of-trade shock remaining acute.

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

This is not primarily a +23% real-export boom or a +28% domestic-import-demand boom. The key macro signal is the widening gap between nominal trade values and trade volumes.

Signal hierarchy:

1. **Terms-of-trade/import-cost shock remains dominant.**
2. **Export demand is genuinely strong, but much less explosive in real terms.**
3. **The external balance deteriorated sequentially.**
4. **The physical supply shock is easing faster than the price shock.**
5. **AI/technology exposure operates on both sides of the ledger.**
6. **Weak yen effects are two-sided.**

The bulk of the spectacular nominal trade growth is price and currency amplification, not equivalent real-activity acceleration.

There is nevertheless a meaningful positive quantity signal on exports. Semiconductor and auto strength confirm that Japan's industrial/export sector remains much firmer than domestic household demand.

The trade deficit should also be decomposed into:

**Oil / energy:** predominantly shock-sensitive and potentially reversible if crude prices, shipping conditions and the yen normalize.

**Technology / capital equipment:** potentially stickier because semiconductor and electrical-equipment imports can represent investment associated with data-center, digital and AI capacity.

The July configuration is therefore unusual: record exports coexist with a widening deficit because imported inflation is outrunning external income growth.

State transition: continuation.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
June had already established an export-resilient but energy-squeezed external configuration. July extends that sequence, with exports accelerating further but import costs rising even faster and the deficit widening for a third month. Japan retains a powerful industrial/export cushion while the energy bill continues to absorb an increasing share of that external strength.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

The release is BoJ-hawkish through imported inflation, growth-supportive through exports, but negative through household purchasing power and the external balance.

* strong export volumes reduce the probability that the external shock causes an industrial recession
* semiconductor and auto demand continue to support manufacturing activity
* successful crude-supply rerouting reduces the tail risk of production shortages
* but the energy bill keeps imported inflation elevated
* yen depreciation magnifies that channel
* higher import prices reduce real household purchasing power

This creates a hawkish inflation / weak-quality growth tension rather than a straightforward normalization signal.

Macro configuration: strong real exports / record nominal gross trade / successful supply rerouting / severe energy-price and FX amplification / persistent trade deficit.

## FRIDAY 21 AUG 2026

### Japan — Nationwide CPI / Core CPI (Jul)

Release Date: 21 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 01:30
Importance: ****

**1. Reuters Data**

CPI, Core Nationwide YY: Actual n/a | Prior: 1,6% | Poll: 1,8%
CPI, Overall Nationwide: Actual n/a | Prior: 1,7% | Revised: 1,6%

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

The professional read is hawkish beneath a subsidy-suppressed sub-2% headline.

The Statistics Bureau subsequently confirmed July CPI at +1.9% y/y overall, +1.8% ex-fresh food and +1.9% ex-fresh food and energy. The Reuters core actual therefore matched the 1.8% consensus, but the underlying composition was stronger than a simple in-line/sub-target reading suggests.

Key tape focus:

* core inflation accelerated from 1.6% to 1.8%
* core-core accelerated from 1.7% to 1.9%
* services inflation edged up, consistent with gradual labour-cost pass-through
* standard core remained below target largely because government fuel subsidies restrained measured energy inflation
* upstream/wholesale pressure, weak yen and wage pass-through point toward higher consumer inflation ahead

The supplied scout provides the wider policy and composition tape.

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

This release changes the inflation file because underlying inflation turns higher at the same time as the subsidy wedge keeps the conventional core measure below target.

Signal hierarchy:

1. **Underlying inflation re-accelerated.**
2. **The standard BoJ-linked core measure also accelerated, but remained optically below target.**
3. **Services are beginning to participate.**
4. **Food inflation broadened the household squeeze.**
5. **Energy remains distorted by policy.**
6. **The producer-consumer pipeline remains unusually wide.**

The key analytical split is:

**Subsidized / potentially reversible bucket**

* direct energy prices
* part of the oil/import shock
* government-administered subsidy effects

**Broader / potentially stickier bucket**

* core-core acceleration
* food and daily necessities
* service-price increases
* labour-cost pass-through

That distinction matters more than whether conventional core is currently 1.8% or 2.0%.

There is also an important data-vintage issue. July is the first monthly CPI release using the 2025 base, with historical data back-cast earlier in August.

State transition: reversal.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
June nationwide CPI had risen, but softer core-core inflation kept the move from becoming a broad domestic-inflation upgrade. July changes that hierarchy: core-core rebounds and services inflation firms alongside headline/core acceleration. The inflation file moves toward renewed underlying pass-through, even though household demand remains fragile.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

This is the strongest BoJ-supportive release of the week to this point.

The arguments for looking through the sub-target headline have strengthened:

* government subsidies suppress the measured energy contribution
* wholesale/import-cost pressure remains exceptionally high
* core-core inflation has turned upward
* services inflation is beginning to respond to labour costs
* firms appear more willing to pass costs through

The counter-case remains important: Japan is not displaying conventional demand overheating. Q2 consumption was flat and much of the prospective inflation acceleration originates in oil, FX and imported costs.

The next high-value test is August Tokyo CPI, particularly whether ex-fresh-food-and-energy inflation and services continue to broaden.

Macro configuration: subsidy-suppressed headline / re-accelerating core-core / nascent services pass-through / intense upstream pipeline / weak domestic-demand counterweight.

### Japan — S&P Global Manufacturing / Services / Composite PMI Flash (Aug)

Release Date: 21 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 02:30
Importance: ***

**1. Reuters Data**

S&P Global Mfg PMI Flash SA: 55,1 | Prior: 54,5
S&P Global Comp Op Flash SA: 53,4 | Prior: 52,7
S&P Global SVC PMI Flash SA: 52,3 | Prior: 51,2

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

The professional framing is stronger private-sector growth with unusually elevated corporate pricing power, led again by manufacturing.

The composite PMI rose to 53.4, its highest since February, while manufacturing climbed to 55.1 and services to 52.3.

Key tape focus:

* composite growth fastest in six months
* manufacturing continues to lead
* factory new orders and export demand strongest since early 2018
* semiconductor/AI pipelines remain central
* services activity improved, but foreign service demand remained weak
* selling-price pressure remained near record levels despite some easing in input inflation
* supply-chain disruption persisted

Annabel Fiddes of S&P Global highlighted robust new-work pipelines in semiconductor and AI-related industries while stressing that the outlook remains conditional on avoiding further price or demand shocks.

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

This is a genuine activity improvement, but the manufacturing headline still needs to be separated into end-demand strength, supply-chain effects and residual precautionary behaviour.

Growth breadth improved:

* composite: 52.7 → 53.4
* manufacturing PMI: 54.5 → 55.1
* services PMI: 51.2 → 52.3

The strongest evidence lies in manufacturing demand:

* total manufacturing new orders increased at the fastest pace since January 2018
* export orders rose at the sharpest rate since early 2018
* semiconductor and AI-related industries were explicitly identified as sources of new work
* employment and purchasing activity remained expansionary

But the stockbuilding caveat is not fully eliminated. Supplier delivery times were still lengthening and some precautionary purchasing remains possible.

Services are more mixed than the 52.3 headline:

* domestic new business strengthened
* services export business contracted markedly
* employment growth became marginal
* outstanding work increased only modestly

The inflation signal may be more important for the BoJ.

Sequence:

Nationwide CPI → core-core and services inflation turning higher
PMI → firms raising selling prices at near-record rates
Input pipeline → still elevated despite some sequential easing

Persistent near-record selling-price inflation after input costs begin to ease would be stronger evidence of second-round pass-through.

State transition: improvement.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
Earlier Japan PMI releases had already shown manufacturing strength but repeatedly carried a precautionary-stockbuilding caveat, while services lagged. August strengthens the signal because manufacturing orders and exports accelerate further while services also regain momentum. The regime shifts toward a more credible private-sector expansion, though still with uneven domestic breadth.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

This is BoJ-supportive and reinforces the CPI message released the same morning.

* stronger composite activity reduces concern that tightening would occur into an immediate private-sector slowdown
* semiconductor/AI demand provides a powerful corporate/export cushion
* the rebound in services reduces the degree to which growth is exclusively industrial
* near-record selling-price inflation corroborates CPI evidence of broader corporate pass-through
* easing input-cost inflation is not yet translating into correspondingly weak output-price inflation

The qualifications remain material: services employment is barely increasing, some manufacturing demand may still represent front-loading and household consumption remains weak.

Macro configuration: manufacturing/AI-led acceleration / improving services activity / residual stockbuilding risk / easing but elevated input costs / near-record selling-price pressure.

## MONDAY 25 AUG 2026

### Japan — Services Producer Price Index (Jul)

Release Date: 25 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: ***

**1. Reuters Data**

Service PPI: 3,6 | Prior: 3,2 | Revised: 3,4

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

The professional framing is firmer corporate-services inflation, but with an unusually important freight distortion.

Headline SPPI rose to +3.6% y/y from a revised 3.4%, but the decomposition substantially qualifies the headline:

* ocean freight transportation +67.4% y/y
* international transportation strongly higher
* SPPI excluding international transportation remained around +3.1%
* services with a high labour-cost ratio remained around +3.0%

The tape therefore contains two simultaneously valid narratives:

* persistent services prices remain compatible with labour shortages and continuing cost pass-through
* the marginal July acceleration is overwhelmingly transport/freight-related rather than a fresh broadening in domestic wage-sensitive services

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

The release resolves much of the central ambiguity: July SPPI is hot, but the acceleration is not predominantly domestic/sticky.

Signal hierarchy:

1. Headline corporate-services inflation remains elevated.
2. Marginal acceleration is concentrated in international transportation.
3. The clean ex-freight aggregate did not accelerate.
4. The labour-sensitive aggregate is similarly stable.
5. There is nevertheless broad underlying firmness across staffing, repair, food-service and professional/business categories.

The cleaner two-bucket interpretation is:

**External / potentially reversible**

* ocean freight
* international transport
* conflict-related routing and fuel costs

**Domestic / potentially persistent**

* wage-intensive business services
* staffing
* maintenance
* professional services
* food/service-sector labour costs

The first bucket explains most of the incremental July deterioration. The second explains why inflation remains elevated even after stripping the international-shipping shock.

The revision pattern also matters. The spring services-price shock was stronger and more persistent than initially observed in real time.

But underlying ex-international-transport services inflation is better described as persistent than accelerating.

State transition: continuation.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
May SPPI had already shown that Japan's services-price pipeline was elevated and heavily influenced by transport and freight costs. July extends that regime: headline inflation rises further as ocean freight reaches extreme levels, while the ex-international-transport measure remains stable. The BoJ inflation constraint persists, but its quality remains partly cost-push rather than purely wage-demand driven.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

This is BoJ-supportive, but less hawkish than the 3.6% headline initially appears.

The positive policy signal:

* corporate services inflation remains high
* previous months have been revised upward
* businesses continue to demonstrate pricing power
* SPPI sits alongside July core-core CPI acceleration and near-record PMI selling-price inflation

But if the BoJ is specifically looking for evidence that wages and labour shortages are generating endogenous services inflation, July does not materially strengthen that case.

The appropriate interpretation is:

underlying services inflation remains sufficiently firm to preserve normalization optionality, while an external freight shock temporarily adds another layer of headline pressure.

Macro configuration: persistent domestic services inflation / extreme war-freight overlay / headline acceleration stronger than underlying acceleration / continuing BoJ constraint.

### Japan — Leading Indicator Revised / Coincident Index (Jun)

Release Date: 25 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 07:00
Importance: **

**1. Reuters Data**

Leading Indicator Revised: 0 | Prior: 0

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

No Tier 1 institutional debate detected.

The June Leading Index was revised slightly higher and remained unchanged from May at a near-five-year high. The Coincident Index was revised higher and remained at a multi-year high, while the official cycle assessment stayed constructive.

The professional/desk framing is therefore high-level cyclical resilience rather than fresh monthly acceleration.

A separate Conference Board leading-index construction was softer in June, providing a useful caution against treating the Cabinet Office measure as an unambiguous acceleration signal.

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

The release is best interpreted as high current-cycle levels with plateauing forward momentum, not as evidence that Japan is entering a broad domestic boom.

The Reuters `0 / 0` row is a point-change representation, so the information lies in the levels and revisions.

Signal hierarchy:

1. leading index: plateau at a strong level
2. coincident index: stronger current activity
3. revisions: directionally constructive
4. household-facing hard data: weaker than the coincident index implies
5. likely reconciliation: strength concentrated in manufacturing, exports, semiconductor/AI-linked production and industrial components

Cross-data tension:

Current-cycle / industrial indicators → strong
Household domestic demand → weak
Forward indicators → positive, but no longer uniformly accelerating

State transition: stabilization.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
Japan’s prior leading-index sequence had already improved sharply from winter levels while industrial and semiconductor-linked indicators strengthened. The June revision extends that high-level resilience but no longer adds momentum, while household-sensitive data remain weaker. Japan stays in a strong industrial cycle / softer domestic-demand configuration rather than moving to broad-based expansion.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

Low standalone BoJ/JPY relevance.

This print is useful mainly because it pushes against a recessionary interpretation of weak Q2 GDP:

* coincident activity remains high
* the official cycle assessment remains constructive
* upward revisions support the current-activity side
* there is no evidence here of a broad contemporaneous collapse

But the BoJ reaction function is dominated by inflation breadth, wages and services-price pass-through rather than a revised composite indicator.

Macro configuration: high cycle levels / flat forward momentum / strong coincident activity / industrial concentration / weak household cross-check.

## THURSDAY 27 AUG 2026

### Japan — Tokyo CPI / Tokyo CPI ex Fresh Food (Aug Preliminary)

Release Date: 27 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 01:30
Importance: ****

**1. Reuters Data**

CPI Tokyo Ex fresh food YY: 1,8 | Prior: 1,9 | Revised: 1,7 | Poll: 1,7 | Surprise: 0,1
CPI, Overall Tokyo: 1,9 | Prior: 2 | Revised: 1,8

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

The professional read is another underlying-inflation confirmation despite policy measures actively suppressing the conventional headline.

Tokyo core CPI accelerated to 1.8% y/y, its third consecutive increase on the revised series and 0.1pp above the Reuters poll. Headline CPI rose to 1.9%.

More importantly for the BoJ, the measure excluding both fresh food and energy reached 2.0%, bringing the cleaner underlying gauge to the Bank's target even as the standard core measure remained below 2%.

The inflation mix remains distorted by policy:

* energy prices restrained by government measures
* Tokyo childcare policy mechanically reducing measured service prices
* food remaining an important positive contributor
* core nevertheless accelerating
* core-core reaching 2.0%

The print also landed after Deputy Governor Himino emphasized that the BoJ would reassess its baseline inflation scenario and associated risks at the September meeting.

Teikoku Databank's cost-pass-through ratio falling to 39.9% from 42.1% provides the principal counter-signal.

The supplied scout provides the detailed policy-distortion decomposition and named-economist tape.

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

This is a stronger inflation signal than the 1.8% conventional-core headline suggests.

Hierarchy:

1. **Core CPI accelerated for a third month.**
2. **Core-core reached the target.**
3. **Policy interventions are depressing the measured headline.**
4. **Food/pass-through remains active.**
5. **Corporate pass-through is not uniformly strengthening.**
6. **The 2025-base revision matters for comparisons.**

Analytical split:

**Policy-suppressed / potentially reversible**

* subsidized energy
* gasoline-tax effects
* free childcare
* eventual reversal of war-related freight/oil costs

**Underlying / potentially persistent**

* core-core at 2.0%
* food and necessities pass-through
* broader corporate price-setting
* wage/service-price transmission

The key qualification is that core-core reaching 2% proves breadth has improved; it does not prove that 2%+ inflation is sustainably demand-driven. Q2 consumption was flat, household-sensitive activity remains weak and small-firm pass-through capacity is deteriorating.

State transition: deterioration.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
July nationwide CPI had already reversed the earlier underlying-disinflation trend. August Tokyo CPI extends that move: core rises again and core-core reaches 2% despite explicit policy suppression of headline inflation. The inflation constraint is becoming more persistent, not merely more visible.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

This is one of the clearest BoJ-supportive prints in the August Japan file.

The reaction-function implication is stronger than the modest +0.1pp Reuters surprise because composition matters more than surprise:

* core inflation has accelerated for three months
* core-core has reached 2%
* energy is not generating the acceleration
* government measures are actively suppressing measured inflation
* July nationwide CPI showed the same underlying direction
* PMI output prices and SPPI confirm substantial corporate pricing pressure
* Himino did not push back against the September decision being live

The remaining counter-case is about quality and persistence, not direction.

Macro configuration: policy-suppressed headline / third consecutive core acceleration / core-core at target / broader pass-through / weak-household counterweight / materially stronger BoJ normalization constraint.

### Japan — Unemployment Rate / Labour Force Survey (Jul)

Release Date: 27 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 01:30
Importance: **

**1. Reuters Data**

Unemployment Rate: 2,4 | Prior: 2,5 | Poll: 2,5 | Surprise: -0,1

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

The professional framing is tighter headline unemployment, but with a materially weaker composition than the 2.4% rate suggests.

The unemployment rate fell to 2.4% from 2.5%, the lowest in one year and better than the 2.5% Reuters consensus.

However:

* unemployment declined
* employment also declined
* labour force contracted more than employment
* the unemployment-rate decline was therefore not generated by a hiring surge

The labour situation remains healthy in level terms but July is not a material acceleration in labour demand.

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

The headline is better; the underlying flow signal is not.

Signal hierarchy:

1. Stock of labour-market slack remains low.
2. Employment did not generate the improvement.
3. Participation/labour-supply composition matters.
4. July is materially weaker in quality than a hiring-driven fall in unemployment.
5. The broader level remains tight.

The unemployment/vacancy combination is coherent once stocks and flows are separated:

Unemployment stock → very tight
Hiring/vacancy flow → flat-to-softer

The BoJ does not need rising employment every month to normalize; the key is whether scarce labour still generates wage growth and service-price pass-through.

State transition: false dawn.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
Japan's labour file had previously been characterized by low unemployment, rising participation and strong employment levels while the jobs/applicants ratio remained broadly flat. July preserves the tight level but changes the composition: unemployment falls as labour supply contracts rather than as employment expands. The labour story shifts toward tightness increasingly supported by constrained supply rather than stronger hiring demand.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

This is BoJ-supportive in level, but not a fresh hawkish labour impulse.

The positive policy read:

* 2.4% unemployment confirms very limited slack
* firms still operate in a structurally tight labour environment
* that remains compatible with sustained wage pressure

But:

* employment fell
* labour force fell even more
* participation softened
* vacancy demand did not strengthen
* wages remain the missing confirmation variable

Macro configuration: very low unemployment / weaker employment flows / contracting labour supply / headline tightness stronger than demand momentum / wage transmission still unverified.

### Japan — Jobs/Applicants Ratio / Employment Referrals (Jul)

Release Date: 27 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 01:30
Importance: **

**1. Reuters Data**

Jobs/Applicants Ratio: 1,18 | Prior: 1,18 | Poll: 1,19 | Surprise: -0,01

**2. Professional Market Commentary**

No Tier 1 institutional debate detected.

The active jobs-to-applicants ratio remained at 1.18, unchanged from June and below the 1.19 Reuters consensus.

The flow detail was modestly softer:

* active job openings edged lower
* active job seekers increased
* new jobs/applicants declined
* regular-worker jobs/applicants remained around balance

The professional message is therefore still-tight vacancy levels but no incremental labour-demand tightening.

**3. Comment — Economist Layer**

This is the cleaner demand-side counterweight to the falling unemployment rate.

Signal hierarchy:

1. **Level remains tight.**
2. **Momentum is flat.**
3. **The surprise is mildly soft.**
4. **Forward vacancy creation weakened.**
5. **Underlying flows softened.**
6. **Sector breadth remains mixed, with industrial/semiconductor demand stronger than household-facing hiring.**

The unemployment/vacancy combination is:

Unemployment stock → very tight
Vacancy-flow momentum → flat/softening

Historically, the jobs/applicants ratio had already been stuck around 1.18 even while unemployment improved. July extends that pattern rather than changing it.

State transition: no material change.

**Context from prior regional live file:**
Japan's jobs/applicants ratio had repeatedly indicated tight labour availability without fresh acceleration in recruitment. July preserves exactly that distinction, while the fall in new vacancies adds a modest softening signal. Labour demand remains supportive of wage pressure in level terms but increasingly lacks momentum.

**4. HF Take — Strategist Layer**

Standalone BoJ relevance is low-to-moderate.

The ratio does not undermine normalization because:

* labour-market slack remains low
* vacancies still exceed applicants materially
* semiconductor/manufacturing hiring remains resilient
* the co-timed unemployment rate is only 2.4%

But it argues against treating Japan's labour market as newly overheating:

* headline vacancy ratio is flat
* the print missed consensus
* active vacancies edged lower
* active applicants increased
* new-vacancy momentum weakened

For JPY/JGBs, this is a small dovish offset to the stronger Tokyo CPI signal released in the same window.

Macro configuration: tight vacancy stock / softer new-hiring flow / industrial hiring resilience / household-sector caution / wage channel decisive.

# D. CENTRAL BANK TAPE

Summary — Policy reaction function

The BoJ entered the window at **1.00%**, following the 30–31 July **8–1 hold** with Takata dissenting in favour of 1.25%; the next meeting is **17–18 September**. The decisive in-window development was Deputy Governor Himino's 27 August speech: the faster-normalization case moved from anonymous/committee-background pressure into an explicit center-level doctrine. Himino argued that still-accommodative conditions continued to press the accelerator, that the Bank should ease off through timely rate hikes, and that upside inflation risks now deserved greater attention. Crucially, he neutralized the main dovish data point — weak Q2 GDP — by treating it as likely technical. The late-window yen strengthening is the principal offset: it removes some FX urgency and, if sustained, could reduce imported inflation into the post-September decision horizon.

**Bank of Japan — Deputy Governor Ryozo Himino — 27 Aug 2026**
Importance: ****

Key policy signal:
Himino delivered the clearest in-window endorsement of timely further normalization. He characterized still-loose monetary conditions as continuing to stimulate the economy, argued that gradual adjustment now reduces the risk of having to tighten abruptly later, and said the Bank had entered a phase in which an upside deviation of underlying inflation above 2% deserved greater attention. He also pushed directly against the argument that weak Q2 GDP should delay normalization, describing the miss as likely driven by technical factors rather than a fundamental deterioration.

Representative quote:
“still pressing on the accelerator”

Incremental reaction function:
This materially strengthens the September hike case without constituting formal pre-commitment. The key shift is institutional: the faster-pace/overshoot-prevention logic is no longer only a dissent or anonymous Summary-of-Opinions story; a Deputy Governor has effectively adopted it. The September decision therefore becomes less dependent on proving demand overheating and more dependent on whether underlying inflation remains consistent with the Bank's projected path. The remaining uncertainty shifts toward the vote configuration and post-hike guidance.

**Bank of Japan — July Outlook Highlights document — 17 Aug 2026**
Importance: **

Key policy signal:
The document confirmed the Bank's FY26 GDP projection at **0.6%**, up from 0.5%, resolving the earlier forecast discrepancy in the live monitoring tape. This matters because the Bank itself was not operating with a recessionary baseline even as Q2 preliminary GDP disappointed.

Representative quote:
n/a — forecast document rather than policy speech.

Incremental reaction function:
The growth forecast provides institutional backing for Himino's willingness to look through the weak Q2 composition. It does not remove the domestic-demand risk, but it raises the hurdle for one soft GDP estimate to derail September normalization.

**Ministry of Finance / FX institutional overlay — late Aug / 28 Aug 2026**
Importance: **

Key policy signal:
No new intervention or escalation was identified during the window; instead, the yen firmed materially late in the period without official action. The 28 August MoF release covering the intervention period was identified in the supplied tape, but the amount relating to the late-July joint operation was not extracted and therefore remains unavailable for this frozen note.

Representative quote:
n/a.

Incremental reaction function:
The currency channel becomes two-sided. A stronger yen reduces the argument for an urgent BoJ hike to limit imported inflation, but simultaneously allows any September hike to be framed more cleanly as domestic price-stability normalization rather than FX defense. If yen appreciation were to persist materially, it would become more relevant for the **pace after September** than for the September decision itself.

Bottom line — central bank tape

**Bias: hawkish hold / September tightening strongly signaled, but not formally pre-committed.**

The reaction function has shifted. Inflation no longer needs to be visibly demand-overheating for the BoJ to justify another step: the combination of projected inflation overshoot, broader core measures, persistent price-setting and the risk of falling behind the curve is sufficient to keep normalization active. Himino also substantially lowered the policy weight placed on the weak Q2 GDP print. The main uncertainty is therefore **not whether 1.00% is still the destination**, but whether September delivers 1.25% with a clean vote and how strongly the Bank signals further moves thereafter. Continued yen appreciation and persistent weakness in consumption/capex would be the principal mechanisms for slowing the post-September cadence.

The supplied tape reports September hike pricing moving from roughly 80% toward roughly 90% after Himino, but flags those pricing figures as secondary-sourced rather than a clean OIS pull; they should therefore be treated as indicative, not as a hard mechanical anchor.

# E. SIGNAL TENSION CHECK

Main contradiction:
The BoJ's center is explicitly preparing the ground for timely further tightening just as the domestic economy remains weak in the channels that would normally validate endogenous inflation — household consumption, realized capex and hiring flows — while a stronger yen is beginning to reduce the imported-inflation impulse.

Why it matters:
The September decision increasingly rests on **overshoot prevention and inflation persistence**, not on evidence of conventional demand overheating.

Implication:
A September hike can coexist with weak domestic-demand quality; the more difficult question is whether the Bank can continue tightening beyond 1.25% if yen strength and weak household demand begin pulling inflation back toward target.

# F. 5 KEY RELEASES TO DIG INTO

**** Tokyo CPI Aug — 27 Aug

Why it matters: Core accelerated for a third month and core-core reached 2.0% despite energy/childcare policy suppression. This is the cleanest late-window confirmation that underlying inflation is broadening immediately before a BoJ communication that explicitly emphasized upside-price risk.

**** Nationwide CPI Jul — 21 Aug

Why it matters: Core-core and services inflation turned higher beneath a subsidy-suppressed conventional core measure, shifting the inflation file away from a purely imported-cost story toward broader pass-through.

**** GDP Q2 Preliminary — 17 Aug

Why it matters: The principal counterweight to the hawkish inflation and central-bank tape. Growth remained positive but was carried by external demand while consumption stalled and capex contracted sharply. Himino's subsequent decision to dismiss much of the miss as technical makes the September GDP revision unusually important for testing the Bank's reaction-function judgment.

*** S&P Global Flash PMI Aug — 21 Aug

Why it matters: Composite activity reached its strongest pace in six months, manufacturing orders/export demand accelerated to 2018-vintage highs and selling-price pressure remained near record levels. It materially weakens the case that weak Q2 GDP marks the beginning of a broad downturn.

*** Services PPI Jul — 25 Aug

Why it matters: Headline services inflation accelerated to 3.6%, but most of the marginal deterioration came from international freight. It provides the cleanest distinction between persistent domestic service-price pressure and the potentially reversible external-cost shock.

# G. RED TEAM QUESTIONS

1. Is Himino's 27 August speech genuinely a September timing signal, or does it only confirm the direction of normalization while leaving the meeting date materially more open than the market assumes?

2. Does the Q2 GDP second estimate validate Himino's “technical factors” dismissal by revising capex materially higher, or does weak realized domestic demand survive the revision?

3. How much of Tokyo core-core at 2.0% survives administered-price/base effects, and is the residual inflation breadth sufficient to justify the BoJ's overshoot-prevention framing?

4. After stripping international freight from SPPI, is domestic wage-sensitive services inflation actually accelerating or simply plateauing near 3%?

5. Does wage growth remain strong enough to validate a self-sustaining wage-price cycle when unemployment is falling partly through labour-force contraction and vacancy creation is softening?

6. If the late-August yen appreciation persists, at what point does reduced imported inflation materially weaken the case for a December follow-up hike even if September delivers 1.25%?

7. Would a September hike accompanied by a split vote or deliberately cautious Ueda guidance reveal that the market has over-read Himino's speech as evidence of a faster tightening cadence?

# H. CURRENCY MARKET-CONSENSUS SCAN

### Bottom line

**Pair convention: USD/JPY — lower USD/JPY = stronger JPY.**

The publicly visible institutional view entering the window was **medium-term constructive JPY but tactically much less clean**. MUFG's early-August forecasts put USD/JPY at 158 for Q3 and 156 for Q4 from a 31 July close of 159.23, while ING's 6 August profile was considerably more cautious near term: 160 at 1M, 158 at 3M/6M and 154 at 12M, with an explicit 155–160 range expected before mid-September. The common medium-term message was eventual yen appreciation; the disagreement was the speed and whether the US side of the rate differential would allow it to emerge immediately.

Spot did not validate the constructive thesis during the window. On 17 August USD/JPY traded around 159.06 even after weak Japanese GDP because Fed expectations were simultaneously being repriced, demonstrating how dominant the US-rate leg remained. By the 28 August London close USD/JPY was **160.03**, versus 159.23 at the July close. MUFG nevertheless kept its own Q3/Q4 forecasts at 158/156, while the consensus figures displayed in its publication moved from 160/159 at the start of August to 159/158 at the end. **Forecast expectations became modestly more JPY-constructive while spot moved in the opposite direction.**

The target sample should therefore not be described as a clean, high-conviction yen bull consensus. The medium-term asymmetry favoured JPY, but tactical conviction remained capped by carry, a wide US–Japan rate differential and the need for the Fed side of the pair to cooperate. Intervention risk and faster BoJ normalization created a ceiling risk for USD/JPY, but did not by themselves produce sustained yen appreciation.

**Public-bank Q3/Q4 range:** USD/JPY **156–160**

**Constructive JPY cluster:** USD/JPY **156–158**

**More cautious / tactical view:** USD/JPY **158–160**, with ING explicitly allowing a **155–160** near-term range before mid-September.

**Bias at window close:** **medium-term constructive JPY / tactically two-sided and carry-sensitive**. Spot at 160.03 sat above the constructive 156–158 cluster and at the upper end of the cautious public-bank range.

### What changed through the data window

At the start of the period, the July joint intervention had already established a policy asymmetry around extreme yen weakness. MUFG saw intervention risk and potential BoJ tightening as limiting further USD/JPY upside, while ING argued that faster BoJ normalization would help but could not generate a durable reversal unless the Fed side also stopped supporting the dollar.

The weak Q2 GDP release did not produce a sustained yen selloff because US rate expectations were being pushed back at the same time. Later in the window, Japan's inflation tape became more supportive of BoJ normalization, but spot still failed to move toward the constructive target cluster. The important point-in-time distinction is therefore **forecast change versus spot convergence**: MUFG's own 158/156 path remained unchanged, its displayed consensus shifted about one yen lower in USD/JPY, yet spot moved from 159.23 at the July close to 160.03 on 28 August.

Consensus strengthened only modestly; price did not confirm it.

### Main consensus drivers

**US–Japan rate differential / Fed path:**
The US side remained decisive tactically. Reuters' 17 August tape showed the yen appreciating despite soft Japanese GDP because traders were reducing expectations for Fed tightening, while ING explicitly argued that faster BoJ tightening would not be sufficient without a less supportive Fed backdrop for the dollar.

**BoJ normalization / intervention asymmetry:**
The combination of another possible BoJ hike and demonstrated official willingness to intervene around extreme yen weakness created asymmetric resistance to an uncontrolled USD/JPY rise. Immediately before the data window, former top FX diplomat Mitsuhiro Furusawa argued that additional intervention and faster BoJ hikes were plausible if yen weakness persisted.

**Imported inflation / external balance:**
A weak yen was increasingly costly because it amplified energy and import prices. That strengthened Japan's incentive to resist disorderly depreciation even though the same external shock weakened household real incomes and complicated the case for aggressive tightening.

**Carry versus policy convergence:**
The medium-term JPY thesis depended on narrowing policy differentials; the tactical obstacle remained carry. USD/JPY ending the window around 160 despite a more constructive Japan inflation story showed that BoJ normalization alone had not yet overwhelmed the rate-spread channel.

### Where consensus could be wrong

**Consensus too bullish JPY:**
If the Fed remains sufficiently hawkish to preserve a wide US–Japan rate differential while the BoJ normalizes only gradually, carry can continue dominating. Persistent USD/JPY trading around or above 160 despite additional BoJ tightening would argue that the 156–158 constructive cluster is too aggressive.

**Consensus too cautious JPY:**
A synchronized narrowing of the differential — BoJ tightening combined with weaker US rate expectations — could interact with intervention asymmetry and produce a much faster carry unwind than the gradual public-bank path assumes. ING's explicit 155 lower bound before mid-September illustrates that downside tail.

**Key falsification:**
If a material narrowing in expected US–Japan policy-rate differentials and/or an additional BoJ tightening step fails to pull USD/JPY sustainably below 160, the medium-term bullish-JPY thesis should be materially downgraded.
