### JAPAN — BOJ RATE DECISION / OUTLOOK REPORT (31 JUL)

Release Date: 31 Jul 2026 | Local Time: No fixed feed time
Importance: ****

#### 1. Reuters Data

BoJ Rate Decision: 1,00% | Prior: 1,00% | Poll: 1,00% | Surprise: 0,000%

#### 2. Professional Market Commentary

The BoJ held the policy rate at 1.00% by an 8–1 vote.

Hajime Takata dissented in favour of an immediate increase to 1.25%.

The substantive event was the inflation outlook rather than the mechanical hold.

Key elements:

* underlying inflation projected to strengthen further
* core inflation expected to move clearly above 2% during the second half of FY2026
* wage increases increasingly passed into selling prices
* higher crude oil prices remain an inflation driver
* yen depreciation adds to import costs
* the BoJ explicitly recognised upside risk that underlying inflation itself could exceed target
* Takata’s dissent showed a live case for faster normalisation

The institutional debate centred on timing rather than direction.

Capital Economics retained a materially hawkish terminal-rate view; Sompo Institute Plus saw increased probability of an earlier move; SMBC emphasised yen depreciation as a potential catalyst for accelerated tightening. Nomura provided the principal counterweight, stressing political constraints and limited breadth of hawkish support on the board.

#### 3. Comment — Economist Layer

This is a meaningful escalation of the BoJ reaction function.

Previously, the main argument for higher rates could still be framed as:

weak yen + imported inflation + wage gains → gradual normalisation.

The July Outlook Report adds something stronger:

underlying inflation itself may move above target.

That matters because it shifts the internal policy debate from merely withdrawing extraordinary accommodation toward actively preventing an inflation overshoot.

The inflation forecast nevertheless contains two separate buckets:

**Potentially reversible**

* oil
* international freight
* yen-import-price shock

**Potentially persistent**

* wage-to-price passthrough
* corporate pricing behaviour
* inflation expectations
* underlying CPI above 2%

The policy question is therefore not whether inflation is high; it is how much of the expected overshoot remains after the external shock normalises.

State transition: hawkish escalation within the normalisation cycle.

Context from prior regional live file:
The BoJ had already raised the policy rate to 1.00% in June as wholesale inflation and yen-based import costs accelerated sharply. The prior file showed CGPI at 7.1%, import prices near 30% and a market increasingly focused on another hike. July moves the central bank’s own forecast framework closer to that market interpretation.

#### 4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Direction is clearly toward further tightening; timing remains conditional.

The reaction function now has three competing forces:

* inflation and pricing behaviour → earlier hike
* weak yen / FX intervention pressure → earlier hike
* household demand and political constraints → slower hike

For JPY, this is structurally supportive because the BoJ is no longer merely defending a one-off imported inflation spike.

However, the cleaner trade is conditional on domestic demand not deteriorating rapidly enough to challenge the forecast.

---

### JAPAN — HOUSING STARTS (JUNE)

Release Date: 31 Jul 2026 | Local Time: 07:00
Importance: **

#### 1. Reuters Data

Housing Starts YY: 18,6% | Prior: 33,9% | Poll: 12,8% | Surprise: 5,80%

#### 2. Professional Market Commentary

Housing starts exceeded expectations but slowed sharply from May.

Major segments remained positive:

* owner-occupied housing
* rental housing
* built-for-sale housing

However:

* growth rates decelerated substantially
* two-by-four construction contracted
* the y/y comparison remained heavily flattered by weak 2025 base effects

The release therefore represents continued construction resilience, but not a second acceleration.

#### 3. Comment — Economist Layer

The headline beat is real but optically strong.

May had already recorded +33.9% y/y after the building-standards disruption depressed the comparison base. June remains elevated at +18.6%, but the deceleration is consistent with that base effect gradually fading.

The weak two-by-four segment also suggests import-cost and construction-material pressure is not uniformly supportive.

State transition: continuation of a base-effect-qualified recovery.

Context from prior regional live file:
May housing starts had jumped 33.9% with broad segment strength but a large post-regulation base effect. June remains stronger than consensus but slows materially, confirming that part of the apparent boom was optical.

#### 4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Housing remains a secondary growth support rather than a core BoJ trigger.

The release mildly reduces growth downside risk, but it should carry substantially less weight than consumption, wages, inflation and industrial activity.

## MONDAY 3 AUG 2026

### JAPAN — S&P GLOBAL MANUFACTURING PMI FINAL (JULY)

Release Date: 3 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 02:30
Importance: ***

#### 1. Reuters Data

S&P Global Manufacturing PMI Final SA: 54,5 | Prior: 54,7

#### 2. Professional Market Commentary

The final PMI remained exceptionally strong despite a small downward revision from the flash estimate.

Survey internals showed:

* seventh consecutive month of expansion
* fastest output growth since early 2014
* strongest new-order growth in roughly 4½ years
* employment rising
* backlogs increasing rapidly
* purchasing activity accelerating
* exporters reporting firmer demand

But S&P Global also explicitly identified defensive stock-building.

Manufacturers reported accumulating raw materials and finished goods because of supply-chain disruption and higher conflict-related costs.

That is a critical qualification: the exceptional PMI is partly a war-stockpiling signal, not simply a final-demand boom.

#### 3. Comment — Economist Layer

The PMI confirms the industrial-production improvement but explains part of its strength.

The combined hard/soft industrial signal is:

* production: strong
* orders: strong
* backlogs: strong
* purchasing: strong
* inventories: rising defensively
* confidence: constrained by conflict uncertainty

The central analytical error would be to book the whole PMI surge as domestic or external final demand.

The stockpiling evidence indicates that supply-risk hedging is pulling production and purchasing forward.

State transition: continuation of manufacturing acceleration, with significant defensive-stockbuilding distortion.

Context from prior regional live file:
The July flash PMI had already shown manufacturing output growing at its fastest rate since 2014 and prices charged approaching survey records. The final 54.5 reading confirms that broad industrial strength while adding direct evidence that precautionary inventories are amplifying the cycle.

#### 4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

Manufacturing resilience gives the BoJ room to tighten, but its durability depends heavily on the conflict path.

A normalisation in shipping and energy risks that produces falling inventories and softer new orders would expose how much of the present boom is temporary.

The print is therefore JPY-positive at the margin, but less so than an equivalent PMI generated by clean final-demand acceleration.

## TUESDAY 4 AUG 2026

### JAPAN — MONETARY BASE (JULY)

Release Date: 4 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: *

#### 1. Reuters Data

Monetary Base YY: -13,87% | Prior: -13,71%

#### 2. Professional Market Commentary

No meaningful independent institutional debate developed around the release.

The BoJ data showed continued rapid balance-sheet contraction:

* monetary base around -13.8% y/y
* current-account balances down around 16.6%
* reserve balances down around 15.3%
* sequential annualised contraction slower than in June

The main signal is continued quantitative tightening alongside higher policy rates.

#### 3. Comment — Economist Layer

The y/y contraction deepens, but the sequential pace of runoff has slowed.

This creates a useful distinction:

* stock of reserve-based liquidity: contracting rapidly
* sequential runoff: less aggressive than June
* broad money: previously still positive y/y
* evidence of private-credit stress: absent

The correct interpretation remains normalisation of the BoJ balance sheet rather than a general monetary contraction.

State transition: continuation.

Context from prior regional live file:
June M2 and broadly defined liquidity remained positive year on year even as the monetary base contracted sharply. July therefore extends the established regime: quantitative tightening at the central-bank balance-sheet level without evidence that broad private liquidity has broken.

#### 4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

This reinforces the direction of policy normalisation but is not a meaningful standalone JPY catalyst.

The relevant risk would be a transition from reserve contraction to sustained broad-money and bank-credit weakness. That is not yet visible.

## WEDNESDAY 5 AUG 2026

### JAPAN — S&P GLOBAL SERVICES / COMPOSITE PMI FINAL (JULY)

Release Date: 5 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 02:30
Importance: ***

#### 1. Reuters Data

S&P Global Composite Output Final SA: 52,7 | Prior: 53,1
S&P Global Services PMI Final SA: 51,2 | Prior: 51,9

#### 2. Professional Market Commentary

Both final readings were below their flash estimates.

The service-sector internals were notably weaker than the composite headline:

* services growth slowed
* new business grew only marginally
* new-business momentum was the weakest in the 25-month expansion sequence
* foreign services demand contracted again
* employment growth was marginal
* backlogs fell to a 17-month low
* confidence remained historically weak

At the same time, pricing signals were exceptionally strong:

* input-cost inflation remained close to a four-year high
* firms cited Middle East disruption, staff costs and yen weakness
* selling prices rose at the second-fastest rate in the series
* composite selling-price inflation was also near a series high

Manufacturing strength kept the composite at 52.7 despite the services slowdown.

#### 3. Comment — Economist Layer

This is the clearest survey representation of Japan’s current macro tension.

**Demand side**

* services new business fading
* household-facing activity weakening
* confidence poor
* retail already weak

**Price side**

* input costs elevated
* selling prices near record pace
* upstream producer/import inflation strong
* Tokyo underlying CPI firming

The resulting configuration is stagflation-adjacent rather than a straightforward overheating cycle.

The composite headline hides the divergence because exceptionally strong manufacturing offsets weak household-facing services.

State transition: deterioration in demand quality with continued inflation escalation.

Context from prior regional live file:
The July flash survey had already shown unusually strong manufacturing alongside elevated prices charged. The final release materially lowers the services side while retaining near-record selling-price inflation, sharpening the distinction between factory resilience and domestic-demand fragility.

#### 4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

This is a difficult configuration for the BoJ.

The inflation side argues for continued tightening; the household-facing activity side argues against an aggressive cadence.

For JPY, the medium-term support from policy normalisation remains intact, but the risk shifts from “BoJ cannot hike because inflation is too low” toward “BoJ may need to hike into weakening consumption.”

That distinction increases policy-error risk and JGB volatility.

## FRIDAY 7 AUG 2026

### JAPAN — HOUSEHOLD SPENDING (JUNE)

Release Date: 7 Aug 2026 | Local Time: 01:30
Importance: ***

#### 1. Reuters Data

All Household Spending YY: -3,3% | Prior: -0,4% | Poll: 1,0% | Surprise: -4,30%
All Household Spending MM: -6,4% | Prior: 3,7% | Poll: -3,1% | Surprise: -3,30%

#### 2. Professional Market Commentary

Household spending delivered a severe downside surprise.

Key tape focus:

* real spending -3.3% y/y
* seventh consecutive annual decline
* -6.4% m/m
* widespread category weakness
* food -2.5%
* utilities -7.1%
* furniture/household goods -11.4%
* clothing -19.9%
* transport/communication -5.7%

The official explanation materially qualifies the headline.

June was affected by:

* typhoons and heavy rainfall
* cooler weather
* weaker travel and dining-out demand
* an unwind of precautionary purchases made earlier in the Middle East conflict

Most importantly, consumable-goods spending fell as households reduced stockpiling of plastic bags and wrapping products that had begun in March.

This provides direct official confirmation that some apparent spring consumption resilience represented defensive hoarding rather than recurring demand.

At the same time, real wages reportedly continued to improve, making the failure of income gains to translate into spending economically important.

#### 3. Comment — Economist Layer

The June retail warning is confirmed.

The sequential chronology is revealing:

* May retail: very strong
* May household spending: +3.7% m/m, near-flat y/y
* June retail: sharp miss
* June household spending: -6.4% m/m / -3.3% y/y
* July services new business: weakest in a 25-month expansion sequence

The consumption deterioration is therefore no longer a single-indicator phenomenon.

However, June is not a clean structural collapse.

The official stockpiling-unwind explanation changes the interpretation of both sides of the sequence:

* spring strength was overstated by defensive purchases
* June weakness is overstated by their reversal

Weather adds a second temporary drag.

The underlying message is consequently weaker than the raw -3.3% headline but materially softer than the spring data suggested.

State transition: deterioration, partly distorted by stockpiling unwind and weather.

Context from prior regional live file:
May household spending had risen 3.7% m/m and narrowed its annual contraction to only 0.4%, leading the prior tape to judge that the consumption floor was improving. The prior note explicitly warned that a renewed June annual decline beyond roughly 1.5% would challenge that interpretation. June at -3.3% decisively triggers that falsification condition.

#### 4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

This is the strongest growth-side challenge to the BoJ’s hawkish July message.

The central bank needs wage gains to generate:

wages → income → consumption → pricing power → durable 2% inflation.

June shows that the middle part of that mechanism remains unreliable.

That does not eliminate further tightening because:

* part of the weakness is temporary
* inflation pressure remains elevated
* manufacturing remains resilient
* the BoJ has explicitly identified upside underlying-inflation risks

But it argues strongly against treating the hiking cycle as unconstrained.

For JPY, the medium-term policy support remains, while near-term hike timing becomes more sensitive to July consumption and nationwide inflation confirmation.

D. CENTRAL BANK TAPE

Summary — Policy reaction function

The 31 July meeting ratified the hawkish path guidance that had been circulating before the decision. The policy rate remained at 1.00%, but the reaction function moved clearly tighter: Takata voted for 1.25%, the Outlook Report projected CPI “clearly above” 2% from H2 FY26, and Ueda explicitly kept a September move live while describing financial conditions as accommodative and inflation risks as meaningfully skewed upward. No dovish dissent materialized. In parallel, the 30 July MoF yen-buying operation demonstrated that FX defence is now an active policy channel rather than merely verbal optionality. The combined regime is therefore further BoJ normalisation plus active yen defence, with household-demand weakness — rather than insufficient inflation — becoming the principal brake on cadence.

BoJ Monetary Policy Meeting Statement — 31 Jul
Importance: ****

Key policy signal:
The BoJ held at 1.00% by an 8–1 vote, but the hold was hawkish in composition. Underlying inflation was described as approaching 2%, upside deviation risk remained live, and the only dissent was for tighter policy. Sato’s debut vote and Asada’s vote both remained with the majority, so no dovish counter-dissent emerged.

Representative quote:
“Underlying inflation approaching 2%.”

Incremental reaction function:
The committee distribution shifted right relative to June. The relevant debate is increasingly the timing of the next hike rather than whether normalisation continues. The absence of a dovish dissent weakens the prior two-dissenter / slower-cadence thesis, although Sato remains untested in an actual majority hike vote.

BoJ Outlook Report — 31 Jul
Importance: ****

Key policy signal:
The central projection strengthened materially, with CPI expected to accelerate to a level clearly above 2% from H2 FY26 before moving back toward roughly 2% later in the projection period. The report also reduced the April tail-risk framing around significant growth downside and significant price upside.

Representative quote:
“Clearly above 2%.”

Incremental reaction function:
The Bank is now explicitly looking through the current sub-2% inflation prints toward pipeline inflation and pass-through. Monthly CPI therefore becomes a direct test of the BoJ’s own projection. A confirming acceleration supports an earlier hike; repeated misses would force the Bank to defend a hawkish path against its own realised data.

Governor Kazuo Ueda — 31 Jul
Importance: ****

Key policy signal:
Ueda personally owned the hawkish message at the post-meeting presser. He kept a hike as soon as September in play, stressed meaningful upside inflation risks and continued to describe financial conditions as accommodative.

Representative quote:
“Meaningful upside risks.”

Incremental reaction function:
The policy signal moved from sourced guidance to on-record Governor communication. September becomes a genuine decision window rather than a market extrapolation, with the bar defined by confirmation of the inflation pipeline and the absence of a sufficiently severe domestic-demand deterioration.

Hajime Takata — 31 Jul
Importance: ****

Key policy signal:
Takata proposed an immediate increase to 1.25%, arguing that the inflation environment had shifted into a new phase and required a more nimble response to overseas-driven upside price risks.

Representative quote:
“Shifted to a new phase.”

Incremental reaction function:
The hawkish pole is now expressing itself through the vote rather than speeches alone. Takata establishes 1.25% as an immediately defensible policy setting and pulls the center’s timing debate forward. The next question is when the majority joins him, not whether his direction of travel is institutionally admissible.

MoF / FX Intervention — 30 Jul
Importance: ****

Key policy signal:
MoF intervened to buy yen after USDJPY had reached roughly 163.99, driving the pair toward 157.96. The scout identifies a US rate check alongside the operation with Medium-High confidence on the US-role characterization. The operation was the first confirmed intervention since May and occurred immediately before the BoJ decision.

Representative quote:
“Zone around 162–165.”

Incremental reaction function:
The FX channel is now demonstrated rather than hypothetical. Intervention can buy the BoJ time and reduce the need to use the policy rate solely as an emergency yen-defence instrument, but the sequencing also creates pressure for monetary-policy follow-through. A renewed break toward the demonstrated intervention zone would raise the probability of additional MoF action and strengthen the argument for an earlier hike.

Bottom line — central bank tape

The July meeting is a hawkish ratification event, not a neutral hold. The vote, the Outlook and Ueda’s presser all move the reaction function toward another hike, while the FX intervention adds a second institutional support channel for JPY. The unresolved constraint is increasingly domestic demand: weak retail, household spending and services activity argue against treating September as mechanically guaranteed. The direction is tighter; the timing depends on whether realised CPI validates the projected H2 inflation acceleration without a deeper consumption break.

E. SIGNAL TENSION CHECK

Main contradiction: Japan has strong manufacturing/output momentum and persistent inflation passthrough, while household-facing demand is weakening sharply.

Why it matters: The BoJ is increasingly being asked to normalise policy because inflation risks are rising even though the wage-to-consumption mechanism needed for a durable domestic inflation cycle remains incomplete.

Implication: The hiking direction remains intact, but the optimal cadence is increasingly constrained by weak consumption and a growing risk of tightening into a stagflation-adjacent demand slowdown.

F. 5 KEY RELEASES TO DIG INTO

**** **BoJ Rate Decision / Outlook Report (31 Jul)**
Why it matters: The hold itself was expected, but the first explicit acknowledgement that underlying inflation could overshoot 2%, together with Takata’s dissent for 1.25%, materially shifts the reaction function toward faster normalisation. The central question is how much survives if oil, freight and yen-import pressure normalise.

**** **Tokyo CPI (31 Jul)**
Why it matters: Verified underlying inflation accelerated despite fuel subsidies suppressing the energy bucket. The remaining weakness in 1.1% services inflation is the critical test of whether Japan is moving from imported inflation toward a self-sustaining wage-price cycle.

*** **Household Spending (7 Aug)**
Why it matters: The -3.3% y/y / -6.4% m/m collapse confirms the retail/services demand crack, but official evidence that prior stockpiling unwound in June means both the earlier strength and current weakness were distorted. This is the key growth-side challenge to the BoJ path.

G. RED TEAM QUESTIONS

1. Does the collapse in June household spending persist after the weather and stockpiling-unwind effects disappear, or is the apparent consumption break being overstated by temporary distortions?

2. Does nationwide CPI validate the Tokyo re-acceleration and the BoJ’s forecast of inflation moving clearly above 2%, particularly through wage-sensitive services rather than imported goods and energy?

3. If manufacturing inventories unwind as Middle East supply risks normalise, do production, orders and backlogs remain strong enough to preserve the industrial-resilience leg of the regime?

4. Is Takata’s 1.25% dissent an early indication of a broader committee shift toward September, or does the majority remain materially more gradual once weak retail, household spending and services activity are incorporated?

5. Can the BoJ tighten into weakening household demand without breaking the wage → income → consumption → pricing-power transmission mechanism on which durable domestic inflation ultimately depends?

6. Does FX intervention merely buy temporary time, or does coordination between MoF intervention and a faster BoJ path create a durable enough change in the USD/JPY reaction function to prevent another move toward the intervention zone?

H. CURRENCY MARKET-CONSENSUS SCAN

### Bottom line

**Pair convention: USD/JPY — lower USD/JPY means a stronger yen.**

At the beginning of the window, the public institutional view remained structurally constructive JPY but tactically sceptical. ING’s 28 July BoJ preview expected the Bank to hold at 1.00%, doubted that a modest hawkish shift alone would materially strengthen the yen and saw USD/JPY remaining bid near 163–164, with an outside risk of 165 if Ueda disappointed. Its year-end forecast remained 158. In other words, the prevailing thesis was still **eventual yen recovery, but no clean near-term catalyst while US rates, energy and the wide carry differential dominated**.

The 30–31 July intervention/BoJ sequence materially changed the tactical asymmetry. MUFG described the move as making the risk-reward of selling JPY much less attractive and maintained a September BoJ hike call. Its August forecast profile put USD/JPY at **158 in Q3, 156 in Q4, 154 in Q1 2027 and 152 in Q2 2027**, versus spot 159.23 at the 31 July close. MUFG explicitly argued that joint intervention, a potentially faster BoJ cadence and over-priced Fed tightening should provide additional yen support.

The broader strategist community was more cautious than the intervention-induced price action initially suggested. The Reuters survey conducted 31 July–5 August showed median USD/JPY forecasts of **159 in three months, 157 in six months and 154 in twelve months**. Almost 95% of respondents judged that intervention by itself would not sustainably reverse yen weakness; nearly all of those respondents said a lasting effect required BoJ tightening. Thus the market consensus at the end of the window was **medium-term constructive JPY, but still explicitly conditional on fundamentals rather than official flow alone**.

By 3 August, ING likewise argued that coordinated intervention was significant but primarily bought time: a durable turn required softer US data, no renewed Fed tightening and/or stronger Japanese policy support. ING retained a **158 year-end / 152 end-2027** USD/JPY profile. MUFG’s early-August work similarly judged that the intervention had increased near-term downside risk in USD/JPY and forecast faster BoJ hikes, including September.

**Public-bank Q4 / year-end range:** USD/JPY **156–158** — MUFG 156; ING 158.

**Constructive JPY cluster:** USD/JPY **154–158** across the visible 6M/12M Reuters medians and public bank targets; Reuters’ 12M median was 154.

**More cautious / tactical view:** pre-intervention USD/JPY **163–165** remained plausible under a hawkish Fed / insufficiently hawkish BoJ configuration; after intervention, the Reuters 3M median was still **159**, indicating that strategists did not extrapolate the initial yen surge into a one-way trend.

**Bias at window close:** **JPY constructive medium term, tactically two-sided after intervention**. The intervention and hawkish BoJ meeting improved the downside asymmetry in USD/JPY, but the public forecast cluster still expected only gradual appreciation rather than an immediate structural break.

### What changed through the data window

The window began with USD/JPY near the 163–164 area and institutional commentary still focused on the inability of gradual BoJ tightening to overcome the US–Japan yield differential, high energy costs and strong-dollar backdrop. ING expected the pair to remain supported into the BoJ meeting and saw 165 as possible if Ueda failed to signal faster normalisation.

The 30 July intervention was the principal regime-changing market event. USD/JPY fell by roughly five big figures, and MUFG subsequently argued that the intervention — particularly the Japan-US coordination element — changed the risk-reward equation around outright yen shorts. Its August outlook retained a September BoJ hike and shifted the tactical balance toward further USD/JPY downside rather than a rapid return to the pre-intervention highs.

The 31 July BoJ decision then supplied partial fundamental validation. The Bank held at 1.00%, but the hawkish dissent, stronger inflation outlook and Ueda’s openness to faster tightening increased speculation about an earlier next hike. MUFG maintained a September move in its baseline and forecast USD/JPY at 158 in Q3 and 156 in Q4.

However, the consensus did **not** convert into an aggressive bullish-JPY call. The 31 July–5 August Reuters poll still placed USD/JPY at 159 in three months and explicitly found overwhelming agreement that intervention alone was insufficient. This is the critical distinction: **the forecast path became more credible because the policy mix improved, but strategists continued to require a change in rate differentials or other fundamentals for durable appreciation**.

By 6–7 August, USD/JPY had stabilised well below the pre-intervention level but was rebounding around the 158 area. MUFG noted that Japanese retail traders had held record USD/JPY shorts before the intervention and were likely buyers into the decline, helping to limit the move. On 7 August the pair moved back above roughly 158 as US yields rose, underscoring that the rates channel remained dominant despite the new intervention floor.

### Main consensus drivers

**BoJ cadence / rate differential:**
The principal constructive-JPY condition became a faster normalisation cycle. MUFG maintained a September hike call, while Reuters respondents overwhelmingly argued that intervention would need accompanying BoJ rate increases to generate lasting appreciation.

**US rates / Fed reaction function:**
The yen remained highly dependent on the US side of the pair. ING explicitly argued that intervention would only produce a durable trend change if US data softened enough to prevent further Fed tightening; MUFG likewise linked the 7 August USD/JPY rebound above 158 to higher US yields.

**Intervention credibility / coordination:**
Joint or coordinated intervention changed the tactical distribution because market participants could no longer assume that Japan was acting alone. MUFG viewed US involvement as materially improving the impact of the operation and reducing the attractiveness of rebuilding large USD/JPY longs immediately.

**Energy / domestic flow structure:**
High energy prices continued to weaken Japan’s terms of trade and constrain JPY appreciation, while persistent household investment abroad and domestic flow behaviour remained structural headwinds. ING explicitly identified lower energy costs or stronger repatriation as conditions for a more durable yen recovery.

### Where consensus could be wrong

**Consensus too bullish JPY:**
If weak household demand prevents the BoJ from following through with faster tightening, while US yields remain high and energy prices stay elevated, intervention gains can fade and USD/JPY can return toward the pre-intervention 163–165 zone. The Reuters survey’s overwhelming view that intervention alone was insufficient directly captures this risk.

**Consensus too cautious JPY:**
If Ueda’s faster-path optionality converts into a September hike, US rate expectations soften and official intervention has permanently reduced willingness to rebuild yen shorts, USD/JPY could reach the 154–158 forecast cluster faster than the gradual consensus path implies. MUFG’s 156 Q4 forecast and explicit September hike call represent the constructive version of this scenario.

**Key falsification:**
A durable JPY turn requires **policy and rate differentials to validate the intervention signal**. A faster BoJ path together with softer US yields would validate the constructive consensus; a consumption-induced BoJ delay combined with resilient US yields would demonstrate that intervention merely produced a temporary positioning reset.

<<JPY_WEEK_BEGIN>>
DATA WINDOW: 29 June 2026 to 24 July 2026

A. JPY WEEK — MACRO SYNTHESIS

Japan moved through the period into a **stronger but increasingly two-speed normalization regime**. Early-window data materially strengthened the BoJ case: retail sales, household spending, record employment, the Q2 Tankan and manufacturing surveys showed resilient domestic and corporate activity, while Tankan inflation expectations, CGPI at 7.1% y/y and yen-basis import prices near 30% exposed a powerful weak-yen inflation pipeline. Broad money continued to expand despite balance-sheet normalization, limiting evidence that tighter policy was generating acute private-liquidity stress.

The later sequence introduced meaningful counterweights without reversing that regime. Core machinery orders collapsed, services confidence weakened, the trade deficit widened as energy imports accelerated, chain-store sales contracted and core-core inflation remained softer than headline measures. At the same time, July manufacturing stayed exceptionally strong: AI-linked demand, exports, precautionary stockbuilding and near-record corporate selling-price inflation reinforced the industrial and pricing-power side of the economy. The central-bank tape strengthens the policy asymmetry: the 22 July twin Bloomberg/Reuters sourced stories signalled openness to a **faster-than-consensus normalization cadence**, explicitly tying yen weakness to upside inflation risk, while the absence of on-record BoJ speeches preserved optionality. MoF verbal intervention escalated as USDJPY moved through 163 toward 164 without confirmed operations, increasing the burden on the rate and guidance channel. The cumulative state is therefore **industrial/AI-led expansion plus imported and corporate-price inflation, offset by fragile household, services and capex channels; hawkish drift in the BoJ path, but a near-term hold remains compatible with the data**.

B. JPY MACRO SIGNAL SCOREBOARD

Growth: +
Supporting evidence:
Retail sales rose 5.3% y/y, household spending beat sharply, the Q2 Tankan showed broad corporate resilience and large-firm capex plans of 11.5%, while June and July PMIs confirmed strong manufacturing activity driven by AI, technology and export demand. Industrial production also posted consecutive gains and June exports accelerated to 19.3% y/y. The upgrade is qualified by a 12.4% collapse in core machinery orders, weaker chain-store sales and services growth lagging manufacturing.

Labor: ~
Supporting evidence:
Unemployment remained at 2.5% alongside record employment and rising participation, preserving the tight aggregate labour-market regime. However, the jobs-to-applicants ratio slipped to 1.17, new vacancies weakened materially and employment growth was concentrated partly in non-regular work. The stock of labour tightness remains supportive, but forward hiring evidence no longer points to renewed acceleration.

Inflation: -
Supporting evidence:
The inflation pipeline deteriorated materially. CGPI accelerated to 7.1% y/y, yen-basis import prices rose 29.7%, Tankan inflation expectations increased and July manufacturing selling-price inflation approached survey records. Nationwide CPI subsequently rose to 1.7% and core CPI to 1.6%, although softer core-core inflation and the large imported-energy component prevent a clean broad domestic-overheating conclusion.

Financial Conditions: ~
Supporting evidence:
BoJ balance-sheet normalization is reducing reserve-based liquidity, but M2 still grew 2.2% y/y and broadly defined liquidity 4.5%, providing no evidence of acute monetary-system stress. At the same time, yen weakness is materially tightening real household and corporate conditions through imported energy and input costs, while higher financing costs contributed to weaker services sentiment and investment uncertainty.

Policy Constraint: -
Supporting evidence:
The reaction function shifted more clearly hawkish over the period. The Tankan, strong manufacturing surveys, resilient household spending, elevated corporate inflation expectations and the CGPI/import-price shock already supported continued normalization. The 22 July Reuters and Bloomberg sourced stories added an explicit cadence signal: officials were described as open to moving faster than the market’s roughly six-month/twice-yearly assumption, with yen weakness treated as an upside-inflation mechanism and FY26 growth potentially revised higher. Against that, June core CPI at 1.6%, softer core-core inflation, weak machinery orders and fragile household/services evidence still provide cover for patience. MoF’s escalating rhetoric without confirmed intervention increases the burden on BoJ guidance. The resulting configuration is **hawkish hold / faster-path optionality**, not an immediate mechanically forced hike.

C. FULL RELEASE ARCHIVE

MONDAY 29 JUN 2026

JAPAN — RETAIL SALES / LARGE-SCALE RETAIL SALES (MAY)

Release Date: 29 Jun 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: ***

1. Reuters Data

Retail Sales YY: 5,3% | Prior: 2,8% | Poll: 3,2% | Surprise: +2,10%
Large-Scale Retail Sales YY: 5% | Prior: 2%

2. Professional Market Commentary

The professional tape framed the release as a broad consumption upside surprise, with retail sales recording their strongest annual rise since November 2023 and beating the full forecast range.

Key tape focus:

* third consecutive monthly increase
* sharp upward revision to April
* strength in autos, machinery and department stores
* continued tourist-spending support
* appliance purchases boosted by regulatory pull-forward
* weakness in non-store retail, fuel and clothing

The print strengthens the domestic-demand side of the BoJ normalisation case, but the appliance surge and nominal-data basis limit the clean read-through to real consumption. ([Ministère de l'Économie][1])

3. Comment — Economist Layer

The primary signal is a meaningful consumption improvement, but the composition contains temporary support.

Autos and machinery provide better demand-quality evidence than a food- or subsidy-only increase. Department-store gains also indicate continued tourism support. However, appliance sales were materially boosted by purchases ahead of new efficiency standards, while the figures are not adjusted for inflation.

The signal hierarchy is therefore:

* broad headline and monthly momentum: strong
* discretionary durable categories: constructive
* appliance component: partly front-loaded
* real-volume interpretation: less certain
* policy relevance: supportive, but secondary to wages and underlying inflation

State transition: improvement.

Context from prior regional live file:
April retail sales had already exceeded expectations and supported the resilience side of Japan’s mixed activity profile. May materially extends that sequence, with stronger breadth and a third consecutive monthly gain. Household demand is no longer the obvious weak link in the near-term BoJ normalisation case. ([GitHub][2])

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The release gives the BoJ more confidence that tighter policy would not immediately break consumption.

For JPY, the domestic-demand signal is supportive because it reduces the policy-divergence argument against the currency. The reaction should nevertheless remain conditional: June sales must avoid a reversal in autos, machinery and appliances once regulatory pull-forward fades.

The next verification tests are household spending, real wages and whether retail volumes remain firm after the appliance distortion normalises.

TUESDAY 30 JUN 2026

JAPAN — LABOUR FORCE SURVEY (MAY)

Release Date: 30 Jun 2026 | Local Time: 01:30
Importance: **

1. Reuters Data

Unemployment Rate: 2,5% | Prior: 2,5% | Poll: 2,5% | Surprise: 0,00%

2. Professional Market Commentary

The headline was stable and received limited standalone market attention.

The official detail showed:

* employment at a record level
* labour-force participation continuing to rise
* the population outside the labour force at a record low
* employment increasing for a fourth consecutive year-on-year period
* non-regular employment accounting for much of the annual increase

The professional interpretation remains “tight at the aggregate level,” with more forward-looking hiring indicators needed to identify any genuine turning point. ([stat.go.jp][4])

3. Comment — Economist Layer

The full-employment regime remains intact.

Stable unemployment alongside higher participation and record employment is a stronger result than a flat unemployment rate viewed in isolation. Labour supply is continuing to expand without causing a rise in unemployment.

The weaker-quality element is the concentration of annual employment growth in non-regular work. That matters for wage transmission because non-regular jobs may provide less durable bargaining power than full-time permanent employment.

Signal hierarchy:

* unemployment rate: stable
* employment level: strong
* participation: strong
* employment quality: mixed
* inflation transmission: still conditional on wages

State transition: continuation.

Context from prior regional live file:
Japan’s previous labour and wage evidence had maintained a tight-market narrative but had not produced a decisive acceleration in household inflation. May preserves labour-market tightness and removes an immediate growth objection to further BoJ normalisation. The key unresolved issue remains whether tight employment converts into durable wage and services-price pressure.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The release is modestly supportive of further BoJ tightening, but it is not a standalone catalyst.

JPY and JGB implications depend on whether wage settlements, scheduled earnings and services inflation validate the strong employment base. A rise in unemployment above 2.7% or a stall in participation would be needed to create a clearer dovish labour signal.

JAPAN — JOBS-TO-APPLICANTS RATIO (MAY)

Release Date: 30 Jun 2026 | Local Time: 01:30
Importance: **

1. Reuters Data

Jobs/Applicants Ratio: 1,17 | Prior: 1,18 | Poll: 1,18 | Surprise: −0,010

2. Professional Market Commentary

The ratio slipped to a five-month low as growth in active applicants exceeded growth in available openings.

Official and professional attention centred on:

* a continued ratio above 1.0
* applicants increasing faster than openings
* job-switching for improved conditions
* greater labour-force participation by older workers
* a pronounced annual decline in new vacancies
* weakness in entertainment, accommodation, food services and construction

The tape therefore distinguishes between a still-tight labour-market stock and a softer vacancy-flow signal.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

The jobs ratio is the forward-softening counterpart to the stable unemployment rate.

The level still indicates more openings than applicants, but the directional signal is weaker. The 8.9% annual fall in new openings is particularly relevant because vacancy creation normally turns before employment and unemployment.

The internal hierarchy is:

* jobs ratio level: tight
* monthly direction: softer
* new vacancy flow: materially weaker
* labour supply: increasing
* sector breadth: consumer-facing softness

State transition: stabilization with a soft edge.

Context from prior regional live file:
Japan’s labour regime had remained tight enough to support the BoJ’s normalisation bias, while forward indicators were less uniform. May sharpens that divergence: realised employment is strong, but vacancy creation is cooling. The labour file remains supportive, though no longer unambiguously accelerating.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The release argues against extrapolating the record employment level into an increasingly tight labour market.

For the BoJ, persistent vacancy weakness would slow the expected wage-price transmission mechanism even if unemployment remains low. A ratio returning above 1.19 with new openings turning positive year on year would neutralise the concern.

JAPAN — INDUSTRIAL PRODUCTION PRELIMINARY (MAY)

Release Date: 30 Jun 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: ***

1. Reuters Data

Industrial Output Preliminary MM SA: 0,5% | Prior: 0,5% | Poll: 1,1% | Surprise: −0,60%

2. Professional Market Commentary

The tape viewed the print as a consensus miss but not an industrial reversal.

Key points:

* second consecutive monthly production increase
* shipments also rising
* inventories declining
* transport equipment and chemicals leading the gain
* annual production still contracting
* manufacturers projecting a strong June increase and flat July
* energy-supply concerns appearing contained so far

Professional commentary gave more weight to the consecutive monthly gains and firm Q2 trajectory than to the miss versus the poll. METI characterised industrial production as fluctuating indecisively and reported an expected June increase followed by flat July output. ([Ministère de l'Économie][5])

3. Comment — Economist Layer

The hard-data signal is gradual recovery rather than acceleration.

The monthly increase confirms that March’s industrial weakness did not persist, while falling inventories reduce the risk that production is merely accumulating unsold output. However, the negative annual rate and repeated tendency for realised production to underperform forecast surveys limit confidence.

Transport-equipment strength is constructive, but the June forecast must be treated cautiously given the supply shock and recent forecast errors.

State transition: continuation.

Context from prior regional live file:
The prior Japan production sequence had shifted from a sharp March miss to an April gain, leaving the industrial sector in a fragile recovery rather than a clean expansion. May adds a second positive month but again falls short of expectations. The trajectory is improving, while the strength and durability of the recovery remain unproven. ([GitHub][2])

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The print prevents an immediate growth-based dovish shift in the BoJ reaction function.

It is not strong enough to generate an aggressive hawkish repricing because annual output remains lower and forecast optimism has not yet been realised. June production is the decisive test: a material undershoot of the projected 3.7% increase, particularly with transport equipment reversing, would weaken the resilience narrative.

JAPAN — HOUSING STARTS (MAY)

Release Date: 30 Jun 2026 | Local Time: 07:00
Importance: **

1. Reuters Data

Housing Starts YY: 33,9% | Prior: 11,4% | Poll: 31,8% | Surprise: +2,10%

2. Professional Market Commentary

The headline exceeded expectations and marked the second consecutive annual increase.

Strength was broad across:

* owner-occupied housing
* rental construction
* built-for-sale housing
* two-by-four construction

The tape nevertheless emphasised a favourable comparison against the post-April 2025 regulatory disruption and earlier pull-forward activity. The annual growth rate therefore overstates the clean underlying construction acceleration.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

This is an improvement signal with a large base-effect qualification.

The breadth of the increase is constructive, particularly because owner-occupied, rental and built-for-sale categories all expanded. However, the comparison period was depressed by the introduction of new building standards, making the annual rate mechanically strong.

The release is best interpreted as housing activity normalising after regulatory distortion rather than entering a 30%-plus structural boom.

State transition: improvement, base-effect qualified.

Context from prior regional live file:
March housing starts had collapsed under regulatory timing and base effects rather than a conventional demand shock. May’s surge represents the mirror image of that distortion. The housing signal is improving, but the level must be assessed after comparison effects normalise. ([GitHub][2])

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The release is marginally BoJ-hawkish through construction resilience but remains secondary to inflation, wages and Tankan evidence.

A June annual rate below roughly 10% would confirm that May was largely a comparison effect. Persistent double-digit growth after normalisation would provide more credible evidence of domestic-demand strength.

WEDNESDAY 1 JUL 2026

JAPAN — TANKAN SURVEY (Q2)

Release Date: 1 Jul 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: ****

1. Reuters Data

Big Manufacturing Index: 22 | Prior: 17 | Poll: 16 | Surprise: +6,0
Big Manufacturing Outlook: 17 | Prior: 14 | Poll: 13 | Surprise: +4,0
Big Non-Manufacturing Index: 37 | Prior: 36 | Poll: 35 | Surprise: +2,0
Big Non-Manufacturing Outlook: 28 | Prior: 29 | Poll: 30 | Surprise: −2,0
All Big Capex Estimate: 11,5% | Prior: 3,3% | Poll: 10,5% | Surprise: +1,00%
Small Manufacturing Index: 9 | Prior: 7 | Poll: 4 | Surprise: +5,0
Small Manufacturing Outlook: 2 | Prior: 4 | Poll: 1 | Surprise: +1,0
Small Non-Manufacturing Index: 15 | Prior: 16 | Poll: 14 | Surprise: +1,0
Small Non-Manufacturing Outlook: 8 | Prior: 8 | Poll: 9 | Surprise: −1,0
All Small Capex Estimate: −8,3% | Prior: −8,1% | Poll: −4,5% | Surprise: −3,80%

2. Professional Market Commentary

The Tankan materially exceeded expectations and overturned the pre-release consensus for conflict-driven deterioration.

Key tape focus:

* large-manufacturer sentiment at its highest since March 2018
* fifth consecutive quarterly improvement
* large non-manufacturers at a multi-decade high
* small manufacturers also beating sharply
* large-firm capex plans remaining strong despite expected profit contraction
* corporate inflation expectations rising across one-, three- and five-year horizons
* small-firm capex remaining deeply negative
* corporate USD/JPY assumptions materially stronger than the prevailing spot exchange rate

The official survey confirms broad corporate resilience but also exposes a clear large-versus-small-firm divide. ([Banque du Japon][13])

3. Comment — Economist Layer

This is the week’s strongest policy-relevant DM release.

The primary signal is not simply better corporate sentiment. It is the conjunction of:

* sustained manufacturing improvement
* record non-manufacturing conditions
* double-digit large-firm capex plans
* rising medium-term inflation expectations
* resilience despite the energy and geopolitical shock

That combination directly supports the BoJ’s argument that inflation is becoming more embedded and that firms can continue investing through margin pressure.

The internal weakness is concentrated in smaller firms. Their negative capex plans and weaker outlooks show that the cost shock is not being absorbed evenly. The assumed exchange rate also understates actual yen weakness, implying either future earnings upgrades for exporters or greater import-cost stress for unhedged firms.

State transition: improvement.

Context from prior regional live file:
The Q1 Tankan had already shown resilient large firms alongside negative small-firm capex, while the June Reuters Tankan pointed to improving manufacturing sentiment. Q2 strengthens the large-firm and inflation-expectation signals substantially without resolving the SME divide. Japan’s corporate regime shifts from resilient-but-bifurcated to strongly resilient with a more explicit inflation-entrenchment risk. ([GitHub][2])

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The Tankan increases the probability that the BoJ continues normalisation if incoming CPI and wage data remain consistent.

It is supportive for JPY through rates and domestic resilience, although the currency’s extreme weakness and intervention risk complicate the immediate transmission. The stronger reaction-function read is higher confidence in an additional hike, not an unconditional acceleration of the cycle.

The main falsification test is the September Tankan: large-manufacturer conditions falling toward the projected 17, inflation expectations dropping below 2.5%, or SME weakness spreading into large-firm capex would challenge the hawkish interpretation.

JAPAN — S&P GLOBAL MANUFACTURING PMI FINAL (JUNE)

Release Date: 1 Jul 2026 | Local Time: 02:30
Importance: ***

1. Reuters Data

Manufacturing PMI Final SA: 54,8 | Prior: 54,9

2. Professional Market Commentary

The final PMI confirmed a strong manufacturing quarter.

The survey showed:

* the strongest quarterly performance in more than 12 years
* new orders growing at their fastest rate since January 2022
* employment growth near an eight-year high
* rapid accumulation of backlogs
* continued strength in AI, technology and semiconductor demand
* elevated input and output inflation
* substantial client inventory-building against price and supply risks

The compiler explicitly warned that part of the strength was driven by stockpiling and may not extend into the second half. ([pmi.spglobal.com][14])

3. Comment — Economist Layer

The expansion is real, but its demand quality is mixed.

Strong employment and backlogs indicate genuine capacity pressure. AI and semiconductor demand provide an identifiable final-demand channel. However, the fastest new-order growth partly reflects precautionary buying and inventory accumulation ahead of potential disruption.

The correct hierarchy is:

* headline expansion: strong
* employment and backlogs: constructive
* AI/semiconductor demand: genuine positive
* general new orders: partly front-loaded
* inflation pressure: elevated
* H2 sustainability: uncertain

State transition: continuation, front-loading qualified.

Context from prior regional live file:
Japan manufacturing had already recovered from the March industrial shock, with April and May production stabilising and the earlier PMI sequence improving. June extends the survey expansion but introduces a clearer stockpiling distortion. The manufacturing regime is strong in the short term, while the durability of end-demand remains the key unresolved question.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The PMI reinforces the Tankan’s near-term BoJ-hawkish message but should receive less policy weight because of its inventory component.

The relevant test is whether July new orders and output hold after stock-building slows. A rapid fall in orders with inventories remaining elevated would convert the current strength into a classic front-loading false dawn.

TUESDAY 7 JUL 2026

JAPAN — HOUSEHOLD SPENDING (MAY)

Release Date: 7 Jul 2026 | Local Time: 01:30
Importance: ***

1. Reuters Data

All Household Spending YY: −0,4% | Prior: −0,5% | Poll: −2,5% | Surprise: +2,10%
All Household Spending MM: 3,7% | Prior: 1,6% | Poll: 1,4% | Surprise: +2,30%

2. Professional Market Commentary

The report materially exceeded expectations and corroborated the earlier retail-sales improvement.

Key tape focus:

* the strongest monthly spending increase in the recent sequence
* the mildest annual contraction in six months
* furniture and household goods rising sharply
* education spending rebounding
* clothing, healthcare and food spending increasing
* transport and communication remaining a major drag
* household nominal income increasing only modestly
* wage growth falling slightly short of expectations

The professional interpretation was that the consumption floor is holding despite continued pressure on real household income.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

The release materially improves the Japan household-demand signal, but it does not yet establish a full real-income recovery.

The 3.7% monthly increase and large consensus beat are too strong to dismiss as noise. The improvement is also reasonably broad across household goods, education, clothing, healthcare and food.

The composition nonetheless contains volatility:

* education reversed an unusually sharp previous decline
* durable replacement and subsidy effects likely supported household goods
* transport and communications remained very weak
* nominal income growth was insufficient to remove the real-income squeeze

The macro significance comes from cross-release confirmation. Retail sales, Tankan, services PMI and household spending now point in the same direction: domestic consumption is proving more resilient than expected.

State transition: improvement.

Context from prior regional live file:
February and March household spending had reinforced the weak-consumption side of Japan’s macro file, even as retail indicators offered partial offsets. May narrows the annual contraction to almost zero and delivers a strong sequential rise. The income-to-spending channel is no longer clearly broken, though negative real-income pressure remains a constraint. ([GitHub][1])

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The release supports further BoJ normalisation by strengthening the household-demand condition.

The policy read remains conditional on wages. If nominal earnings continue to lag inflation, spending resilience may depend on subsidies, savings drawdown or temporary durable purchases rather than a self-sustaining wage-consumption cycle.

A renewed annual decline beyond 1.5% in June would raise the probability that May reflected temporary support.

## WEDNESDAY 8 JUL 2026

### JAPAN — BALANCE OF PAYMENTS (MAY)

Release Date: 8 Jul 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: ***

#### 1. Reuters Data

Current Account NSA JPY: 3 968,3B | Prior: 3 907,8B | Poll: 4 121,3B | Surprise: −153,00B
Current Account Balance SA: 3 064 500M | Prior: 4 211 100M
Trade Balance Customs Basis SA: 204 491,00M | Prior: 469 637,00M

#### 2. Professional Market Commentary

The current-account surplus remained large but missed expectations, while the seasonally adjusted balance fell sharply.

The professional tape focused on:

* the customs trade surplus more than halving
* elevated imported energy costs
* extreme yen weakness amplifying the import bill
* investment income continuing to support the overall surplus
* the external account remaining healthier than the trade account alone

The Ministry of Finance confirmed the May preliminary balance-of-payments release. ([mof.go.jp][1])

#### 3. Comment — Economist Layer

The primary signal is a deterioration in the trade and terms-of-trade leg, not a breakdown in Japan’s overall external position.

Japan continues to generate a large current-account surplus because its foreign-asset income remains substantial. However, the decline in the seasonally adjusted balance and the smaller customs trade surplus show the pressure created by the combination of expensive energy and a weak yen.

Signal hierarchy:

* current-account level: still strong
* monthly momentum: weaker
* trade balance: deteriorating
* foreign-investment income: main buffer
* yen-import-price transmission: adverse

The external surplus therefore remains structurally supportive for Japan, but its current composition is increasingly dependent on income earned abroad rather than goods trade.

State transition: mild deterioration — trade leg weakening, income leg continuing.

Context from prior regional live file:
Japan’s external position had remained supported by investment income even when energy imports weakened the goods balance. May intensifies that divergence as the trade surplus narrows materially. The current account remains robust, but the domestic terms-of-trade burden is increasing.

#### 4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The report does not create a direct BoJ policy signal, but it reinforces the weak-yen inflation channel.

A weaker trade balance reduces the fundamental support ordinarily associated with Japan’s external surplus, while the strong income account prevents a conventional balance-of-payments stress interpretation.

A recovery in the seasonally adjusted trade surplus above ¥400bn as oil prices normalise would reduce the concern. Persistent deterioration would increase the policy pressure surrounding JPY weakness and imported inflation.

## THURSDAY 9 JUL 2026

### JAPAN — M2 / BROAD MONEY (JUNE)

Release Date: 9 Jul 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: **

#### 1. Reuters Data

M2: 1 291 809 700M | Prior: 1 292 450 700M | Revised: 1 292 433 100M
Broad Money: 2 327,9T | Prior: 2 328,6T | Revised: 2 328,2T

#### 2. Professional Market Commentary

The outstanding levels were marginally lower on the month, but the official growth rates remained positive.

The BoJ’s completed release showed:

* M2 growth of 2.2% y/y
* M3 growth of 1.5% y/y
* broadly defined liquidity growth of 4.5% y/y
* M2 falling at a 0.6% seasonally adjusted annualised monthly rate
* M3 falling at a 1.0% annualised rate

The result is therefore not an outright broad-money contraction. ([Banque du Japon][8])

#### 3. Comment — Economist Layer

Japan is displaying a conventional policy-normalisation distinction between the monetary base and broad private-sector liquidity.

The monetary base is contracting rapidly as the BoJ reduces reserves and accommodation. M2 and broad liquidity are still expanding year on year because bank deposits, credit creation and other financial assets continue to grow.

The correct configuration is:

* monetary base: sharply contracting
* monthly broad-money momentum: softer
* M2 annual growth: positive
* broadly defined liquidity: still firm
* evidence of credit-system stress: absent

State transition: continuation — quantitative tightening without broad monetary contraction.

Context from prior regional live file:
The shrinking monetary base had raised the question of whether BoJ tightening was beginning to restrict private liquidity. June money-stock data show no such break yet. Broad money continues to expand even as reserve-based liquidity contracts.

#### 4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The release allows the BoJ to continue normalisation without evidence of acute monetary transmission stress.

A sustained outright decline in M2 over several months, accompanied by weaker lending and deposits, would be a more meaningful dovish signal. Current data do not meet that threshold.

## FRIDAY 10 JUL 2026

### JAPAN — CORPORATE GOODS PRICE INDEX (JUNE)

Release Date: 10 Jul 2026 | Local Time: 01:50
Importance: ****

#### 1. Reuters Data

Corporate Goods Price Index MM: 0,4% | Prior: 0,9% | Revised: 1,1% | Poll: 0,3% | Surprise: +0,10%
Corporate Goods Price Index YY: 7,1% | Prior: 6,3% | Revised: 6,6% | Poll: 6,8% | Surprise: +0,30%

#### 2. Professional Market Commentary

Wholesale inflation accelerated to its highest annual rate since March 2023 and exceeded expectations.

Official detail showed:

* producer-price index at 135.4
* yen-basis import prices rising 29.7% y/y
* yen-basis export prices rising 20.7%
* contract-currency import prices increasing only 0.1% m/m
* contract-currency export prices falling 0.4% m/m
* a major contribution from fuel and non-ferrous metals
* the weak yen acting as the dominant import-price amplifier

The BoJ confirmed the 0.4% monthly producer-price increase and the 29.7% rise in yen-basis import prices. ([Banque du Japon][9])

Professional commentary focused on the risk that wholesale pressure would pass into consumer prices faster than in previous cycles.

#### 3. Comment — Economist Layer

This is Japan’s clearest inflation-pipeline escalation.

The headline is reinforced by three features:

* May was revised materially higher
* import inflation accelerated further
* the price shock extends beyond oil into metals and industrial inputs

The yen basis versus contract-currency distinction is critical. Much of the inflation acceleration is not generated by foreign suppliers raising underlying contract prices; it is generated by the currency conversion into an exceptionally weak yen.

The domestic inflation regime now contains several mutually reinforcing elements:

* wholesale prices at 7.1%
* yen import prices near 30%
* wage settlements above 5%
* elevated Tankan inflation expectations
* strong domestic services demand
* government subsidies temporarily restraining measured consumer inflation

The wedge between wholesale and consumer inflation is therefore partly artificial. It may represent delayed pass-through rather than genuine upstream disinflation.

State transition: escalation.

Context from prior regional live file:
Tankan expectations, services input costs and household demand had already strengthened the domestic-inflation case. June CGPI adds a powerful upstream and currency channel. Japan is moving from an oil-shock story toward a broader wage, expectations and import-price inflation regime.

#### 4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The release materially strengthens the case for another BoJ hike, with October remaining a plausible live meeting if consumer-price and wage transmission continue.

The release is not unambiguously JPY-positive because extreme yen weakness is itself creating the inflation problem and may raise political pressure around policy coordination.

The hawkish signal would weaken if:

* CGPI falls below 6% for two consecutive months
* yen-basis import-price inflation drops below 20%
* services inflation fails to accelerate
* firms absorb costs through margins rather than consumer-price increases

WEDNESDAY 15 JUL 2026

JAPAN — Reuters Tankan Non-Manufacturers Index (Jul)

Release Date: 15 Jul 2026 | Local Time: 01:00
Importance: ***

1. Reuters Data

Reuters Tankan N-Man Idx: 25 | Prior: 32

2. Professional Market Commentary

The Reuters Tankan showed a widening split between resilient technology-facing manufacturing and services firms affected by imported costs.

Manufacturing sentiment held at +13, supported by semiconductor, memory-chip, electronic-component and AI-server demand. Non-manufacturing sentiment fell seven points to +25 as firms cited the Middle East conflict, yen weakness, higher costs and rising interest rates.

Forward expectations were less negative: manufacturers projected +14 in October, while non-manufacturers expected sentiment to remain at +25.

3. Comment — Economist Layer

The level of services confidence remains positive, but the seven-point decline is too large to classify as no change.

Signal hierarchy:

* level: +25 still represents net optimism
* momentum: material deterioration
* manufacturing offset: external AI and semiconductor demand remains strong
* services driver: cost and margin pressure rather than generalized demand collapse
* inflation interpretation: cost pass-through can be hawkish even when sentiment weakens
* policy limitation: private monthly survey, subordinate to official Tankan and hard activity

The key distinction is demand destruction versus supply-cost compression. Current evidence fits cost compression more closely, with services firms remaining optimistic in net terms but increasingly squeezed by currency, energy and financing costs.

State transition: deterioration — services confidence weakened materially while manufacturing resilience continued.

Context from prior regional live file:
The official Q2 Tankan had shown eight-year-high large-manufacturer sentiment and record corporate inflation expectations. July’s Reuters survey does not reverse that corporate resilience, but it reveals sharper stress among services firms. Japan’s corporate regime remains resilient but increasingly bifurcated by cost exposure.

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

This is not an unambiguously dovish BoJ release.

The services decline supports patience if interpreted as weaker domestic momentum, but the cause—yen weakness and imported-cost pressure—also reinforces concern that firms may continue raising prices.

Market mapping:

* JPY: renewed depreciation worsens the cost channel
* JGB front end: modest patience signal, not a normalization reversal
* BoJ: next test is whether wages, activity and inflation expectations confirm pass-through without a broader demand break

This is the clearest Japanese downside release of the week.

* leading quality: relevant for future business investment

The release reverses April’s improvement and reopens the question of whether investment decisions are being deferred because of cost, financing and geopolitical uncertainty.

One month cannot certify a capex downturn, but the decline is too large to dismiss as routine noise. June is the decisive arbiter.

State transition: deterioration — April’s capex rebound was reversed by a substantially larger-than-expected decline.

Context from prior regional live file:

4. HF Take — Strategist Layer

The release gives the BoJ a genuine patience argument, but not enough evidence to abandon normalization.

Conditional interpretation:

* stabilization in June would classify May as payback
* another weak month would imply uncertainty is reaching investment decisions
* continued AI and machine-tool strength with weak core orders would confirm a two-speed capex cycle

Market mapping:

* JGB front end: downside impulse to near-term tightening expectations
* JPY: mixed, because weaker investment is negative while delayed tightening can worsen imported inflation

Japan — S&P Global Flash Composite / Manufacturing / Services PMI (Jul)

Importance: ****
