February reverses that mix: export momentum cooled sharply, imports rebounded strongly, and the balance improved mostly because the prior deficit was extremely large. HF Take — Strategist Layer

For BoJ mapping, this is mixed rather than decisively positive. The headline surplus should not be read as a reason to accelerate hawkish BoJ repricing.

Japan — JP BOJ Rate Decision (Mar)
Release Date: 19 Mar 2026 | Local Time: JST morning
Importance: ****

Reuters Data

JP BOJ Rate Decision: 0.75% | Prior: 0.75% | Poll: 0.75% | Surprise: 0.000%

Professional Market Commentary

The official BoJ statement delivered a clear hawkish hold rather than a dovish pause. The Policy Board kept the overnight call-rate target at around 0.75% by an 8–1 vote, stated that Japan's economy "has recovered moderately, although some weakness has been seen in part," and left the medium-term underlying-inflation view intact. Crucially, the statement also recorded Hajime Takata's proposal to raise the rate to around 1.0%, which was defeated by majority vote.

Comment — Economist Layer

The hold was tactical, not strategic. The key information was the vote structure and the retained normalization bias. An 8–1 hold with an explicit 1.0% dissenter means the center of gravity on the Board is still moving in a hawkish direction even if the Bank chose to wait through a new oil-risk shock. The official text also still projects underlying CPI inflation to rise gradually and be generally consistent with target later in the January projection window. That is not a pivot away from hikes.
State transition: no material change in action / escalating normalization pressure in forward guidance.

Context from prior regional live file:
The prior DM live file had already built a medium-term BoJ normalization story around firm services prices, solid capex, and uneven but positive activity. March's decision preserves that structure. What changed is the tactical overlay: the Bank paused at 0.75%, but the emergence of an explicit 1.0% proposal inside the Board makes the next-step debate more live than before.

HF Take — Strategist Layer

This is a hawkish hold for JPY medium-term mapping, even if the immediate market read can still be JPY-soft because no hike was delivered. The binding issue is timing, not direction. April is now a credible live meeting if wage, FX, and inflation signals remain firm.

<<JPY_WEEK_BEGIN>>
DATA WINDOW: 15 Feb 2026 to 13 Mar 2026

A. JPY WEEK — MACRO SYNTHESIS

Japan’s merged window delivered the analytical resolution to the main early-2026 Japan question: the initial Q4 GDP disappointment was not the final growth signal. The preliminary GDP release looked too soft for a clean BoJ-normalisation backdrop, but the data that followed — machinery orders, MOF capex, PMIs and then the 10 Mar GDP revision — confirmed that the capex-led growth pulse was more credible than the first estimate implied. Revised Q4 GDP moved to 0.3% q/q and 1.3% annualised, with capex upgraded to 1.3% q/q and domestic demand contributing positively, shifting Japan into the firmer-growth bucket within DM.

The constraint was that the upgrade remained investment-led, not household-led, while inflation did not force urgency. Retail and chain-store data improved inside the first block, but January household spending stayed negative y/y, labour softened marginally, housing remained weak, and consumption still failed to validate a clean wage-led demand cycle. Inflation moderated through nationwide CPI, Tokyo CPI and CGPI, with subsidies suppressing pipeline pressure even as yen-based import prices kept the forward cost risk alive. The correct merged-window read is therefore: Japan’s growth picture materially upgraded and capex credibility restored, but household demand and inflation still argue for BoJ gradualism rather than near-term urgency.

B. JPY MACRO SIGNAL SCOREBOARD

Growth: +
Supporting evidence:
The first Q4 GDP estimate was weak, but subsequent releases changed the interpretation. Machinery orders surged 19.1% m/m and 16.8% y/y, MOF business capex rose 6.5% y/y, final PMIs held firmly in expansion across manufacturing and services, and the revised Q4 GDP print upgraded growth to 0.3% q/q and 1.3% annualised. Capex was revised from 0.2% to 1.3% q/q and domestic demand contribution moved to 0.3pp. Household spending remained weak, but the investment-led growth upgrade was real.

Labor: ~
Supporting evidence:
Jobs-to-applicants softened marginally to 1.18 from 1.20 and unemployment edged up to 2.7% from 2.6%, but both remained tight in level terms. No later labour release appeared in the 9–13 Mar block. Service PPI at 2.60% preserved evidence that wage/service-price transmission had not broken, but the labour bucket did not deliver a decisive new acceleration signal.

Inflation: ~
Supporting evidence:
Nationwide core CPI cooled to 2.0%, Tokyo ex-fresh food fell to 1.8%, and CGPI softened to -0.1% m/m and 2.0% y/y. This reduced near-term inflation urgency. But the CGPI commentary flagged government fuel subsidies as the suppression mechanism and yen-based import-price acceleration as the underlying risk. Inflation was therefore moderating, not collapsing, and not cleanly forcing either a dovish reversal or an urgent hike.

Financial Conditions: ~
Supporting evidence:
Monetary base contraction deepened to -10.58% y/y, consistent with gradual balance-sheet normalisation, while the current account returned to surplus at ¥941.6bn, reducing external-stress risk. No liquidity stress was visible. The main financial-conditions risk remained yen/import-cost sensitivity: if JPY weakened further, the cost-push channel could quickly rebuild pressure even as headline pipeline inflation softened.

Policy Constraint: ~
Supporting evidence:
The BoJ retained normalisation optionality but without urgency. Capex improvement and the GDP revision strengthened the growth leg of the normalisation case, but household spending remained negative y/y and inflation measures softened toward target or below. The practical policy implication was gradualism: June-to-October normalisation optionality stayed alive, but the data did not justify a forceful near-term hawkish acceleration.

Japan — Corp Goods Prices / CGPI (Feb)

Corp Goods Price MM: -0.1% | Prior: 0.2% | Poll: 0.1% | Surprise: -0.20%

Professional Market Commentary

This modestly lowers the urgency of a near-term hawkish inflation interpretation. The BoJ mix now looks more balanced: firmer domestic growth, but no fresh pipeline-inflation acceleration yet. That keeps June normalization optionality alive without making it automatic.

SIGNAL TENSION CHECK

Main contradiction: Capex and investment signals — machinery orders +19.1% m/m, MOF capex +6.5% y/y, firm PMIs, and the Q4 GDP capex revision to +1.3% q/q — materially upgraded Japan’s growth picture, while household spending remained negative y/y and inflation softened through Tokyo CPI and CGPI. The growth leg of the BoJ normalisation case improved; the household and inflation urgency legs did not.

Why it matters: The BoJ’s normalisation case rests on two legs: sustained growth above potential and a self-reinforcing wage-price cycle with household validation. This merged window delivered the first leg more credibly than the preliminary GDP print suggested, but did not deliver the second leg with enough force to justify accelerated tightening.

Implication: Japan is the relative DM growth positive of the window, but the policy conclusion is gradualism, not urgency. The BoJ retains June-to-October 2026 normalisation optionality, with spring wage negotiations and Q1 consumption/inflation data as the next regime-defining catalysts.

3 KEY RELEASES TO DIG INTO

Japan GDP Revised / Capex Q4 2025 (released 10 Mar 2026)
Why it matters: This is the analytical resolution of the central Japan question across the window. The capex upgrade from 0.2% to 1.3% q/q, combined with the domestic demand contribution revised to 0.3pp, confirms that Japan's late-2025 growth pulse was genuine domestic-demand stabilisation rather than noise. It is the single release that most materially changes the Japan assessment versus the preliminary read.

Japan Machinery Orders December (released 19 Feb 2026)
Why it matters: The 19.1% m/m surge against a 4.5% consensus is the key forward-looking capex signal that foreshadowed the Q4 GDP revision. Understanding whether this represents durable investment recovery or a base-effect rebound from November's collapse is central for the BoJ normalisation timeline.

Japan Household Spending January (released 10 Mar 2026)
Why it matters: Arriving the same day as the GDP revision, this print is the essential counterweight. Real spending negative y/y at -1.0% despite firmer worker income means the consumption leg of the BoJ normalisation case is still undelivered. The tension between credible capex improvement and still-negative household spending is the configuration that keeps the BoJ in gradualism mode rather than urgency mode.

<<JPY_WEEK_BEGIN>>
DATA WINDOW: 5 Jan 2026 to 30 Jan 2026

A. JPY WEEK — MACRO SYNTHESIS

Japan’s January window confirmed the opening-2026 regime: services resilience and external-income stability were enough to prevent a growth-scare read, but hard capex, trade and inflation data gave the BoJ no reason to accelerate normalisation. The first half of the month showed a two-speed economy: services PMI and Reuters Tankan non-manufacturing stayed firm, broad money and the current account showed no stress, but the services hard-data picture remained volatile and no new inflation or labour signal forced a policy reassessment.

The Jan 19–30 block delivered the decision-grade resolution. The BoJ held at 0.75% with a data-dependent message and no preset hiking path; machinery orders collapsed, trade disappointed on the balance, and Tokyo CPI softened enough to remove near-term tightening urgency. Flash PMIs were the offset, with manufacturing and services both still expansionary. The correct merged-window read is therefore ultra-gradual normalisation intact, but delayed: services resilient, goods/capex fragile, inflation at or below the near-term comfort threshold, and the next hike pushed into a later-2026 conditional window unless wages, core-core inflation or JPY/import-price pressure re-accelerate.

B. JPY MACRO SIGNAL SCOREBOARD

Growth: ~
Supporting evidence:
Services PMI final printed 52.5, composite PMI 51.5, Reuters Tankan non-manufacturing held firm at 33, and January flash PMIs remained expansionary with manufacturing at 51.5, services at 53.4 and composite at 52.8. But machinery orders fell -11.0% m/m and -6.4% y/y, tertiary industry activity corrected sharply, and the December trade balance missed expectations. The net read is services resilience against fragile capex and external hard data, not clean acceleration.

Labor: ~
Supporting evidence:
No dedicated Japan labour release was present in the merged window. The operative prior framing of marginally soft jobs-to-applicants and stable unemployment remained unchanged. No new labour evidence arrived to alter the BoJ wage-price assessment.

Inflation: ~
Supporting evidence:
December nationwide CPI core remained above target at 2.4%, but headline fell to 2.1% from 2.9%. Tokyo CPI then softened materially, with ex-fresh food at 1.8% and overall Tokyo at 1.6% in the release table, while the commentary stressed slowing core-core and subsidy effects. The inflation signal is disinflationary enough to remove near-term BoJ urgency, but not weak enough to break the medium-term normalisation framework.

Financial Conditions: ~
Supporting evidence:
Broad money was stable at ¥2,238.9tn, the monetary-base contraction slowed to -8.66% y/y, and the current-account surplus remained large. The BoJ hold at 0.75% preserved existing conditions rather than adding a new tightening impulse. Rate differentials still left JPY sensitive to global rates and import-price risk, but there was no domestic liquidity-stress signal.

Policy Constraint: ~
Supporting evidence:
The BoJ hold and Ueda’s data-dependent framing confirmed an ultra-gradual path. Services resilience and core CPI above target preserved the medium-term normalisation case, but Tokyo CPI softness, falling core-core, machinery-orders weakness and trade-balance disappointment removed the case for near-term acceleration. The policy constraint is symmetric: no reversal, but no urgency.

HF Take — Strategist Layer

Neutral-to-dovish for BoJ; external channel not strengthening. JPY-negative via trade balance optics. Combined with machinery orders weakness, the hard-data picture for Japan's growth remains two-speed: services holding, goods and external under pressure.

Comment — Economist Layer

Inflation easing but still above target. Disinflation gives BoJ cover to stay patient despite services strength. Headline falling from 2.9% to 2.1% reflects the waning of rice and energy price pressures flagged in the BoJ's own outlook.

State transition: continuation of disinflation trajectory.

Status quo reinforced. JPY muted. Removes urgency for near-term tightening without triggering concerns about undershooting. The BoJ can point to core at 2.4% as still-above-target while acknowledging the downward trajectory.

Japan — BoJ Rate Decision
Release Date: 23 Jan 2026
Importance: ****

Reuters Data

Policy Rate: 0.75% | Unchanged

Professional Market Commentary

BoJ Governor Ueda: "BoJ would need to check impact of rate change each time before moving again." Signals data-dependent approach, no preset hiking path.

ING Economist: "We expect BoJ's next rate hike in October 2026 (not Q1/Q2). However, if JPY weakens and drives up import prices, timeline could be moved forward to Q2 2026."

Comment — Economist Layer

No shift; BoJ remains cautious and data-dependent. Tightening path remains extremely gradual. The hold was universally expected and the statement introduced no new hawkish or dovish signals beyond the established framework.

State transition: continuation — no regime change.

HF Take — Strategist Layer

Limited transmission. JPY remains more a function of global rates and risk than of domestic policy surprise. The October 2026 next-hike framing from ING is consistent with the consensus read: BoJ is not accelerating, it is waiting for sustained high-confidence confirmation from wages and services inflation.

--- WEEK OF 26 JAN TO 30 JAN 2026 ---

Japan — Tokyo CPI (Jan)
Release Date: 30 Jan 2026
Importance: ****

Reuters Data

CPI Tokyo Ex fresh food YY: 1.8% | Prior: 2.0% | Poll: 1.7% | Surprise: +0.10%
CPI Overall Tokyo: 1.6% | Prior: 1.5%

Professional Market Commentary

BoJ Outlook Report (Jan 23): "Year-on-year CPI (ex-fresh food) likely to decelerate to level below 2% in H1 2026, with waning effects of rise in food prices (rice) and partly due to government measures to address rising prices. However, mechanism in which wages and prices rise moderately in interaction will be maintained, underlying CPI inflation will continue rising moderately."

ING Economist: "Headline inflation eased to 2.0% in Tokyo December, below expectations (vs 2.7% Nov, 2.3% consensus), mainly due to renewed energy subsidies, stable rice prices, low petroleum costs. These factors expected to keep inflation low into 2026. Core inflation ex-fresh food and energy slowed to 2.6% (vs 2.8% Nov). With headline expected to drop below 2.0% and core easing toward 2.0%, BoJ likely to carefully assess impact of previous rate hikes before considering further hikes."

ING Forecast: "We expect BoJ's next rate hike in October 2026 (not Q1/Q2). However, if JPY weakens and drives up import prices, timeline could be moved forward to Q2 2026."

CNBC Analysis: "Tokyo CPI 2.0% is AT BoJ's 2% target, lowest since March 2022. Core-core 2.9% down from 3.0% is key — this strips food/energy volatility. Food inflation fell to 5.1% from 6.1% (13-month low), driven by slowest rice price rise in 16 months."

Comment — Economist Layer

Tokyo CPI 2.0% headline is at the BoJ target for the first time since March 2022, and 20bp below the 2.2% consensus. This is a material disinflation surprise that removes urgency for near-term BoJ tightening. Tokyo is the leading indicator for national CPI (released approximately one week earlier), so Tokyo 2.0% signals national CPI will also fall toward or below 2.0% in January. Core-core ex-fresh food and energy at 2.6% versus 2.8% prior is the key underlying measure — still above target but falling, which suggests the wage-price interaction the BoJ is waiting to see is weakening rather than strengthening. Subsidies helped hold the line on energy; yen-based import prices accelerated, so the underlying cost-push risk has not disappeared entirely.

State transition: deterioration on the inflation side — disinflation faster than expected, removing near-term hawkish urgency.

HF Take — Strategist Layer

Next hike pushed to Q4 2026 (October per ING forecast) from prior market expectations of Q1–Q2 2026. BoJ will need to see: (a) headline CPI above 2.0% for a sustained period, (b) core-core above 2.5% and accelerating, (c) spring wage negotiations delivering 5%+ raises, (d) JPY stability. JPY weakening pressure near-term — BoJ pause extends while Fed holds at 4.50%, rate differential persists. JPY likely 152–157 range versus USD in Q1 2026. If BoJ signals "no hike until October" explicitly, JPY could test 160+ with intervention risk from MoF at that level. Japan equity supported by BoJ pause and JPY weakness (export competitiveness) but vulnerable to global slowdown and China weakness via trade channel.

SIGNAL TENSION CHECK

Main contradiction: Services surveys and flash PMIs pointed to resilient activity, while machinery orders, tertiary activity, the trade balance and Tokyo CPI argued for a more cautious hard-data and inflation picture. The survey-versus-hard-data divergence was the central analytical tension of the window.

Why it matters: The BoJ reaction function is anchored to hard data, wages and sustained inflation confirmation, not survey optimism alone. If capex and external weakness persisted into Q1, the later-2026 next-hike timeline became the default; if PMI resilience translated into hard activity and wage confirmation, the timeline could compress.

Implication: The BoJ remained on hold through at least Q2 2026 unless wages, core-core inflation or yen/import-price pressure forced a reassessment. The spring wage negotiations were the next regime-defining catalyst.

3 KEY RELEASES TO DIG INTO

Japan Tokyo CPI January (released 30 Jan 2026)
Why it matters: This was the single most important Japan macro signal of the block. It removed near-term urgency for a follow-up hike and pushed institutional consensus toward a later-2026 next move. Core-core was the measure to watch: if it kept falling, the wage-price loop the BoJ was waiting for was weakening rather than strengthening.

Japan BoJ Rate Decision (released 23 Jan 2026)
Why it matters: The hold at 0.75% was expected, but Ueda’s framing — need to check the impact of each rate change, no preset path — was the reaction-function anchor for the entire monitoring window. Understanding what the BoJ was waiting for, sustained wage-price interaction rather than headline inflation at target alone, was essential for mapping the normalisation timeline.

Japan Machinery Orders November (released 19 Jan 2026)
Why it matters: The -11.0% m/m collapse against a -5.1% consensus was the starkest hard-data negative of the block. Capex intentions this weak were inconsistent with a growth backdrop that would justify accelerating the hiking cycle. It was the clearest single signal that Japan's recovery remained uneven and that the BoJ could not rely on investment momentum to support its normalisation case.
